\widehat{\pi}^{\text{(a)}}:=\frac{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]}{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{1}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]},

(8)

where the operators
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}},\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{1}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}
are computed in the same fashion as
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}},\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{1}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
but on
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}
instead of
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}
, and
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]
is the average of
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
evaluated on the test data in
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}
, i.e.,

\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]=\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}/2}\sum_{X_{i}^{\textup{\text{te}}}\in\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}}\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}(X_{i}^{\textup{\text{te}}}).

Finally, steps (2)-(4) are repeated, but with the roles of
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}
reversed. This results in another quantifier
\widehat{\pi}^{(b)}
. The final quantifier is then simply the average
\widehat{\pi}:=\frac{\widehat{\pi}^{(a)}+\widehat{\pi}^{(b)}}{2}
.

Having described the binary (
m=1
) class case, we now turn to the multiclass (
m\geq 1
) case. The extension is conceptually identical, but there are three main differences. First,
\gamma^{\ast}
is now a
m
-dimensional vector. Namely,
\gamma^{\ast}=(\gamma_{1}^{\ast},\dots,\gamma_{m}^{\ast})
where
\gamma_{y}^{\ast}
is given by:

\gamma_{y}^{\ast}:=\frac{\frac{\pi_{y}^{\ast}}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}{\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{k}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{k}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}.

(9)

To estimate
\gamma^{\ast}
in step 2, we take the same approach as before: we use
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}
to choose a preliminary quantifier for
\pi^{\ast}
, which we then use to replace
\pi^{\ast}
in display (
9
), resulting in a plug-in estimator
\widehat{\gamma}
of
\gamma^{\ast}
.

Second,
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
is now a
\mathbb{R}^{m}
-valued gradient. For each
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle s_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\delta}{\delta{\beta}}\Big\{\log p_{\beta}(x)\Big\}\Big\lvert_{\beta=\gamma^{\ast}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\big(p_{1}(x)-p_{0}(x),\dots,p_{m}(x)-p_{0}(x)\big)}{p_{\beta}(x)}

\displaystyle\equiv\big(s_{\gamma^{\ast},1}(x),\dots,s_{\gamma^{\ast},m}(x)\big).

Each of the
m
different component functions must be estimated. In our algorithm, we estimate each component function separately, and use an approach similar to what was used in the binary class case. Similar to before, one can show that, for each
y\in[m]
, among all functions
h_{y}:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
such that
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[h_{y}]=\mathbb{E}_{y}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[h_{y}]
, the function
h_{y}
that maximizes
\mathbb{E}_{y}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[h_{y}]
is
h_{y}=s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
. This suggests constructing an estimate
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}
of
s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
in the following manner: given a function class
\mathcal{H}_{y}
that we believe contains
s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
, choose
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}
to be the member of
\mathcal{H}_{y}
that maximizes
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\lambda_{y}\Omega(h_{y})^{2}
, subject to the constraint that
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\widehat{\gamma}}[h_{y}]=\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]
. Analogous to before,
\lambda_{y}>0
is a regularization parameter and
\Omega
is some norm on
\mathcal{H}_{y}
. When
\mathcal{H}_{y}
is taken to be a union of RKHS’s, the optimization problem can be solved using the same algorithm as before by
Hmam, line
. After
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}
has been estimated for each
y\in[m]
, the final score function estimate is
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}:=(\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},1},\dots,\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},m})
.

The third difference lies in how
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
is used to construct an ACC quantifier. Similar to the method-of-moments approach used in the
m=1
case, we make two changes to display (
5
): we replace
f
with
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
, and we substitute all population moments with their sample-based counterparts. As a result, the multi-class analogue to equation (
8
) is then the vector
\widehat{\pi}^{(a)}
which solves

\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]=\overbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\vrule&&\vrule\\
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{1}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]&\ldots&\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{m}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]\\
\vrule&&\vrule\end{bmatrix}}^{=:\text{ }\widehat{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}}\overbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{\pi}_{1}^{(a)}\\
\vdots\\
\widehat{\pi}_{m}^{(a)}\end{bmatrix}}^{=:\text{ }\widehat{\pi}^{(a)}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}.

(10)

The final algorithm for the multi-class case is presented below.

Semiparametric Efficient Label Shift Estimation (SELSE) Procedure
:

Step 1.

If there is a class
y
with only one sample in
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
, or if
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{te}}}
only has one sample, then output
\widehat{\pi}=\frac{1}{m+1}\vec{1}_{m}
. Else, randomly split
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
into two equally sized parts,
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
, such that
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
have equal class proportions. Randomly split
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{te}}}
into two equally sized parts,
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{te,II}}}
. Define
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}:=(\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}},\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}})
and
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}:=(\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}},\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{te,II}}})
.

Step 2.

Use
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}
to construct an estimate
\widehat{\gamma}
of
\gamma^{\ast}
.

Step 3.

Use
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}
and
\widehat{\gamma}
to construct an estimate
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
of the score function
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
. This is done by setting
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}:=(\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},1},\dots,\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},m})
, and for each
y\in[m]
, choosing
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}
such that

\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}\in\argmax_{h_{y}\in\widehat{C}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}}(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}])-\lambda_{y}\Omega(h_{y})^{2},

where
\widehat{C}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}:=\Big\{h_{y}\in\mathcal{H}_{y}\text{ }:\text{ }\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\widehat{\gamma}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]=\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]\Big\}
.

Step 4.

Use
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}
and
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
to compute
\widehat{\pi}^{(a)}
, which is the solution to:

\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]=\overbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\vrule&&\vrule\\
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{1}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]&\ldots&\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{m}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}]\\
\vrule&&\vrule\end{bmatrix}}^{=:\text{ }\widehat{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}}\overbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{\pi}_{1}^{(a)}\\
\vdots\\
\widehat{\pi}_{m}^{(a)}\end{bmatrix}}^{=:\text{ }\widehat{\pi}^{(a)}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}.

If the matrix
\widehat{A}
is not invertible, set
\widehat{\pi}^{(a)}=\frac{1}{m+1}\vec{1}_{m}
.

Step 5.

Repeat Steps
(2)-(4)
, but now with the roles of
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}
reversed, and let
\widehat{\pi}^{(b)}
denote the new result of step (4). Output
\widehat{\pi}:=\frac{\widehat{\pi}^{(a)}+\widehat{\pi}^{(b)}}{2}
.

2.4
Optimization Algorithm

In this subsection, we present our approach for solving the optimization problem in step 3 of our SELSE algorithm. To make the problem simpler, we assume for each
y\in[m]
that the solution can be written as a linear combination of functions. That is, we assume that the solution lies in the span of
\phi_{y,1},\dots,\phi_{y,d}
, for some
d\geq 1
and known functions
\phi_{y,i}:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
. This assumption is true when, for example,
\mathcal{H}_{y}
is a RKHS or has a known function basis.

Given this linearity assumption, our approach is to rewrite the optimization problem as a quadratic program, with a single quadratic equality constraint. This then enables us to use the algorithm presented by
Hmam, line
, which provably finds the global optimum. Towards that end, we start with rewriting our objective function. Let
h_{y}=\langle w_{y},\phi_{y}\rangle
, where
w_{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\phi_{y}:=(\phi_{y,1},\dots,\phi_{y,d})
. Also, assume that there exists a matrix
P_{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that
\lambda_{y}\Omega(h_{y})^{2}=w_{y}^{T}P_{y}w_{y}
. For example, this is true if
\Omega
is a RKHS norm or if
\Omega(h_{y})=||w_{y}||_{2}
; in the former case,
P_{y}
is the Gram matrix scaled by
\lambda_{y}
and in the latter case,
P_{y}=\lambda_{y}I_{d\times d}
. Then, one can show that the objective function in step 3 of SELSE can be rewritten as:

-2\big\langle w_{y},\tfrac{1}{2}\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[\phi_{y}]-\tfrac{1}{2}\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[\phi_{y}]\big\rangle-w_{y}^{T}P_{y}w_{y}.

Turning this into a minimization problem, the objective function for
w_{y}
then becomes:

w_{y}^{T}P_{y}w_{y}+2w_{y}^{T}p_{y},

where
p_{y}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[\phi_{y}]-\tfrac{1}{2}\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[\phi_{y}]
. As for the constraint in step 3 of SELSE, one can show that it is equivalent to

w_{y}^{T}Q_{y}w_{y}+2w_{y}^{T}p_{y}=0,

where
Q_{y}:=\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\widehat{\gamma}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[\phi_{y}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. Thus, the final optimization problem to be solved is:

\text{Find }w_{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ to minimize: }w_{y}^{T}P_{y}w_{y}+2w_{y}^{T}p_{y}

\text{Subject to: }w_{y}^{T}Q_{y}w_{y}+2w_{y}^{T}p_{y}=0.

Let
\widehat{w}_{y}
denote the solution to the above problem. Then, the
{y}^{\text{th}}
score function estimate is given by
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}=\widehat{w}_{y}^{T}\phi_{y}
. Note that the optimization routine has to be run for each
y\in[m]
; after that has been done, the final score function estimate in Step 3 of SELSE is
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}=(\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},1},\dots,\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},m})
.

2.5
Verifying the Asymptotic Optimality of
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
for ACC/PACC Quantifiers

Having presented a concise overview of our algorithm, we now focus on substantiating the claim that, among all functions
f:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
, choosing
f=s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
minimizes the asymptotic variance of the normalized error of ACC/PACC quantifiers.

2.5.1
Ideal Characteristics of
f

Towards that end, it will be helpful to first answer an important question: what even are the
characteristics
of functions
f
that cause this variance to be small? To identify those characteristics, we consider the following thought experiment. Suppose that we replaced
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
in the system of equations in display (
10
) with some other function
f
, and then defined
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
to be the quantifier that solves this new system of equations; i.e.,
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}
solves

\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]=\overbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\vrule&&\vrule\\
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{1}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]&\ldots&\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{m}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]\\
\vrule&&\vrule\end{bmatrix}}^{=:\text{ }\widehat{A}_{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}}\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}.

(11)

For the sake of simplicity in this thought experiment, we will assume that
f
is fixed– i.e., unlike
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
, the function
f
does not depend on
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}
. We also assume that both
\widehat{A}_{f}
and
A_{f}
(the population level counterpart to
\widehat{A}_{f}
) are positive definite. Then
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}=\widehat{A}_{f}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f])
, and one can show that the error satisfies

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}+n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}(\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}-\pi^{\ast})

\displaystyle=\overbrace{\sqrt{\frac{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}+n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}A_{f}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f])}^{O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),

where
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]:=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\pi_{y}^{\ast}\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]
. The normalization factor of
\sqrt{\frac{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}+n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}
plays the same role as the usual
“
\sqrt{n}
”
in parametric statistics; the reason for the difference is that, in the quantification setting, there are two types of samples (train and test), and we cannot create consistent estimators for
\pi^{\ast}
unless
both

n^{\textup{\text{tr}}},n^{\textup{\text{te}}}\to\infty
. The normalization above reflects this, since
\frac{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}+n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\leq\min(n^{\textup{\text{tr}}},n^{\textup{\text{te}}})
. The correctness of this scaling will also be seen in the Theoretical Results section.

Overall, the display above implies that the asymptotic variance of the normalized error of
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}
is equal to the variance of
\sqrt{\frac{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}+n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}A_{f}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f])
. To see how the choice of
f
impacts this variance, we make use of the following Lemma.

Lemma 2.1

(
Variance of First Order Term using
f
)
.

For any integer
q\geq 1
and function
f:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{q}
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\Big[\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]\Big]

\displaystyle=\Bigg[\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg]\Big(\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]-A_{f}\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}A_{f}^{T}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}A_{f}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}A_{f}^{T},

and, if
q=m
and
A_{f}
is positive definite, then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\Big[A_{f}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f])\Big]

\displaystyle=\Bigg[\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg]\Big(A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]A_{f}^{-T}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}.

To clarify notation, for each
\beta
in the
m+1
dimensional probability simplex,
\mathcal{I}(\beta;\textup{Cat})
denotes the Fisher Information Matrix for
\beta
when
Y\sim\textup{Cat}(\beta)
, where
\text{Cat}(\beta)
denotes the categorical distribution supported on
\mathcal{Y}
. For us,
\textup{Cat}(\beta)
is a generalization of the Bernoulli distribution to multiple outcomes; specifically,
\mathbb{P}_{\textup{Cat}(\beta)}[Y=0]=1-\sum_{k=1}^{m}\beta_{k}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\textup{Cat}(\beta)}[Y=y]=\beta_{y}
for each
y\in[m]
. An exact expression for
\mathcal{I}(\beta;\textup{Cat})^{-1}
can be found in Lemma (
2.5
).

By Lemma (
2.1
), the variance matrix of
\sqrt{\frac{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}+n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}A_{f}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f])
is equal to

2\Bigg[\frac{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}+n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}+\frac{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}+n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg]\Big(A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]A_{f}^{-T}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Big)+2\frac{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}+n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1},

where the factor of
2
is because
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}
only contains half of the total train and test data, and is eliminated in Step 5 of SELSE when we take the average of
\widehat{\pi}^{(a)}
and
\widehat{\pi}^{(b)}
. Crucially, the expression above indicates that the impact of
f
on the asymptotic variance of
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}
’s normalized error is entirely captured by
A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]A_{f}^{-T}
. This fact provides us valuable insights about the exact characteristics of
f
that make the aforementioned asymptotic variance
“small”
. Interestingly, these traits are conceptually identical for both the binary and multi-class cases, so to facilitate the discussion, we focus on the simpler
m=1
setting (with all formal proofs allowing for
m\geq 1
). In this case,
f
is scalar-valued and

A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]A_{f}^{-T}=\frac{\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]}{(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}},

indicating that the asymptotic variance is low whenever
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]
is small and
(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}
is large. This makes sense in light of the first order term of
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}-\pi^{\ast}
, which when
m=1
, is equal to

\frac{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]}{\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]}.

(12)

Roughly, if
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]
is small then with high probability so is the numerator, and if
(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}
is large then so is the denominator. Thus, it is desirable to choose
f
so that
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]
is small and
(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}
is large, as this would make the expression displayed above very small. Choosing
f
so that both of these criteria are met is also nontrivial, since the criteria are at odds with one another: for example, if
f
is any constant function, then
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]
is zero, but so is
(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}
.

At first glance, these two characteristics may appear a bit arbitrary– indeed, aside from the fact that
\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]
appears in the denominator of display (
12
), what exactly is the intuition for why ACC/PACC quantifiers benefit from large
(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}
? Furthermore, why is
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]
even the right metric for the stochastic magnitude of the numerator in display (
12
)? We now attempt to address both of these questions.

The answer to the first question is simple, and stems from the fact that ACC/PACC quantifiers are, at their core, using the method of moments to solve a system of linear equations. Specifically, when
m=1
, instead of choosing
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}
to solve
\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}[f]=(1-\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)})\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]+\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]
, ACC quantifiers choose
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}
to solve
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]=(1-\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)})\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]+\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{1}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]
. This substitution of population-level moments for empirical moments is what makes it important to choose
f
so that
\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]
and
\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]
are sufficiently different. If they are too similar, then even if
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]
and
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{1}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]
are close to
\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]
and
\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]
respectively, the solution
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}
will be hypersensitive to small errors in those approximations. The larger
(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}
is, the less susceptible
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}
will be to errors in moment estimation.

The answer to the second question is a bit longer, but nonetheless still straightforward:
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]
is an excellent proxy for
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\big[\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]\big]
, which captures the stochastic size of
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]
because
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\big[\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]\big]=0
. To help see why
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]
is a good proxy for
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\big[\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]\big]
, it may help to first consider a simpler metric, such as

\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\textup{\text{te}}}}[f]+\textup{\text{Var}}_{0}[f]+\textup{\text{Var}}_{1}[f].

(13)

This metric seems reasonable if one considers the following expansion of
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]

\displaystyle=(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}[f])+(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f])

\displaystyle=(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}[f])+(1-\pi^{\ast})(\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f])+\pi^{\ast}(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{1}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]).

(14)

Indeed, since each of the three summands in line (
14
) has mean zero, this expansion of
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]
suggests that in order for
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\big[\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]\big]
to be small, it would be reasonable to simply choose
f
so that
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[f],\textup{\text{Var}}_{0}[f]
and
\textup{\text{Var}}_{1}[f]
are all small. However, simply minimizing (
13
) would be a gross oversimplification: depending on the relationship between
n^{\textup{\text{te}}}
,
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
,
\pi^{\ast}
and
\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
, the relative contributions of
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[f],\textup{\text{Var}}_{0}[f]
and
\textup{\text{Var}}_{1}[f]
to
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\big[\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]\big]
may be far from equal. In fact, in certain circumstances,
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\big[\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]\big]
may be nearly equal to only one of those three variances. This is precisely where
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]
excels as a proxy for
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\big[\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]\big]
:
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]
accurately accounts for each variance’s contribution, regardless of the relationship between
n^{\textup{\text{te}}}
,
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
,
\pi^{\ast}
and
\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
. This is highlighted by the behavior of
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]
when the aforementioned parameters take on extreme values relative to one other, such as in the following settings:

•

If
n^{\textup{\text{te}}}<<n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
, then
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]\approx\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[f]
because
\gamma^{\ast}\approx\pi^{\ast}
, reflecting the fact that the relative scarcity of test data makes
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[f]
the most important of the three variances to minimize, if we are to ensure that
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\big[\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]\big]
is small.

•

If
n^{\textup{\text{te}}}>>n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
,
\pi^{\ast}\approx\tfrac{1}{2}
and
\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\approx y
for some
y\in\{0,1\}
, then
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]\approx\textup{\text{Var}}_{1-y}[f]
because
\gamma^{\ast}\approx 1-y
, reflecting the fact that the relative scarcity of training data from class
1-y
makes
\textup{\text{Var}}_{1-y}[f]
the most important of the three variances to minimize.

•

If
n^{\textup{\text{te}}}>>n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
,
\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\approx\tfrac{1}{2}
and
\pi^{\ast}\approx y
for some
y\in\{0,1\}
, then
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]\approx\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]
because
\gamma^{\ast}\approx y
, reflecting the fact that in line (
14
),
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}[f]\approx 0
and
\pi_{1-y}^{\ast}(\mathbb{E}_{1-y}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{1-y}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f])\approx 0
, where the former is true because of the abundance of test data and the latter is true because
\pi_{1-y}^{\ast}\approx 0
. This makes
\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]
the most important of the three variances to minimize.

2.5.2
Using
f=s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
Maximizes the Ideal Characteristics

In subsection (
2.5.1
), we established that minimizing the asymptotic variance of
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}
’s normalized error amounts to choosing
f
to minimize
A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]A_{f}^{-T}
, which when
m=1
, amounts to minimizing
\frac{\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]}{(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}}
. We also provided intuition for why small values of this metric are desirable. In this section, we now demonstrate that
\frac{\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]}{(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}}
is minimized by
f=s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
, for the case of
m=1
. We then extend this result to the general
m\geq 1
setting, and prove that using a
f
which is linear in
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
is both necessary and sufficient for minimizing the asymptotic variance of
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}
’s normalized error. Finally, we provide two sources of intuition justifying this result.

To prove that
f=s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
minimizes
\frac{\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]}{(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}}
is straightforward, and relies on the relationship between
A_{f}
and
\textup{\text{Cov}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f,s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]
, as depicted in the following Lemma.

Lemma 2.2

(
Moment Identity
)
.

For any integer
q\geq 1
and function
f:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{q}
, we have that

A_{f}=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}\big[fs_{\beta}^{T}\big]=\textup{\text{Cov}}_{\beta}\big[f,s_{\beta}\big],

where
\beta
is any vector in the
m+1
dimensional probability simplex, and
\textup{\text{Cov}}_{\beta}\big[f,s_{\beta}\big]
is the cross-covariance matrix between
f
and
s_{\beta}
.

In the
m=1
case, Lemma (
2.2
) tells us that
\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]=\textup{\text{Cov}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f,s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]
. Using this identity, one can show that:

\displaystyle\frac{\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]}{(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})}\frac{1}{\textup{\text{Cor}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f,s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]^{2}},

where
\textup{\text{Cor}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f,s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]
denotes the correlation between
f(X)
and
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}(X)
when
X\sim p_{\gamma^{\ast}}
. Since
\textup{\text{Cor}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f,s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]^{2}
achieves its maximum value of
1
whenever
f=bs_{\gamma^{\ast}}+c
with
b,c\in\mathbb{R}
and
b\neq 0
, it follows that
\frac{\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]}{(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}}
can be minimized by choosing
f
to be linear in
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
. Thus, for the case of
m=1
, this establishes that the asymptotic variance of
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}
’s normalized error is minimized by linear functions of
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
.

However, several questions remain. For instance, what version of this result holds in the multi-class case? In addition, is there any other function
f
, not necessarily linear in
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
, which also minimizes the asymptotic variance? The latter is an especially important question to answer, because if such a
f
did exist, then whether we should use that
f
in place of
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
comes down to which function is easier to learn. To answer both of these questions, we turn to the following Lemma.

Lemma 2.3

(
Optimal Function
)
.

Let
\beta
be a vector in the
m+1
dimensional probability simplex with all entries bounded away from
0
and
1
, and let
f:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a function for which
A_{f}
is positive definite. If
\mathcal{I}(\beta)
is positive definite, then so is
A_{s_{\beta}}
, and

\mathcal{I}(\beta)^{-1}=A_{s_{\beta}}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[s_{\beta}]A_{s_{\beta}}^{-T}\preceq A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]A_{f}^{-T}.

Further, if
A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]A_{f}^{-T}
and
\mathcal{I}(\beta)^{-1}
have the same eigenvalues, then
f=Bs_{\beta}+c
a.e.
[p_{\beta}]
for some constant vector
c\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and constant invertible matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
.

To clarify notation in the Lemma above,
\mathcal{I}(\beta)=\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[s_{\beta}]=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[s_{\beta}s_{\beta}^{T}]
is the
Fisher Information Matrix
for the parameter
\beta
, when
X\sim p_{\beta}
and
\beta
is a member of the
m+1
dimensional probability simplex. Furthermore, the relation
\preceq
denotes the Loewner order, which we use to generalize our method of comparing variances when
m=1
to when
m\geq 1
. Namely, for any two square matrices
F_{1},F_{2}
of the same dimension,
F_{1}\preceq F_{2}
means that the
{i}^{\text{th}}
eigenvalue of
F_{1}
is no bigger than the
{i}^{\text{th}}
eigenvalue of
F_{2}
, for all
i
.

When we set
\beta=\gamma^{\ast}
, Lemma (
2.3
) directly addresses our two questions from earlier. First, it generalizes the binary class result that
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
minimizes the asymptotic variance to the multi-class case: namely, since
A_{s_{\gamma^{\ast}}}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}](A_{s_{\gamma^{\ast}}}^{T})^{-1}\preceq A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f](A_{f}^{T})^{-1}
, it follows that each eigenvalue of the asymptotic variance matrix of
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}
’s normalized error is the smallest when
f=s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
. Of course, this comparison only applies to those
f
for which
A_{f}
is positive definite, but that is not a restriction for ACC/PACC quantifiers– this is because
A_{f}

has
to be positive definite in order for the population-level equation (
6
) to be well-defined, as ACC/PACC quantifiers approximate this equation via method-of-moments. Indeed, as long as
A_{f}
is positive definite,
f
could be any hard, soft or calibrated classifier, ranging from a deep neural network to a simple
k
-NN model– asymptotically,
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
is still the better function to use
3
3

3

Moreover, we can rest assured that
\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})
is positive definite, in all cases for which
\pi^{\ast}
is statistically identifiable. The exact logic for this will be reviewed in the Theoretical Results section, but the main idea is that if
\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})
was
not
positive definite, then there would be at least two component densities
p_{y}
and
p_{y^{\prime}}
that are identical, meaning that the test data carries zero information about
\pi_{y}^{\ast}
and
\pi_{y^{\prime}}^{\ast}
.
. It is also worthwhile to note that functions which are linear in
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
also share this optimality property, since if
f=Bs_{\gamma^{\ast}}+c
for some invertible matrix
B
and vector
c
, then
A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]A_{f}^{-T}=A_{s_{\beta}}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[s_{\beta}]A_{s_{\beta}}^{-T}
. In summary, this means that linearity in
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
is a
sufficient
condition for minimizing the asymptotic variance of
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}
’s normalized error.

Second, Lemma (
2.3
) tells us that the foregoing linearity in
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
is also a
necessary
condition. That is, to obtain the smallest possible asymptotic variance for
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}
’s normalized error, one
has
to use a linear function of
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
. This result can also be strengthened, in light of the following observation. Suppose that
f=Bs_{\gamma^{\ast}}+c
, for any positive definite
B
. Then, using equation (
11
), one can show that
\widehat{\pi}_{f}^{(a)}=\widehat{\pi}_{s_{\gamma^{\ast}}}^{(a)}
, meaning that a quantifier built off of a linear function of
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
is
point-wise equal
to the quantifier built off of just
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
– i.e., they are the same quantifier. Combined with the previous result, this invariance to linear transformations implies that
\widehat{\pi}_{s_{\gamma^{\ast}}}
is effectively the only ACC/PACC quantifier with minimal asymptotic variance.

Having established the unique asymptotic optimality of
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
for ACC/PACC quantifiers in the general
m\geq 1
setting, we now provide some of our intuition behind this result. We do so in two ways. In the first way, we rationalize why
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
is the choice of
f
which minimizes
A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]A_{f}^{-T}
. This compliments our analysis in subsection (
2.5.1
), wherein we interpreted why small values of
A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]A_{f}^{-T}
are ideal for quantification. Since that analysis was done in the
m=1
case, here we restrict ourselves to that setting as well (thus,
A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]A_{f}^{-T}=\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]/(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}
), although the same ideas also hold for
m\geq 1
. The second way compares quantification to a classical statistical mixture problem, and uses the optimal procedure in the latter to justify the optimality of
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
in the former.

As mentioned, one way to understand the asymptotic optimality of
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
is to consider why it minimizes
\frac{\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]}{(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}}
among all
f
, or equivalently, why it maximizes
\frac{(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}}{\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]}
among all
f
. Note that we can also restrict ourselves to those
f
for which
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]=0
, since
\frac{(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}}{\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]}
is invariant to linear transformations of
f
. As such, the metric in question is:

\frac{(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}}{\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]}=\frac{(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}}{\mathbb{E}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f^{2}]}.

Now, intuitively, for this metric to be large,
f(X)
should take on very different values when
X\sim p_{1}
compared to when
X\sim p_{0}
. This would make the numerator
(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}
large. However, in that venture, the values that
f
does take on should not be too extreme, as this would make the denominator
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f^{2}]
very big, undoing the benefits of large
(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}
. Choosing
f=s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
balances these two competing concerns. To see how, let us consider the general behavior of
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}=\frac{p_{1}-p_{0}}{p_{\gamma^{\ast}}}
over a neighborhood
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{X}
of the covariate space, chosen to be sufficiently small so as to ensure that all functions in our discussion are approximately constant over
\mathcal{R}
. There are
2
cases to consider.

•

Case 1: For each
x\in\mathcal{R}
,
p_{1}(x)\approx p_{0}(x)
.

•

Case 2: For each
x\in\mathcal{R}
,
|p_{1}(x)-p_{0}(x)|>>0
.

In Case
1
, the fact that
p_{1}\approx p_{0}
over
\mathcal{R}
means that, for any given
f
, the region
\mathcal{R}
will contribute very little to making the difference
(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}
large, regardless of the magnitude of
f
over
\mathcal{R}
. However, the magnitude of
f
on
\mathcal{R}

will
contribute to making
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f^{2}]
larger. As such,
f
having large magnitude on
\mathcal{R}
can only decrease our metric. In light of this, it is best to choose
f
to be equal to
0
on
\mathcal{R}
, which
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
clearly accomplishes by virtue of its numerator being
p_{1}-p_{0}
. In Case
2
, one of the two class densities is significantly higher than the other in the region
\mathcal{R}
. Thus, choosing
f
to have large magnitude on
\mathcal{R}
seems like a good idea, since it would amplify the magnitude of exactly one of
\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]
and
\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]
far more than the other, causing
(\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])^{2}
to be large. The score function accomplishes this via its numerator
p_{1}-p_{0}
. However, at the same time, the magnitude of the chosen function cannot be too large, as that would also increase our metric’s denominator. In light of this tension,
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
standardizes the difference
p_{1}-p_{0}
via dividing it by
p_{\gamma^{\ast}}
, which as a convex combination of
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
, has a magnitude on the same scale as those densities. This standardization has the effect of making the magnitude of
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
positively
“correlated
4
4

4

in the colloquial, not statistical, sense
”
with the difference in
p_{1}-p_{0}
, whilst being agnostic to their actual scales. Essentially, this is what enables
\mathbb{E}_{0}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]
and
\mathbb{E}_{1}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]
to be very different, without also making
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}^{2}]
too large in the process.

We now turn to our second source of intuition for the optimality of
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
: the classical statistical mixture problem. As mentioned earlier, in a statistical mixture problem, the goal is to learn the mixing coefficient vector
\pi^{\ast}
under the assumption that each component density
p_{y}
is known. Standard parametric theory tells us that, at least asymptotically, the best approach to accomplish this goal is the MLE, since its normalized error distribution has the smallest possible asymptotic variance. This problem is clearly similar to quantification, in that both problems share the same goal of learning
\pi^{\ast}
. However, quantification differs from the classical mixture problem in that the component densities are not assumed to be known. In lieu of perfect information about these densities, we instead have the imperfect
“picture”
of them painted for us by the train data.

This information loss means that the task of quantification is usually more difficult than its classical mixture counterpart. However, an exception occurs when
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
grows far faster than
n^{\textup{\text{te}}}
. Under this asymptotic regime, the relative abundance of train data means that the component densities are essentially known. Thus, when
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}>>n^{\textup{\text{te}}}
, the two problems are equivalent, and so the optimal procedure for learning
\pi^{\ast}
in a quantification problem should be identical to the optimal procedure for learning
\pi^{\ast}
in a classical mixture problem. The idea that similar problems mean similar optimal procedures is our second source of intuition, and indeed, one can show that using
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
is what ensures this equivalence. This is easiest to see in the binary class case, when we set
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}=\infty
. In this case, the first order Taylor approximation of the classical mixture MLE is

\pi^{\ast}+\frac{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\pi^{\ast}}]}{\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast})}.

(15)

It can also be shown that the first order Taylor approximation of
\widehat{\pi}_{s_{\gamma^{\ast}}}^{(a)}
is given by

\displaystyle\pi^{\ast}+\frac{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]}{\mathbb{E}_{1}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]}.

However, since
\gamma^{\ast}=\pi^{\ast}
when
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}=\infty
, it follows that the display above is equal to

\displaystyle\pi^{\ast}+\frac{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\pi^{\ast}}]}{\mathbb{E}_{1}[s_{\pi^{\ast}}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[s_{\pi^{\ast}}]},

which is identical to display (
15
) because
\mathbb{E}_{1}[s_{\pi^{\ast}}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[s_{\pi^{\ast}}]=\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast})
by virtue of Lemma (
2.2
). Therefore, when
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}=\infty
, the quantifier
\widehat{\pi}_{s_{\gamma^{\ast}}}^{(a)}
mimics the mixture MLE typically employed when the component densities are known. Thus, overall, the optimality of
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
makes sense in this special setting, since it agrees with what is already known about efficient estimation in parametric statistics.

2.6
SELSE vs. EMQ & MLLS

Having provided intuition for using
f=s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
, we briefly compare
\widehat{\pi}_{s_{\gamma^{\ast}}}^{(a)}
against EMQ and MLLS. We motivate this by confronting an important question: what is the need for a new quantifier, in the first place? On the one hand, if we focus only on existing ACC / PACC quantifiers, then we have already discussed the answer to this in detail:
\widehat{\pi}_{s_{\gamma^{\ast}}}^{(a)}
has the smallest possible asymptotic variance matrix among all ACC / PACC quantifiers. However, as subsection (
1.3.2
) thoroughly points out, there are many other quantifiers besides CC variants in use. Since our optimality result only holds for the ACC / PACC family, one should therefore wonder: how do those other quantifiers compare against
\widehat{\pi}_{s_{\gamma^{\ast}}}^{(a)}
?

This question will be answered rigorously in the Theoretical Results section for a broad class of quantifiers, so for now, we restrict ourselves to a high-level comparison against specifically EMQ and MLLS, as we have already introduced these approaches in detail. As discussed in subsection (
2.2.2
), EMQ and MLLS are both based on the maximum likelihood principle: under ideal conditions, EMQ approximates the standard MLE for the classical mixture problem described at the end of subsection (
2.5.2
), and MLLS approximates the MLE for the version of the classical mixture problem that arises when the original test set has been transformed, i.e.,
X_{i}^{\textup{\text{te}}}
in the test set has been replaced by
f(X_{i}^{\textup{\text{te}}})
for some calibrated, soft classifier
f
.

The fact that both EMQ and MLLS are based on the maximum likelihood principle imply that EMQ and MLLS are optimal when the assumptions of the classical mixture setting are met, which includes the assumption that the component densities
p_{y}
are known. However, as discussed at the end of subsection (
2.5.2
), unless
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}>>n^{\textup{\text{te}}}
, this assumption is not valid for quantification tasks. This should cause one to be skeptical about the optimality of EMQ and MLLS for quantification, because intuitively, those methods are based on maximizing
approximations
of likelihood functions formed via the train data, yet their final quantifiers are not adjusted to account for the error in those very approximations. This can be problematic when
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\asymp n^{\textup{\text{te}}}
or
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}<<n^{\textup{\text{te}}}
, because in those situations, the relative uncertainty in the approximated likelihoods are higher. In contrast,
\widehat{\pi}_{s_{\gamma^{\ast}}}^{(a)}
accounts for the uncertainty in the component densities
p_{y}
, as seen from our earlier analysis in subsection (
2.5.1
). To recap, this is because
f=s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
minimizes
A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]A_{f}^{-T}
, and the dependence of
\gamma^{\ast}
on
n^{\textup{\text{te}}},n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
and
\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
enables
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]
to properly balance the varying levels of uncertainty among the different sources of data. For this reason, we would expect
\widehat{\pi}_{s_{\gamma^{\ast}}}
to perform better than EMQ and MLLS, especially when
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\asymp n^{\textup{\text{te}}}
or
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}<<n^{\textup{\text{te}}}
.

2.7
Auxiliary Lemmas & Proofs

Lemma 2.4

(
Cauchy-Schwarz Equality Condition
)
.

Suppose that
\phi
and
\zeta
are
\mathbb{R}^{m}
-valued random vectors, and that
\Upsilon
is the density function for their joint distribution. If
\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]
is invertible and

\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\phi^{\prime}]-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\phi^{\prime}]\big)=0,

then

\phi=\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\zeta\quad\textup{ a.e. }[\Upsilon].

Proof of Lemma (
2.4
)
.

The proof of this Lemma is partially modeled after the proof of Lemma 1.1 in
Tripathi, 1999
. First, observe that, for each
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
:

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}\Big[\big\{\phi-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\zeta\big\}\big\{\phi-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\zeta\big\}^{\prime}\Big]\alpha

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}\Big[\big\{\alpha^{\prime}\phi-\alpha^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\zeta\big\}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\geq 0.

At the same time, since
\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\phi^{\prime}]-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\phi^{\prime}]\big)=0
, we also have that:

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}\Big[\big\{\phi-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\zeta\big\}\big\{\phi-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\zeta\big\}^{\prime}\Big]\alpha

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}\Big[\big\{\phi-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\zeta\big\}\big\{\phi^{\prime}-\zeta^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\phi^{\prime}]\big\}\Big]\alpha

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\prime}\big\{\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\phi^{\prime}]-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\phi^{\prime}]\big\}\alpha

\displaystyle\leq||\alpha||_{2}^{2}\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\phi^{\prime}]-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\phi^{\prime}]\big)

\displaystyle=0,

for each
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Thus, whenever
\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\phi^{\prime}]-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\phi^{\prime}]\big)=0
, it follows that:

\displaystyle\implies\alpha^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}\Big[\big\{\phi-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\zeta\big\}\big\{\phi-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\zeta\big\}^{\prime}\Big]\alpha=0\hskip 18.49988pt\forall\ \alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

\displaystyle\implies\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}\Big[\big\{\phi-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\zeta\big\}\big\{\phi-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\zeta\big\}^{\prime}\Big]=0_{m\times m}

\displaystyle\implies\text{Tr}\Big\{\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}\Big[\big\{\phi-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\zeta\big\}\big\{\phi-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\zeta\big\}^{\prime}\Big]\Big\}=0

\displaystyle\implies\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}\Big[\big|\big|\phi-\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\zeta\big|\big|_{2}^{2}\Big]=0

\displaystyle\implies\phi=\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\phi\zeta^{\prime}]\mathbb{E}_{\Upsilon}[\zeta\zeta^{\prime}]^{-1}\zeta\hskip 9.24994pt\text{a.e.}\hskip 9.24994pt[\Upsilon],

as desired.
∎

Proof of Lemma (
2.2
)
.

Let any
i\in[q]
and any
y\in[m]
be given. The
{(i,y)}^{\text{th}}
entry of
A_{f}
can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\big[A_{f}\big]_{i,y}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{y}[f_{i}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f_{i}]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}(p_{y}(x)-p_{0}(x))f_{i}(x)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{\beta}(x)\frac{p_{y}(x)-p_{0}(x)}{p_{\beta}(x)}f_{i}(x)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}p_{\beta}(x)s_{\beta,y}(x)f_{i}(x)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}\big[f_{i}s_{\beta,y}\big].

This implies that
A_{f}=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}\big[fs_{\beta}^{T}\big]
. Also note that
\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[s_{\beta}]=0
, so
\mathbb{E}_{\beta}\big[fs_{\beta}^{T}\big]=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}\big[fs_{\beta}^{T}\big]-\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f]\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[s_{\beta}]^{T}=\textup{\text{Cov}}_{\beta}[f,s_{\beta}]
, and so

A_{f}=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}\big[fs_{\beta}^{T}\big]=\textup{\text{Cov}}_{\beta}[f,s_{\beta}],

as claimed.
∎

Lemma 2.5

(
Covariance Matrix of a Finite Mixture
)
.

Let
\beta
be a vector in the
m+1
dimensional probability simplex. Then, for any function
f:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
q\geq 1
, we have that:

\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+A_{f}\mathcal{I}(\beta;\textup{Cat})^{-1}A_{f}^{T},

where

\mathcal{I}(\beta;\textup{Cat})^{-1}:=\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{1}(1-\beta_{1})&-\beta_{1}\beta_{2}&\ldots&-\beta_{1}\beta_{m}\\
-\beta_{2}\beta_{1}&\beta_{2}(1-\beta_{2})&\ldots&-\beta_{2}\beta_{m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-\beta_{m}\beta_{1}&-\beta_{m}\beta_{2}&\ldots&\beta_{m}(1-\beta_{m})\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}.

Proof of Lemma (
2.5
)
.

Observe that:

\displaystyle\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[ff^{T}]-\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f]\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f]^{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\mathbb{E}_{y}[ff^{T}]-\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f]\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f]^{T}.

And that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f]\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f]^{T}

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\Bigg)\Bigg(\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\Bigg)^{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\sum_{z=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\beta_{z}\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\mathbb{E}_{z}[f]^{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=0}^{m}(\beta_{y})^{2}\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]^{T}+\sum_{y\neq z}\beta_{y}\beta_{z}\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\mathbb{E}_{z}[f]^{T}.

Thus:

\displaystyle\implies\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\mathbb{E}_{y}[ff^{T}]-\sum_{y=0}^{m}(\beta_{y})^{2}\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]^{T}-\sum_{y\neq z}\beta_{y}\beta_{z}\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\mathbb{E}_{z}[f]^{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{y}[ff^{T}]-\beta_{y}\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]^{T}\Big]-\sum_{y\neq z}\beta_{y}\beta_{z}\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\mathbb{E}_{z}[f]^{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\Big[\big(\mathbb{E}_{y}[ff^{T}]-\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]^{T}\big)+\big(\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]^{T}-\beta_{y}\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]^{T}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\sum_{y\neq z}\beta_{y}\beta_{z}\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\mathbb{E}_{z}[f]^{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\Big[\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+\big(1-\beta_{y}\big)\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]^{T}\Big]-\sum_{y\neq z}\beta_{y}\beta_{z}\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\mathbb{E}_{z}[f]^{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\big(1-\beta_{y}\big)\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]^{T}-\sum_{y\neq z}\beta_{y}\beta_{z}\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\mathbb{E}_{z}[f]^{T}.

Define:

c_{y,z}=\begin{cases}\beta_{y}\big(1-\beta_{y}\big)&y=z\\
-\beta_{y}\beta_{z}&y\neq z.\end{cases}

Then we have that:

\displaystyle\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+\sum_{y=0}^{m}\sum_{z=0}^{m}c_{y,z}\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]\mathbb{E}_{z}[f]^{T}.

Now, the above equality holds for any function
f:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
, so it also holds for the function given by
f(x)-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]
. Thus, we have that:

\displaystyle\implies\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}\Big[f-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}\Big[f-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]\Big]+\sum_{y=0}^{m}\sum_{z=0}^{m}c_{y,z}\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[f-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]\Big]\mathbb{E}_{z}\Big[f-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]\Big]^{T}.

\displaystyle\implies\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+\sum_{y=0}^{m}\sum_{z=0}^{m}c_{y,z}\big(\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]\big)\big(\mathbb{E}_{z}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]\big)^{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+\sum_{y=1}^{m}\sum_{z=1}^{m}c_{y,z}\big(\mathbb{E}_{y}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]\big)\big(\mathbb{E}_{z}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]\big)^{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+A_{f}\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})^{-1}A_{f}^{T}.

∎

Proof of Lemma (
2.1
)
.

By the assumed independence properties of our data, we have that:

\displaystyle\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\Big[\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]\Big]

\displaystyle=\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}}\Big[\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]\Big]+\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}}\Big[\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]\Big]

\displaystyle=\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}}\Big[\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]\Big]+\sum_{y=0}^{m}(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}}\Big[\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tfrac{1}{2}n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[f]+\frac{1}{\tfrac{1}{2}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tfrac{1}{2}n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\Bigg(\sum_{y=0}^{m}\pi_{y}^{\ast}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+A_{f}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}A_{f}^{T}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\tfrac{1}{2}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f],

where the last equality follows from Lemma (
2.5
). Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\Big[\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\Bigg(\sum_{y=0}^{m}\pi_{y}^{\ast}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+A_{f}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}A_{f}^{T}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\Bigg(\frac{\pi_{y}^{\ast}}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg)\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}A_{f}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}A_{f}^{T}

\displaystyle=\Bigg[\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg]\sum_{y=0}^{m}\gamma_{y}^{\ast}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}A_{f}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}A_{f}^{T}

\displaystyle=\Bigg[\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg]\Big(\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]-A_{f}\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\text{Cat})^{-1}A_{f}^{T}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}A_{f}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}A_{f}^{T},

where the last equality again follows from Lemma (
2.5
). Thus, if
q=m
and
A_{f}
is positive definite, it follows that:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\Big[A_{f}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f])\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\Big[\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[f]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[f]\Big]A_{f}^{-T}

\displaystyle=\Bigg[\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg]\Big(A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[f]A_{f}^{-T}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\text{Cat})^{-1}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1},

(16)

as desired.
∎

Proof of Lemma (
2.3
)
.

First, note that
A_{s_{\beta}}=\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[s_{\beta}]\equiv\mathcal{I}(\beta)
by Lemma (
2.2
), so
A_{s_{\beta}}
is positive definite as claimed because
\mathcal{I}(\beta)
is assumed to be positive definite. Thus, the inverses of both matrices are well-defined. This also means that

\displaystyle A_{s_{\beta}}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[s_{\beta}](A_{s_{\beta}}^{T})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}(\beta)^{-1}\mathcal{I}(\beta)\mathcal{I}(\beta)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}(\beta)^{-1},

as was also claimed. Next, we need to show that
\mathcal{I}(\beta)^{-1}\preceq A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f](A_{f}^{T})^{-1}
for all functions
f:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
for which
A_{f}
is positive definite. However, before we do that, it will aid us greatly in our endeavor if we first take a small digression, and prove the following two facts:

•

\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]
is positive definite.

•

\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g]\preceq\mathcal{I}(\beta)
for any
g:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
satisfying
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g]=A_{g}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[g]=0
.

So, let us start by showing that
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]
is positive definite. Note that the positive definiteness of
A_{f}
means that its rows are linearly independent, and so:

\displaystyle A_{f}\text{ is positive definite}

\displaystyle\implies\nexists\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}/0_{m}\ \text{ s.t. }\ \alpha^{\prime}A_{f}=0_{m}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\implies\nexists\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}/0_{m}\ \text{ s.t. }\ \alpha^{\prime}(\mathbb{E}_{j}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f])=0\ \forall j\in[m]

\displaystyle\implies\nexists\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}/0_{m}\ \text{ s.t. }\ \mathbb{E}_{j}[\alpha^{\prime}f]=\mathbb{E}_{0}[\alpha^{\prime}f]\ \forall j\in[m]

\displaystyle\implies\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}/0_{m},\ \exists j\in[m]\ \text{ s.t. }\ \mathbb{E}_{j}[\alpha^{\prime}f]\neq\mathbb{E}_{0}[\alpha^{\prime}f].

Next, we will prove that

\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}/0_{m},\ \exists j\in[m]\ \text{ s.t. }\ \mathbb{E}_{j}[\alpha^{\prime}f]\neq\mathbb{E}_{0}[\alpha^{\prime}f]\implies\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}/0_{m},\ \nexists\eta\in\mathbb{R}\ \text{ s.t. }\ \mathbb{P}_{\beta}[\alpha^{\prime}f=\eta]=1

(17)

by proving the contrapositive, i.e., that:

\exists\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}/0_{m},\eta\in\mathbb{R}\ \text{ s.t. }\ \mathbb{P}_{\beta}[\alpha^{\prime}f=\eta]=1\implies\exists\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}/0_{m}\ \text{ s.t. }\ \mathbb{E}_{j}[\alpha^{\prime}f]=\mathbb{E}_{0}[\alpha^{\prime}f]\ \forall j\in[m].

Towards that end, note that since
\mathbb{P}_{\beta}[\alpha^{\prime}f=\eta]=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\mathbb{P}_{y}[\alpha^{\prime}f=\eta]
and
\beta_{y}>0
for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, if
\mathbb{P}_{\beta}[\alpha^{\prime}f=\eta]=1
then it must be that
\mathbb{P}_{y}[\alpha^{\prime}f=\eta]=1
for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
as well. Ergo:

\displaystyle\exists\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}/0_{m},\eta\in\mathbb{R}\ \text{ s.t. }\ \mathbb{P}_{\beta}[\alpha^{\prime}f=\eta]=1

\displaystyle\implies\exists\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}/0_{m},\eta\in\mathbb{R}\ \text{ s.t. }\ \mathbb{P}_{y}[\alpha^{\prime}f=\eta]=1\ \forall y\in\mathcal{Y}

\displaystyle\implies\exists\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}/0_{m},\eta\in\mathbb{R}\ \text{ s.t. }\ \mathbb{E}_{y}[\alpha^{\prime}f]=\eta\ \forall y\in\mathcal{Y}

\displaystyle\implies\exists\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}/0_{m}\ \text{ s.t. }\ \mathbb{E}_{j}[\alpha^{\prime}f]=\mathbb{E}_{0}[\alpha^{\prime}f]\ \forall j\in[m].

Thus, the implication in display (
17
) is indeed true, so it follows that:

\displaystyle A_{f}\text{ is positive definite}

\displaystyle\implies\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}/0_{m},\ \nexists\eta\in\mathbb{R}\ \text{ s.t. }\ \mathbb{P}_{\beta}[\alpha^{\prime}f=\eta]=1

\displaystyle\implies\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}/0_{m},\ \textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[\alpha^{\prime}f]>0

\displaystyle\implies\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}/0_{m},\ \alpha^{\prime}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]\alpha>0

\displaystyle\implies\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]\text{ is positive definite}.

Next, we establish that
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g]\preceq\mathcal{I}(\beta)
for any
g:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
satisfying
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g]=A_{g}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[g]=0
. Towards that end, observe that by Lemma (
2.2
), we have that
A_{g}=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[gs_{\beta}^{T}]
, meaning that
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g]=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[gs_{\beta}^{T}]
. So, for any vector
w\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
:

\displaystyle w^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g]w

\displaystyle=w^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[gs_{\beta}^{T}]w

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}\big[(w^{T}g)(w^{T}s_{\beta})\big]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\beta}\big[(w^{T}g)^{2}\big]\mathbb{E}_{\beta}\big[(w^{T}s_{\beta})^{2}\big]}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[w^{T}g]\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[w^{T}s_{\beta}]}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{w^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g]w\cdot w^{T}\mathcal{I}(\beta)w},

where the second to last line is because
\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[g]=0=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[s_{\beta}]
. Thus, we have that
w^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g]w\leq w^{T}\mathcal{I}(\beta)w
, meaning that
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g]\preceq\mathcal{I}(\beta)
.

Having completed our digression, we now turn to proving that
\mathcal{I}(\beta)^{-1}\preceq A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f](A_{f}^{T})^{-1}
for all functions
f:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
for which
A_{f}
is positive definite. Note that this is equivalent to proving that
A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}\preceq\mathcal{I}(\beta)
. Now, given any function
f
such that
A_{f}
is positive definite, it follows from our digression that
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]
is also positive definite, so its inverse exists and we can define a new function
g_{f}:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
as follows:

g_{f}:=A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}(f-\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f]).

Observe that:

\displaystyle A_{g_{f}}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g_{f}]^{-1}A_{g_{f}}^{T}

\displaystyle=\Big(A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}\Big)\Big(A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}\Big)^{-1}\Big(A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}\Big)^{T}

\displaystyle=\Big(A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}\Big)\Big(A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}\Big)^{-1}\Big(A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}\Big)

\displaystyle=A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}.

Thus, given any
f
for which
A_{f}
is positive definite, in order to show that
A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}\preceq\mathcal{I}(\beta)
, it suffices to instead show that
A_{g_{f}}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g_{f}]^{-1}A_{g_{f}}^{T}\preceq\mathcal{I}(\beta)
. Next, note that

\displaystyle\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g_{f}]

\displaystyle=A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}

\displaystyle=A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}

\displaystyle=A_{A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}f}

\displaystyle=A_{g_{f}},

so
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g_{f}]=A_{g_{f}}
. Thus,
A_{g_{f}}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g_{f}]^{-1}A_{g_{f}}^{T}=\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g_{f}]
, so it suffices to instead show that
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g_{f}]\preceq\mathcal{I}(\beta)
. By our digression from earlier, this is indeed true, because
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[g_{f}]=A_{g_{f}}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[g_{f}]=0
. Therefore, we indeed have that
\mathcal{I}(\beta)^{-1}\preceq A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f](A_{f}^{T})^{-1}
for all
f
for which
A_{f}
is positive definite.

Finally, we will prove that if the eigenvalues of
A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f](A_{f}^{T})^{-1}
and
\mathcal{I}(\beta)^{-1}
are identical, then there exists a constant vector
c\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and a constant invertible matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
such that
f=Bs_{\beta}+c
a.e.
[p_{\beta}]
. Towards that end, note that if the eigenvalues of
A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f](A_{f}^{T})^{-1}
and
\mathcal{I}(\beta)^{-1}
are identical, then the eigenvalues of
A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}
and
\mathcal{I}(\beta)
are as well. So,
\text{Tr}\big\{A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}\big\}=\text{Tr}\big\{\mathcal{I}(\beta)\big\}
, i.e.,
\text{Tr}\big\{\mathcal{I}(\beta)-A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}\big\}=0
. But, we just showed that
\mathcal{I}(\beta)^{-1}\preceq A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f](A_{f}^{T})^{-1}
, so
\mathcal{I}(\beta)-A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}
is positive semidefinite, i.e., all the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{I}(\beta)-A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}
must be non-negative. Thus, the fact that
\text{Tr}\big\{\mathcal{I}(\beta)-A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}\big\}=0
means that the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{I}(\beta)-A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}
must actually all be equal to zero. In particular, we have that
\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\beta)-A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}\big)=0
.

In addition, note that
\mathcal{I}(\beta)-A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}
can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[s_{\beta}s_{\beta}^{T}]-\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[s_{\beta}(f-\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f])^{T}]\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[(f-\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f])(f-\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f])^{T}]^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[(f-\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f])s_{\beta}^{T}].

So, since
\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\beta)-A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}A_{f}\big)=0
and
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]
is positive definite, it follows from Lemma (
2.4
) that

\displaystyle s_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[s_{\beta}(f-\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f])^{T}]\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[(f-\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f])(f-\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f])^{T}]^{-1}(f-\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f])\quad\textup{ a.e. }[p_{\beta}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[s_{\beta}f^{T}]\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}(f-\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f])\quad\textup{ a.e. }[p_{\beta}]

\displaystyle=A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}(f-\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f])\quad\textup{ a.e. }[p_{\beta}].

Since
A_{f}
and
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}
are both invertible, it follows that
A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1}
is also invertible. Thus, the display above implies that:

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=(A_{f}^{T}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]^{-1})^{-1}s_{\beta}+\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f]\quad\textup{ a.e. }[p_{\beta}]

\displaystyle=\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]A_{f}^{-T}s_{\beta}+\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f]\quad\textup{ a.e. }[p_{\beta}].

Set
c=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[f]
and
B=\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]A_{f}^{-T}
, where
B
is invertible because
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]
and
A_{f}^{-T}
are both invertible. Thus, we have found a constant invertible matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
and constant vector
c\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that
f=Bs_{\beta}+c\textup{ a.e. }[p_{\beta}]
, which proves the desiderata.
∎

3
Theoretical Results

In this section, we dive into some of SELSE’s theoretical properties, with the ultimate goal of showing that SELSE is semiparametric efficient. Towards that end, the section is organized as follows. First, we make the Methodology section’s discussion of SELSE’s asymptotic behavior rigorous by providing a mathematical foundation for the intuitive arguments used in that section. This includes a more precise analysis of SELSE’s first order error term, and a characterization of the rate of its second order error term. All of this is done in the traditional quantification setting discussed in the Methodology section.

Second, having established SELSE’s asymptotic behavior in the traditional quantification setting, we then set out to prove that SELSE is semiparametric efficient. To that end, we motivate why a semiparametric framework is appealing for studying quantification in the first place, and in particular, the performance of quantifiers. We then argue why adjusting the generative process for our data is necessary if we want to utilize existing results from the semiparametrics literature in our derivation of the bound.

Third, we describe two new data generating regimes that are readily handled by the semiparametric literature and retain all key aspects of the traditional quantification problem, as was originally described in the Methodology section. We explain how these two regimes compare to the original, and provide a high-level game plan of how we will use the new regimes and our previous analysis of SELSE’s asymptotic behavior to prove that SELSE is semiparametric efficient.

Fourth, with this game plan in mind, we then dive into the details of the two new regimes. This includes reviewing their data generating mechanisms, as well as stating the assumptions we make about their parameter spaces. We make sure to present the intuition behind these assumptions, and provide justifications for why they are either necessary or reasonable to assume. We also introduce a large family of quantifiers
\mathcal{E}
that we plan to develop the semiparametric efficiency bound for.

Fifth, having described the two new regimes in precise mathematical detail, we execute our aforementioned game plan, touring the intermediate lemmas and theorems in the two new regimes that strategically position us to prove our main result for SELSE. We also provide the intuition behind these intermediate results, and in doing so, we take a deeper dive into the concept of semiparametric efficiency.

Finally, with this groundwork in place, we present the semiparametric efficiency bound, and show that SELSE is semiparametric efficient. We then conclude the section by analyzing the bound, and we discuss how it reveals deep connections between the general problem of quantification and two other statistical estimation tasks.

3.1
Asymptotics of SELSE in Fixed Sequence Regime

In order to rigorously show that SELSE has the smallest possible asymptotic variance, we first needed to formalize the intuitive arguments that were used in subsection (
2.5
) to identify that matrix, and more broadly, characterize SELSE’s overall asymptotic behavior. In particular, while those arguments were able to correctly deduce the form of SELSE’s asymptotic variance matrix, this conclusion could only be reached by assuming that the true score function
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
was known. However, in reality, SELSE only has access to an estimate
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
. Thus, we begin this subsection by describing the lemmas that enabled us to show that
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
converges to
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
, and determine the rate at which this occurs. We then show how this result allowed us to formally characterize the first and second order terms in SELSE’s normalized error.

The backbone of our proof for showing the convergence of
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
to
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
is a moment condition that characterizes
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
. As mentioned in subsection (
2.5.2
), the components of
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
possess a special property: for each
y\in[m]
,
s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
is a member of

C_{\gamma^{\ast},y}:=\Big\{h_{y}\ \Big\lvert\ h_{y}:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R},\ \textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[h_{y}]=\mathbb{E}_{y}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[h_{y}]\Big\},

(18)

and among all functions
h_{y}
in that set,
\mathbb{E}_{y}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[h_{y}]
is
uniquely
maximized by choosing
h_{y}=s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
. This property follows directly from Lemma (
2.3
), and was our motivation for how we form the estimate
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}
in Step 3 of our algorithm. Indeed, the constraint set

\widehat{C}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}=\Big\{h_{y}\in\mathcal{H}_{y}\text{ }:\text{ }\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\widehat{\gamma}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]=\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]\Big\}

in Step 3 is the empirical counterpart to
C_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
in display (
18
), and in that step, we are choosing
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}
to be the
h_{y}\in\widehat{C}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}
that maximizes the difference
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]
, which is the empirical counterpart to
\mathbb{E}_{y}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[h_{y}]
. This correspondence suggests a strategy for proving the convergence of
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
: since it seems reasonable to believe that
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
will be inside of
C_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
for large sample sizes (with high probability), and the unique maximization property of
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
implies that
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}=s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
whenever it is the case that
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}\in C_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
and
\mathbb{E}_{y}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}]=\mathbb{E}_{y}[s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]
, then in order to argue that
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}\approx s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
, it may suffice to show that
\mathbb{E}_{y}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\approx\mathbb{E}_{y}[s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]
. That is, it is reasonable to believe that bounding the magnitude of the difference

\big(\mathbb{E}_{y}[s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\big)-\big(\mathbb{E}_{y}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\big)

(19)

will lead to a bound on
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}-s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
. To bound the difference above, we consider its positive and negative parts separately. The following lemma addresses its positive part.

Lemma 3.1

(
Rate for Learning Diagonal of
\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})
, When
\gamma^{\ast}
is Known.
)
.

Under Assumptions (
A1
), (
•
‣
A2
), (
A3
), (
A5
), and (
A6
) in the Appendix, we have for each
y\in[m]
that

\big(\mathbb{E}_{y}[s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\big)-\big(\mathbb{E}_{y}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\big)\leq T_{y},

where
T_{y}>0
is a random variable that satisfies

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}[T_{y}]=O\Bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}+\lambda_{y}\Omega^{2}(s_{\gamma^{\ast},y})+\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}[\mathcal{U}_{y}(\lambda_{y})]+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}e^{-C_{1}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg),

for some constant
C_{1}>0
independent of
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
or
n^{\textup{\text{te}}}
. Further, there exists constants
C_{2},C_{3}>0
independent of
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
and
n^{\textup{\text{te}}}
such that, for all
a>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}[T_{y}\geq a]

\displaystyle\leq 16(2m+1)\Big(\exp\Big\{-C_{2}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\tau(a)\Big\}+\exp\Big\{-C_{3}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\sqrt{\tau(a)}\Big\}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbbold{1}{\Big\{\lambda_{y}\Omega^{2}(s_{\gamma^{\ast},y})\geq\frac{1}{12}a\Big\}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{y}[h_{y}]\Big|\geq\frac{1}{6}a\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[h_{y}]\Big|\geq\frac{1}{6}a\Bigg],

where
\tau(a):=\min\big\{1,a^{2},\frac{a}{\lambda_{y}\Omega^{2}(s_{\gamma^{\ast},y})}\big\}
.

The proof for Lemma (
3.1
) can be found in the Appendix. Assumptions (
A1
)-(
A7
