) are actually analogous to the assumptions presented and thoroughly investigated in the upcoming subsections (
3.5.1
)-(
3.5.4
), and are phrased in terms of the semiparametric framework soon to be introduced in subsection (
3.5
). The main reason for this redundancy is that it was far more convenient to prove the lemmas and theorems in the present subsection using Assumptions (
A1
)-(
A7
) than their semiparametric counterparts. However, to avoid unnecessary confusion and in anticipation of subsections (
3.5.1
)-(
3.5.4
), we hold off on describing Assumptions (
A1
)-(
A7
) here, though the curious reader can read about them in the Appendix. The quantities
L
and
\mathcal{U}_{y}(\lambda_{y})
in the statement of Lemma (
3.1
) will also be formally defined in subsections (
3.5.1
)-(
3.5.4
), but essentially,
L\in\mathbb{R}
is a constant that can be shown to uniformly lower bound the components of
\gamma^{\ast}
(proven in Lemma (
6.1
) in the Appendix), and
\mathcal{U}_{y}(\lambda_{y})
denotes a sum of uniform deviation terms that reflect the tension between the (potentially growing) complexity of the space
\mathcal{H}_{y}
and
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
, and should satisfy
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}[\mathcal{U}_{y}(\lambda_{y})]\to 0
as
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\to\infty
.

In a nutshell, Lemma (
3.1
) shows that the positive part of the difference

\big(\mathbb{E}_{y}[s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\big)-\big(\mathbb{E}_{y}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\big)

approaches
0
at a rate which depends on the number of train samples used to approximate
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
, the complexity of
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
and the function space it lives in, as well as the amount of regularization. Provided that
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\to\infty
sufficiently quickly and
\lambda_{y}\to 0
sufficiently slowly, we will have that
T_{y}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
. Of course, what is considered
“sufficiently quick”
or
“sufficiently slow”
will depend on how the complexity of
\mathcal{H}_{y}
grows; to provide the reader a sense of what these rates look like, we consider an example where
\mathcal{H}_{y}
is assumed to be a union of different RKHSs in Lemma (
6.15.2
) in the Appendix. Also note that Lemma (
3.1
) is phrased in terms of
\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast}}
, but we are interested in
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
. To be precise,
\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast}}
is the version of
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
that would have been obtained if
\widehat{\gamma}=\gamma^{\ast}
in Step 2 of our algorithm. The analogue of Lemma (
3.1
) for
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
is Lemma (
6.10
) in the Appendix– we merely opted to describe the result for
\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast}}
, since it is conceptually identical to the result for
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
but the actual statement for the latter is much more complicated, as it also depends on the rate at which
\widehat{\gamma}
estimates
\gamma^{\ast}
.

Bounding the magnitude negative part of display (
19
) is far easier to do than its positive counterpart, and also depends on
T_{y}
. The main idea is that if
\widehat{C}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}=C_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
, then the maximization property of
s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
implies that (
19
) should be non-negative, so if
\widehat{C}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}\approx C_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
, then we have reason to believe it should typically not be too negative. The degree to which
\widehat{C}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}\approx C_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
depends on the extent to which empirical moments can be used to approximate population moments, and this is captured by
\mathcal{U}_{y}(\lambda_{y})
and hence by
T_{y}
. Combining this intuition for the magnitude of the negative part of display (
19
) with Lemma (
3.1
)’s bound on the positive part, we were able to prove the following result about the error of our score function estimate.

Lemma 3.2

(
Rate for Learning the Score Function, When
\gamma^{\ast}
is Known.
)
.

Under Assumptions (
A1
), (
•
‣
A2
), (
A3
), (
A5
) and (
A6
) in the Appendix, we have for each
y\in[m]
that

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Big[\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}-s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\Big]=O\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}[T_{y}]\Big).

Further, for all
a>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Big[\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}-s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\geq a\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\bigg[T_{y}\geq\frac{1}{4}a\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[h_{y}]\Big|\geq\frac{1}{4}a\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{y}[h_{y}]\Big|\geq\frac{1}{4}a\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[h_{y}]\Big|\geq\frac{1}{4}a\Bigg].

Note that Lemma (
3.2
) only applies to
\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
, but we are interested in
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}
. Our reason for presenting Lemma (
3.2
) is the same as our reason for presenting Lemma (
3.1
) instead of Lemma (
6.10
) from earlier: namely, the result for the estimation error
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}-s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
is nearly identical to the result for the estimation error
\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}-s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
, but the statement for the latter is far more complicated than the former, and so it is saved for the Appendix in Lemma (
6.11
).

Overall, Lemma (
3.2
) shows that the quality of
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}
as an estimate of the score function depends on the same factors as
T_{y}
: the amount of training samples and the complexity of the space
\mathcal{H}_{y}
. Provided that these quantities grow at appropriate rates, we can expect that the true score function can be learned. Most importantly, this convergence result opens the floodgates for the results previously described in the Methodology section to be used, which had assumed that SELSE had access to the true score function. In particular, we can now rigorously show that the first order term of SELSE’s total error is
\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}\big(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)
, which is the error incurred when
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}=s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
and is due to using
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}
to approximate the population moments in equation (
5
). In fact, we were able to fully characterize this first order term up to its asymptotic distribution, as seen in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3

(
First Order Term
)
.

Under Assumptions (
A4
), (
A5
), and (
A6
) in the Appendix, we have that

\Bigg(\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\Big[\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}\big(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)\Big]\Bigg)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}\big(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.09723ptd\hskip 4.09723pt}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{m}).

The proof for Lemma (
3.3
) is in the Appendix and makes use of standard triangular array central limit theorems. The following theorem expands on Lemma (
3.3
), providing both a closed form for the variance of SELSE’s first order error term and a rate-exact characterization of its second order error term.

Theorem 3.4

(
Normalized Error of
\widehat{\pi}
)
.

Under Assumptions (
A1
), (
•
‣
A2
), (
A3
), (
A4
), (
A5
), (
A6
) and (
A7
) in the Appendix, we have that

\displaystyle\Bigg(\Bigg[\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg]\Big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Big)+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Bigg)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\widehat{\pi}-\pi^{\ast})

\displaystyle=Z+\epsilon,

where

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\Bigg[\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg]\Big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Big)+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Bigg)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{i}^{\textup{eff}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.09723ptd\hskip 4.09723pt}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{m})

where
n=n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}+n^{\textup{\text{te}}}
,
\psi_{i}^{\textup{eff}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a function of the
{i}^{\text{th}}
data point and is defined in Lemma (
6.8
) in the Appendix, and
\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a random vector satisfying

\displaystyle||\epsilon||_{2}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbb{P}}\Bigg(\sqrt{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}+\max_{y}\lambda_{y}\Omega(s_{\gamma^{\ast},y})^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\min_{y}\lambda_{y}}}e^{-Cn^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{\mathbb{P}}\Bigg(\sqrt{\max_{y}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Big[\mathcal{U}_{y}(\lambda_{y})\Big]+\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Big[||\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\ast}||_{2}^{2}\Big]}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{\mathbb{P}}\Bigg({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1/n^{\textup{\text{te}}}+1/n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}}\bigg\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e^{-C(\min_{y}\lambda_{y})^{2}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Big[\big|\big|\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\ast}\big|\big|_{2}\geq C\Big]\bigg\}\Bigg),

for some global constant
C>0
.

Whereas the first order error was due to the method of moments approximation of equation (
5
), Theorem (
3.4
) makes clear that the second order error
\epsilon
depends on how fast
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}-s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]
goes to
0
, as seen by how the rates for both
\epsilon
and
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}-s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]
depend on the same factors used to bound
T_{y}
, i.e.,
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
and the complexity of
\mathcal{H}_{y}
. Provided that
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
grows sufficiently fast relative to this complexity, Theorem (
3.4
) implies that
\epsilon=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
, in which case asymptotically, the normalized error is fully characterized by the aforementioned first order term. Furthermore, it is important to note that, unlike the other lemmas previously presented in this subsection, Theorem (
3.4
) applies to the
complete
SELSE algorithm, in that it accounts for the fact that
\gamma^{\ast}
must be estimated via
\widehat{\gamma}
. Theorem (
3.4
) is also able to remove the factor of
2
that appeared when we first stated SELSE’s asymptotic variance in subsection (
2.5
) as a result of Step 1’s sample splitting. This was achievable because of Step 5, which takes
\widehat{\pi}
to be the average of
\widehat{\pi}^{(a)}
and
\widehat{\pi}^{(b)}
. Ultimately, Theorem (
3.4
) paints a comprehensive picture of SELSE’s asymptotic behavior, and in doing so, it provides a much needed theoretical backbone to support the arguments made in the Methodology section.

3.2
Motivation for Semiparametric Statistics

Having established SELSE’s asymptotic behavior in the traditional quantification setting, our next goal is to show that SELSE is semiparametric efficient. However, before we can do that, we first must answer two fundamental questions: what even
is
semiparametric statistics, and why are we using it in the first place? This subsection serves to address precisely those questions, and in doing so, will provide motivation for the work we do later on.

In virtually all data analysis settings, it is often desirable to make the weakest possible assumptions about the data at hand– that is, rather than
“speaking”
for the data, we would like the dataset to speak for itself. From a statistician’s point of view, this often translates to restricting oneself to a
nonparametric
model for the data generating mechanism. However, there are also times when one has prior knowledge about certain parts of the underlying mechanism, and incorporating that knowledge would help to improve both the quality and accuracy of the statistical models we fit.

A simple way of incorporating such knowledge is to allow certain components of the model to be
parametric
, rather than nonparametric. Typically, the nonparametric components reflect those aspects of the data generating mechanism that we cannot confidently make assumptions about, and so we must model them flexibly; in contrast, the parametric components reflect those aspects which we feel comfortable making strong assumptions about, and so we can model them rigidly. This is directly applicable to quantification tasks. In quantification tasks, the problem definition assumes that the hidden label of a test sample follows the
\textup{Cat}(\pi^{\ast})
distribution for some
\pi^{\ast}
in the
m+1
dimensional probability simplex, which is a parametric model. However, we do not know anything about the conditional density of the covariate given the label, except of course that they are unchanged between train and test by virtue of the label shift assumption; as such, those component densities are modeled nonparametrically.

Semiparametric statistics
studies precisely these situations. In the language of that field,
\pi^{\ast}
is the finite-dimensional
parameter of interest
, and
\mathbf{p}
is the infinite-dimensional
nuisance parameter
. The goal is to learn the parameter of interest in an effective
fashion. Since the nuisance parameter is generally hard to estimate because it is infinite-dimensional, this goal typically translates to learning the parameter of interest in a fashion that requires us to learn the
least
about the nuisance parameter. In other words: how can we learn
\pi^{\ast}
, given that we don’t care about learning
\mathbf{p}
?

The question stated above articulates both the attitude and objective of the quantification field, and it is for this reason that we believe using a semiparametric framework is appropriate. Moreover, for our own goals, this framework is also actively desirable: as discussed previously, we would like to prove that SELSE has the smallest possible asymptotic variance. So far, we have only been able to establish SELSE’s optimality among ACC/PACC quantifiers, but ideally, we would like to universalize this result to quantifiers in general. Fortunately, the semiparametric literature is equipped with the tools to do exactly that. For an excellent and mathematically rigorous presentation of those tools, we refer the interested reader to the seminal book by
Bickel et al., 1993
; for a simplified and more user-friendly version of those same tools, we point the reader to the paper by
Newey, 1990
.

3.3
Derivation Roadmap

Despite the aforementioned appeal of the semiparametric framework, one limitation of that literature is that its most important results tend to be isolated to regimes where the data is generated IID from a single population. This deviates from the traditional quantification setting which we had presented in the Methodology section, where due to label shift, the train and test sets are generated from
two
different populations. As we developed our research, this put us in an especially awkward position: we saw that analyzing the asymptotic behavior of SELSE was conceptually easier to do in the traditional quantification setting, yet to show that our variance was the smallest among a large class of quantifiers, it seemed like the best approach was to use the single population setting that was ubiquitous in the semiparametric literature.

Faced with this conflict, we decided to make the best of both worlds. On a high level, our approach was as follows. First, we established the asymptotic properties of SELSE in the traditional quantification setting, which included identifying SELSE’s asymptotic variance matrix. In the Appendix, this setting is referred to as the
“
Fixed Sequence Regime
”
. The results of those efforts have already been presented in subsection (
3.1
). Second, we also separately considered a modified version of the traditional quantification setting, wherein all samples are generated IID from a single density, and a random indicator
D\in\{0,1\}
determines whether a sample belongs to test or train; i.e.,
D_{i}
decides whether the
{i}^{\text{th}}
sample is drawn from
p_{\pi^{\ast}}
or
p_{X,Y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
. This setting is referred to as the
“
Fixed
\tau
-IID Regime
”
in the Appendix, because it is assumes that
D\sim\text{Bern}(\tau)
for some fixed
\tau\in(0,1)
. Crucially, notice that the Fixed
\tau
-IID Regime preserves the essence of the quantification problem: we receive labeled train and unlabeled test samples, with the sole distributional discrepancy still being due to label shift. The only difference is that now the
number
of train and test samples is also random. In this regime, our goal was to derive a preliminary version of the semiparametric efficiency bound– the bound was
“preliminary”
because it could only apply to settings where
\tau
was
fixed
. This was inadequate for us, because we also wanted it to cover settings where the train set size grows far faster than the test set size (or vice versa), which requires
\tau\to 0
(or
\tau\to 1
) as
n\to\infty
. However, we made the strategic choice to obtain the semiparametric efficiency bound for this simplified setting first, as it made achieving the desired, more flexible bound far easier.

This is where the
“
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime
”
comes in. This third regime is nearly identical to the Fixed
\tau
-Regime, differing only in that
\tau
is replaced with
\tau_{n}
, which may change with
n
. In this regime, we had three goals. First, using the results from the Fixed Sequence Regime as our stepping stone, we translated the asymptotic properties of SELSE to the
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime. Second, we generalized the semiparametric efficiency bound in the Fixed
\tau
-Regime to accomodate the fact that
\tau_{n}
changes with
n
. Third, combining the results from our first and second goals, we showed that SELSE’s asymptotic variance matrix was equal to the aforementioned semiparametric efficiency bound. Ultimately, by using the three regimes in this way, we were able to show that SELSE is semiparametric efficient.

3.4
Data Generation

We now formally introduce the mechanism used in the
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime. As alluded to in subsection (
3.3
), the modification is conceptually straightforward: rather than observing
n^{\textup{\text{te}}}
IID test samples from
p_{\pi^{\ast}}
and
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
IID train samples from
p_{X,Y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
, we now obtain
n\geq 1
IID copies of a random vector
Z=(X,Y,D)
, which is generated as follows:

•

D\sim\text{Bern}(\tau_{n})

•

If
D=0
, then
(X,Y)\sim p_{X,Y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}

•

If
D=1
, then
X\sim p_{\pi^{\ast}}
and
Y=-1
.

If
D=0
, then
(X,Y)
is a labeled training sample, and if
D=1
, then
X
is an unlabeled test sample (hence the dummy value of
Y=-1
). The probability of generating a test sample from this process is
\tau_{n}\in(0,1)
, which may depend on
n
. For simplicity, we assume that both
\tau_{n}
and
\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
are known. Based on the process displayed above, the density function for the distribution of
Z
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}(z)

\displaystyle:=\big(\tau_{n}p_{\pi^{\ast}}(x)\big)^{d}\big((1-\tau_{n})p_{X,Y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}(x,y)\big)^{1-d}\ \mathbbold{1}{\big\{x\in\mathcal{X},\text{ }y\in\mathcal{Y},\text{ }d\in\{0,1\}\big\}}

\displaystyle=\big(\tau_{n}p_{\pi^{\ast}}(x)\big)^{d}\Bigg((1-\tau_{n})\prod_{j=0}^{m}\big(\pi_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}p_{j}(x)\big)^{\mathbbold{1}{\{y=j\}}}\Bigg)^{1-d}\mathbbold{1}{\big\{x\in\mathcal{X},\text{ }y\in\mathcal{Y},\text{ }d\in\{0,1\}\big\}}.

(20)

The final dataset is then
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}
, with the number of test and train samples given by
N^{\textup{\text{te}}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}
and
N^{\textup{\text{tr}}}:=n-N^{\textup{\text{te}}}
, respectively. Although
N^{\textup{\text{te}}}
and
N^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
are now random, recall that the benefit of working with this data generation mechanism is that all data points are from a
single
population (i.e.,
n
IID samples from a single density), which is studied by the semiparametric literature far more often than settings where there are multiple sets of samples from different populations (i.e.,
n^{\textup{\text{te}}}
test samples and
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
train samples). This regime therefore enables us to make the most out of existing results in the semiparametric literature when proving our efficiency bound.

3.5
Model Specification

Having described the new data generating mechanism, we now present several modeling assumptions. We assume that
\pi^{\ast},\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\in\Delta
, where
\Delta
is a subset of the
m+1
dimensional probability simplex. We also assume that
\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{Q}
, where
\mathcal{Q}:=\mathcal{Q}_{0}\times\dots\times\mathcal{Q}_{m}
and each
\mathcal{Q}_{y}
is a set of densities. The full parameter space is
\Theta^{\text{semi}}=\Delta\times\mathcal{Q}
, and our semiparametric model is

\textbf{M}^{\text{semi},\tau_{n}}:=\big\{\mathcal{J}^{\pi,\mathbf{q},\tau_{n}}\ \big\lvert\ (\pi,\mathbf{q})\in\Theta^{\text{semi}}\big\},

where for each parameter
(\pi,\mathbf{q})\in\Theta^{\text{semi}}
,
\mathcal{J}^{\pi,\mathbf{q},\tau_{n}}
is a density function for the random vector
Z
described in the previous subsection, except with
\mathbf{q}
replacing
\mathbf{p}
and
\pi
replacing
\pi^{\ast}
in equation (
20
).

3.5.1
Assumptions on
\pi^{\ast}
and
\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}

We make several restrictions on both
\Delta
and
\mathcal{Q}
. For
\Delta
, we assume that there exists a constant
\xi\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
such that

\Delta:=\Bigg\{(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\ \Bigg\lvert\ \pi_{0}=1-\sum_{y=1}^{m}\pi_{y},\quad\xi<\pi_{y}<1-\xi\quad\forall y\in\mathcal{Y}\Bigg\}.

Since
\pi^{\ast},\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\in\Delta
, this form for
\Delta
ensures that each class is sufficiently represented in both the train and test sets. For the test set, this is not strictly necessary, but it is reasonable if one chooses
\xi
to be extremely small, and the assumption is convenient for our proofs. However, this type of assumption
is
necessary for the training set: clearly, learning about the mixing parameter used in
p_{\pi^{\ast}}
is only possible if we have some knowledge about each of the component densities involved in the mixture. Requiring that
\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\in\Delta
guarantees this, because it ensures that the average amount of training samples from each component density is greater than
n\xi
.

3.5.2
Assumptions on
\mathbf{p}

Next, we state assumptions about
\mathcal{Q}
. Towards that end, define:

L:=\frac{1}{1+m\big(\frac{1-\xi}{\xi}\big)^{3}}\qquad U:=\max\Bigg(1-\xi,\ \frac{1}{1+m\big(\frac{\xi}{1-\xi}\big)^{3}}\Bigg)

\Gamma:=\Bigg\{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\ \Bigg\lvert\ \gamma_{0}=1-\sum_{y=1}^{m}\gamma_{y},\quad L\leq\gamma_{y}\leq U\quad\forall y\in\mathcal{Y}\Bigg\}.

We assume that there exists constants
\nu,\Lambda,B>0
such that the following conditions hold for all
\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{Q}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
:

B1.

\sigma_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma)\big)>\sqrt{\Lambda}
.

B2.

\sigma_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma;\textup{Cat})-\mathcal{I}(\gamma)\big)>\nu
.

B3.

(q_{0}e^{\rho^{\prime}\zeta_{0}s_{\gamma}-c_{0}(\rho)},\dots,q_{m}e^{\rho^{\prime}\zeta_{m}s_{\gamma}-c_{m}(\rho)})\in\mathcal{Q}
for all
\rho\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
satisfying
||\rho||_{2}<B
and scalars
\zeta_{j}
satisfying
0\leq\zeta_{j}<\frac{1-\xi}{\xi}
.

Assumptions (
B1
) and (
B2
) place bounds on the information that a test sample can contain about
\pi^{\ast}
. Here’s how. On the one hand, Assumption (
B1
) ensures that no two component densities are the same: if it were the case that
\sigma_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma)\big)=0
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
, then it is easy to show that there would exist at least two component densities which are equal to each other with probability 1, and
\pi^{\ast}
would be unidentifiable. A simple example of this unidentifiability comes from the binary class case: if
p_{0}=p_{1}
, then
p_{\beta}
would be the same for all
\beta\in(0,1)
, in which case it is impossible to learn
\pi^{\ast}
from the test data. That is, even though
X\sim p_{\pi^{\ast}}
,
X
contains zero information about
\pi^{\ast}
.

On the other hand, Assumption (
B2
) ensures that the component densities’ supports are not all disjoint. This insight comes from the following lemma:

Lemma 3.5

(
Relationship between
\mathcal{I}(\beta)
and
\mathcal{I}(\beta;\textup{Cat})
)
.

Let
\beta
be a vector in the
m+1
dimensional probability simplex, and suppose that the entries in
\beta
are bounded away from
0
and
1
. Then, we have that

\mathcal{I}(\beta)\preceq\mathcal{I}(\beta;\textup{Cat}),

where equality is achieved if and only if the supports of the class distributions are all disjoint, i.e.,

\mathcal{I}(\beta;\textup{Cat})=\mathcal{I}(\beta)\iff\mathbb{P}_{y}\Bigg[\sum_{j=0\neq y}^{m}p_{j}(X)=0\Bigg]=1\qquad\forall y\in\mathcal{Y}.

Lemma (
3.5
) implies that equality between
\mathcal{I}(\gamma;\textup{Cat})
and
\mathcal{I}(\gamma)
happens if and only if all the component densities’ supports are disjoint; since Assumption (
B2
) prevents this equality, it follows that (
B2
) ensures that the component densities have at least
some
overlap.

Actually, overlapping component densities is not a necessary requirement for establishing the performance of SELSE; indeed, the more separated the densities are, the better quantifiers tend to perform. This can be easily seen for SELSE by examining its asymptotic variance, as presented in Theorem (
3.9
). However, it
is
necessary if we want the efficiency bound to be achievable. To see why, suppose that the supports were all disjoint. Then, every
x\in\mathcal{X}
corresponds to a single class
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, i.e.,
\varrho(x)=y
for some deterministic function
\varrho
. It is easy to show that
\varrho
is a sufficient statistic for
\pi^{\ast}
, implying that the information that each test sample
X
contains about
\pi^{\ast}
equals the information that the missing test label
Y
contains about
\pi^{\ast}
(hence the equality of Fisher Informations in Lemma (
3.5
)). The fact that these information contents are equal
despite
us having only imperfect knowledge about
\varrho
via the training data is problematic: it implies that, in order for a quantifier to be
“efficient”
, its first-order error must be
just as small as
what is possible when
\varrho
is known perfectly! Of course, this requirement can be satisfied if the number of training samples grows far faster than the number of test samples (i.e.,
\tau_{n}\to 0
sufficiently quickly), because then the error incurred for having to learn
\varrho
will not be part of the first order error. However, rather than place restrictions on the relationship between the train and test sample sizes, we prefer to simply assume (
B2
), since in most interesting problems, the supports of the component densities are not all disjoint.

Finally, to understand Assumption (
B3
), it is helpful to first clarify ideas on what is meant by an
“efficiency bound”
in semiparametric statistics. Towards that end, suppose that
\mathcal{M}^{\text{sub},\tau_{n}}
was such that
\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}\in\mathcal{M}^{\text{sub},\tau_{n}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{\text{semi},\tau_{n}}
. Intuitively, the task of learning
\pi^{\ast}
(without necessarily learning
\mathbf{p}
) when the assumed model is
\mathcal{M}^{\text{sub},\tau_{n}}
cannot be harder than when the assumed model is
\mathcal{M}^{\text{semi},\tau_{n}}
, and this holds true for the special case of when the density vector
\mathbf{q}
involved in defining each
\mathcal{J}^{\pi,\mathbf{q},\tau_{n}}\in\mathcal{M}^{\text{sub},\tau_{n}}
belongs to a smooth parametric family. Moreover, for any such smooth parametric submodel, this estimation difficulty can be measured by the
Cramer Rao Lower Bound (CRLB)
. Thus, it is intuitive to consider the supremum of all such CRLBs (one CRLB for each possible smooth parametric submodel of
\mathcal{M}^{\text{semi},\tau_{n}}
) as the semiparametric efficiency bound for learning
\pi^{\ast}
when the assumed model is
\mathcal{M}^{\text{semi},\tau_{n}}
.

Now ideally, we would want
\mathcal{Q}
to be as large as possible, because this corresponds to making fewer assumptions about
\mathbf{p}
. However, making
\mathcal{Q}
larger can only increase the semiparametric efficiency bound, i.e., make it more difficult to learn
\pi^{\ast}
, because it would expand the set of smooth parametric submodels that the aforementioned supremum indexes over. This is precisely where Assumption (
B3
) comes in. Under this assumption, it is possible to construct a parametric submodel with a CRLB that is greater than or equal to the CRLB of
any other possible
parametric submodel. In the semiparametric literature, this is often called the
“least favorable”
submodel. This result is presented in Lemma (
6.20
), with the specific submodel described in Lemma (
6.17
), both located in the Appendix section. This result is significant, because it means that if Assumption (
B3
) holds true, then
\mathcal{Q}
can be assumed to be arbitrarily large, without the extra expense of making it harder to learn
\pi^{\ast}
!

3.5.3
Assumptions on
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}

In addition to Assumptions (
B1
), (
B2
) and (
B3
), we also place constraints on the score function formed from
\mathbf{p}
. Such assumptions are necessary because SELSE assumes that it is possible to learn
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
. However, before diving into those assumptions, it will be helpful to first establish some new notation.

Let
(\bar{n}^{\textup{\text{te}}},\bar{n}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr}}},\dots,\bar{n}_{m}^{\textup{\text{tr}}})
denote any sequence depending on
n
such that
\bar{n}^{\textup{\text{te}}}+\sum_{y=0}^{m}n_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}=n
, with each component approaching infinity. For each
(\pi,\mathbf{q})\in\Theta^{\text{semi}}
, let
\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}
and
\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
denote datasets each containing
\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{n}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
IID samples from
q_{y}
for every
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, and let
\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}
and
\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{te,II}}}
denote datasets each containing
\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{n}^{\textup{\text{te}}}
IID samples from
q_{\pi}
. Define
\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{I}}}:=(\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}},\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}})
and
\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{II}}}:=(\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}},\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{te,II}}})
. These datasets are analogous to what was described in the Methodology Section. Also define
\bar{\gamma}:=(\bar{\gamma}_{1},\dots,\bar{\gamma}_{m})
where

\bar{\gamma}_{y}:=\frac{\frac{\pi_{y}}{\bar{n}^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{(\pi_{y})^{2}}{\bar{n}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}{\frac{1}{\bar{n}^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{k})^{2}}{\bar{n}_{k}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}\qquad\forall\ y\in\mathcal{Y},

(21)

and define

\mathcal{H}^{\text{pre}}:=\Bigg\{s_{\gamma}\ \Bigg\lvert\ s_{\gamma}:=\frac{(q_{1}-q_{0},\dots,q_{m}-q_{0})^{\prime}}{\sum_{j=0}^{m}\gamma_{j}q_{j}},\quad(q_{0},\dots,q_{m})\in\mathcal{Q},\quad\gamma\in\Gamma\Bigg\}.

Finally, let
\mathcal{H}
be any set for which
\mathcal{H}^{\text{pre}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
. We assume that:

4.

\mathcal{H}
is closed under scalar multiplication.

5.

For each
j\in[m]
,
\Omega:\mathcal{H}_{j}\mapsto[0,\infty)
satisfies the following properties for each
h_{j}\in\mathcal{H}_{j}
:

•

\Omega(ch_{j})=|c|\Omega(h_{j})
for all
c\in\mathbb{R}

•

\Omega(h_{j})\geq D||h_{j}||_{\infty}
where
D>0
is a global constant.

We also make the following assumptions about the complexity of
\mathcal{H}
:

6.

For each
j\in[m]
,
(\pi,\mathbf{q})\in\Theta^{\text{semi}}
and sequence
(\bar{n}^{\textup{\text{te}}},\bar{n}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr}}},\dots,\bar{n}_{m}^{\textup{\text{tr}}})
, we assume that there exists a corresponding sequence
\lambda_{j}\in(0,\infty)
which satisfies the following properties:

•

\lambda_{j}\sup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\Omega^{2}\Big(\frac{q_{j}-q_{0}}{q_{\gamma}}\Big)\to 0

•

\lambda_{j}^{2}\sum_{k=0}^{m}\bar{n}_{k}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\to\infty

•

\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Big[\bar{\mathcal{U}}_{j}(\lambda_{j})\Big]\to 0
, where

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{U}}_{j}(\lambda_{j})

\displaystyle:=\sup\limits_{\Omega(h_{j})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{q_{0}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{j}]-\mathbb{E}_{q_{0}}[h_{j}]\Big|+\sup\limits_{\Omega(h_{j})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{q_{j}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{j}]-\mathbb{E}_{q_{j}}[h_{j}]\Big|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sup\limits_{\Omega(h_{j})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}}\Big|\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{q_{\bar{\gamma}}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{j}]-\textup{\text{Var}}_{q_{\bar{\gamma}}}[h_{j}]\Big|.

\mathcal{H}^{\text{pre}}
is the set of all possible score functions that can be created from density vectors
\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{Q}
(hence
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}\in\mathcal{H}^{\text{pre}}
), so ideally, we would choose
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
in Step 3 of SELSE by running the optimization routine on
\mathcal{H}^{\text{pre}}
. However, since there is no reason one should know
\mathcal{H}^{\text{pre}}
ahead of time, we instead run the optimization on a more conveniently known function space
\mathcal{H}
that is a superset of
\mathcal{H}^{\text{pre}}
. Fortunately, many of the common choices for spaces
\mathcal{H}
and penalty terms
\Omega
satisfy Assumptions (
4
) and (
5
). For example, if
\mathcal{H}
is a Sobolev Space or a RKHS, and
\Omega
the appropriate norm, then both multiplication properties in (
4
) and (
5
) hold true. The condition involving the
L_{\infty}
norm is also satisfied in both cases; see Theorem 8.8 in
Brezis, 2010
for when
\mathcal{H}
is a Sobolev space and see page 124 in
Steinwart and Christmann, 2008
for when
\mathcal{H}
is a RKHS with a bounded reproducing kernel.

Even though
\mathcal{H}^{\text{pre}}
is unknown, the hope is that this superset property holds if
\mathcal{H}
is chosen to be a sufficiently rich and complex function class which is allowed to grow with
n
. Indeed, the larger
\mathcal{H}
is, the more likely the superset property is satisfied. However,
\mathcal{H}
also cannot be allowed to grow
“too”
fast: based on how
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},j}
is constructed for each
j\in[m]
, the quality of
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},j}
as an estimator of
s_{\gamma^{\ast},j}
hinges on the degree to which
\mathbb{E}_{q_{0}}[h_{j}]
,
\mathbb{E}_{q_{j}}[h_{j}]
and
\textup{\text{Var}}_{q_{\bar{\gamma}}}[h_{j}]
can be well approximated by their empirical counterparts for each
h_{j}\in\mathcal{H}_{j}
. Indeed, if
\mathcal{H}_{j}
is too large relative to the sample size, then this approximation may be poor for some
h_{j}
. This is an interesting form of tension, and has been studied for decades within statistical learning theory in the context of excess risk bounds for classification and regression tasks
[
Vapnik, 1999
]
. In line with that tradition, our algorithm addresses the foregoing concern via regularization in Step 3, which effectively constrains the optimization over each
\mathcal{H}_{j}
to a ball in which
\Omega(h_{j})
is bounded. Assumption (
6
) requires that this ball’s radius tends to infinity, but at a rate that is sufficiently slow, so as to ensure that the first and second moments of those
h_{j}
inside the ball can be accurately learned.

Finally, even if
\mathcal{H}
grows extremely slowly, the particular form of the score functions in
\mathcal{H}^{\text{pre}}
may actually be enough to ensure that the superset property holds for small
n
. This is because each member of
\mathcal{H}^{\text{pre}}
only depends on the
ratio
between the component densities, not the actual densities themselves. For example, when
m=1
, it is easy to show that

\frac{q_{1}(x)-q_{0}(x)}{q_{\gamma}(x)}=\frac{1}{\gamma+\frac{1}{q_{1}(x)/q_{0}(x)-1}}\qquad\forall x\in\mathcal{X},

meaning that the score is an elementary transformation of the density ratio
q_{1}(x)/q_{0}(x)
, with a similar result holding for the more general
m\geq 1
setting. Essentially, this means that even if SELSE is applied to situations where the component densities are complicated, as long as their
density ratios
are not too complex, then the corresponding score functions will not be too complex either. Hence, in these situations, even if
\mathcal{H}
is not an expressive function class, it may be reasonable to assume that
\mathcal{H}^{\text{pre}}\subset\mathcal{H}
. An example of one of these situations is when the component densities belong to the same exponential family with an elementary sufficient statistic function
T
, but a complicated reference density. In this case, the reference density causes
q_{0},q_{1}
to be complicated functions, yet for some constant
c
, we have that
q_{1}(x)/q_{0}(x)\propto e^{\langle c,T(x)\rangle}
, which is relatively simple as it only depends on
T
.

3.5.4
Assumptions on
\widehat{\gamma}

We also require that there exists an estimator
\widehat{\gamma}
with the following properties:

7.

For each
\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{Q}
,
\pi\in\Delta
and sequence
(\bar{n}^{\textup{\text{te}}},\bar{n}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr}}},\dots,\bar{n}_{m}^{\textup{\text{tr}}})
, there exists an estimator
\widehat{\gamma}
of
\bar{\gamma}
such that:

•

\widehat{\gamma}\in\Gamma
for each
n

•

\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Big[||\widehat{\gamma}-\bar{\gamma}||_{2}^{2}\Big]\to 0

•

For each
a>0
,
\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\big[||\widehat{\gamma}-\bar{\gamma}||_{2}\geq a\big]=o\Big(\sqrt{\frac{1}{\bar{n}^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{\sum_{k=0}^{m}\bar{n}_{k}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}\Big)

These assumptions are easily satisfied when we have access to a preliminary quantifier for
\pi^{\ast}
which can be used with equation (
21
) to form a plug-in estimate
\widehat{\gamma}
of
\bar{\gamma}
, much like in our original algorithm.

3.5.5
Assumptions on
\tau_{n}

We also make the following assumptions about
\tau_{n}
:

8.

\tau_{n}\in(0,1)
for each
n
.

9.

n\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})\to\infty
.

Assumption (
8
) ensures that the dataset contains both test and train samples with high probability. Assumption (
9
) ensures that the expected number of test and train samples goes to infinity, even if the expected proportion of the data that is test or train goes to zero in the limit; that is, both
n\tau_{n},n(1-\tau_{n})\to\infty
even if
\tau_{n}\to 1
or
\tau_{n}\to 0
. These conditions enable us to consider many different sequences of
\tau_{n}
, creating a flexible model for the relationship between the two sample sizes. Examples of such sequences include:

•

\tau_{n}=\frac{1}{n^{\alpha}}
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)

•

\tau_{n}=1-\frac{1}{n^{\alpha}}
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)

•

\tau_{n}=\frac{1}{2}
.

The first two choices for
\tau_{n}
describe a relationship between the train and test sample sizes, wherein one grows faster than the other. On the one hand, when
\tau_{n}=\frac{1}{n^{\alpha}}
, it can be shown that
\mathbb{E}\big[N^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\big]\asymp\mathbb{E}\big[N^{\textup{\text{te}}}\big]^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}
where
\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\in(1,\infty)
, meaning that the average number of train samples grows polynomially in the average number of test samples. As such,
\mathbb{E}[N^{\textup{\text{tr}}}]>>\mathbb{E}[N^{\textup{\text{te}}}]
. On the other hand, when
\tau_{n}=1-\frac{1}{n^{\alpha}}
, then
\mathbb{E}\big[N^{\textup{\text{te}}}\big]\asymp\mathbb{E}\big[N^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\big]^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}
, i.e., the average number of test samples grows polynomially in the average number of train samples. As such,
\mathbb{E}[N^{\textup{\text{te}}}]>>\mathbb{E}[N^{\textup{\text{tr}}}]
. Later in this section, we will interpret the semiparametric efficiency bound in situations where
\tau_{n}\to 0
and
\tau_{n}\to 1
, and show how in each setting, the task of quantification reduces to other statistical estimation problems. The simple case of when
\tau_{n}
equals or converges to a constant in
(0,1)
can then be thought of as an intermediate
“blend”
of those two extremes.

3.6
Family of Quantifiers

Next, we describe a large family of quantifiers
\mathcal{E}
whose asymptotic variance matrix is lower bounded by our semiparametric efficiency bound, which will be presented in the subsequent subsection. This family is extremely large, containing not only SELSE and ACC/PACC quantifiers, but also other popular competitors such as HDy, EMQ and MLLS. We say that a quantifier
\widetilde{\pi}_{n}
is contained in
\mathcal{E}
if
\widetilde{\pi}_{n}
satisfies the following criteria:

C1.

For each fixed
\tau\in(0,1)
,
\widetilde{\pi}_{n}
is regular with respect to
\mathcal{M}^{\text{semi},\tau}
and asymptotically linear with influence function
\psi_{\tau}
and second order error
\delta_{\tau}
.

C2.

If
\tau_{n}
is a sequence satisfying Assumptions (
8
) and (
9
), then:

(a)

\mathbb{P}_{Z_{1:n}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\Big[\big|\delta_{\tau_{n}}(Z_{1:n})\big|\geq c\Big]\to 0
for all
c>0
.

(b)

\frac{(1-\tau_{n})^{2}}{\tau_{n}n}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}\Big[||\tau_{n}\psi_{\tau_{n}}(X,-1,1)||_{2}^{4}\Big]+\frac{\tau_{n}^{2}}{(1-\tau_{n})n}\sum\limits_{y=0}^{m}\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[||(1-\tau_{n})\psi_{\tau_{n}}(X,y,0)||_{2}^{4}\Big]\to 0
.

Regularity and asymptotic linearity are important concepts in semiparametric statistics. Intuitively, a
regular
estimator is one that is not systematically biased towards certain values of the estimand. For example, quantifiers that are set equal to or shrunk towards a specific estimand value, or are superefficient for particular values (cf. Hodge’s estimator) are all non-regular. The idea is that, by having an a priori preference towards certain estimand values, these quantifiers are making use of information contained
outside
of the semiparametric model, and thus should be excluded from consideration
[
Newey, 1990
]
. Further, a quantifier that is
asymptotically linear
with influence function
\psi_{\tau}
and second order error
\delta_{\tau}
is one whose normalized estimation error can be written as

\sqrt{n}(\widetilde{\pi}_{n}-\pi^{\ast})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{\tau}(Z_{i})+\delta_{\tau}(Z_{1:n}),

where
\psi_{\tau},\delta_{\tau}
are
\mathbb{R}^{m}
-valued functions,
\psi_{\tau}(Z_{i})
is mean zero with a finite and nonsingular covariance matrix, and
\delta_{\tau}(Z_{1:n})\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.09723ptp\hskip 4.09723pt}0
. Quantifiers of this form are unbiased to the first order, and more specifically, have an asymptotic error distribution of
\mathcal{N}\big(0,\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau}}[\psi_{\tau}]\big)
. This leads to a natural way of comparing asymptotically linear quantifiers: if
\psi_{\tau,1}
and
\psi_{\tau,2}
are influence functions for two different quantifiers and
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau}}[\psi_{\tau,1}]\preceq\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau}}[\psi_{\tau,2}]
, then the quantifier with
\psi_{\tau,1}
as its influence function is more desirable, since its first order error is stochastically smaller. Traditionally, the influence function with the smallest variance matrix is called the
efficient influence function
. The variance matrix of the efficient influence function is called the
semiparametric efficiency bound
, and a quantifier which is asymptotically linear in the efficient influence function is called
semiparametric efficient
, because its first order variance achieves the semiparametric efficiency bound. For a rigorous treatment of regularity and asymptotic linearity, we refer the reader to
Newey, 1990
and
Bickel et al., 1993
.

While condition (
C1
) places requirements on the asymptotic behavior of quantifiers for
fixed

\tau\in(0,1)
, we would also like to analyze this behavior in settings where one of the train or test sample sizes grows far faster than the other, i.e., when
\tau_{n}
approaches either
0
or
1
. Since the influence function and the second order error may vary for different
\tau
, analyzing the foregoing behavior requires placing restrictions on
\{(\psi_{\tau},\delta_{\tau})\mid\tau\in(0,1)\}
. This is where conditions (
C2a
) and (
C2b
) come in. By requiring that
\big|\delta_{\tau_{n}}(Z_{1:n})\big|\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.09723ptp\hskip 4.09723pt}0
even as
\tau_{n}
changes, condition (
C2a
) ensures that the asymptotic variance of
\widehat{\pi}_{n}
is completely determined by
\psi_{\tau_{n}}
. On top of that, condition (
C2b
) is a simple moment condition that controls the size of the influence function. It is also easy to satisfy: for example, the condition holds whenever the norm of the influence function is uniformly bounded.

3.7
Intermediate Results

Having fully described our semiparametric model and the assumptions we make about its parameter space, we now walk through some of the intermediate results that were key to establishing SELSE’s semiparametric efficiency. Towards that end, we start by taking a deeper dive into the concept of a semiparametric efficiency bound, which we had introduced in subsection (
3.5.2
). This will be done first for the Fixed
\tau
-IID Regime, and generalized later on to the
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime.

3.7.1
Fixed
\tau
-IID Regime

As mentioned previously, an intuitive way to think about a semiparametric efficiency bound is that it is the largest possible CRLB for a smooth parametric submodel, i.e., it is the CRLB for the least favorable parametric submodel. However, for any given smooth parametric submodel, what
exactly
is the form of the CRLB? Answering this question will be quite helpful for our purposes, so we turn to that now.

Recall that a parametric submodel differs from a semiparametric model in that the infinite dimensional nuisance parameter in the semiparametric model is now parameterized by a finite dimensional vector. That is, in the context of the Fixed
\tau
-IID Regime, a parametric submodel
\mathcal{M}^{\text{sub},\tau}\subset\mathcal{M}^{\text{semi},\tau}
assumes that
\mathbf{p}
belongs to a parametric family. Accordingly, each density vector
\mathbf{q}
in this family can be parameterized by some
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
, where
\mathcal{R}
is some open set of finite dimensional vectors. The full parameter space for general
(\pi,\rho)
is then
\Delta\times\mathcal{R}
, and the submodel is given by
\mathcal{M}^{\text{sub},\tau}=\{\mathcal{J}^{\pi,\rho,\tau}\mid(\pi,\rho)\in\Delta\times\mathcal{R}\}
. For example, if the covariates were all one dimensional and we were confident that the component densities were gaussian pdfs with unit variance, then
\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
and each
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
corresponds to a different vector of
m+1
means, with one mean for each of the
m+1
classes.

Now, the fact that the parameter space is finite dimensional for
\mathcal{M}^{\text{sub},\tau}
means that the score function for the joint vector
(\pi,\rho)
is easy to compute: it is simply
S_{\pi,\rho}(z):=(S_{\pi}(z),S_{\rho}(z))
, where

\displaystyle S_{\pi}(z)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\delta}{\delta{\pi}}\log\mathcal{J}^{\pi,\rho^{\ast},\tau}(z)

\displaystyle=ds_{\pi}(x)

(22)

and

S_{\rho}(z):=\frac{\delta}{\delta{\rho}}\log\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\rho,\tau}(z),

where
\rho^{\ast}
is the parameter vector that the parametric submodel identifies with
\mathbf{p}
. Note that our use of the word
“score”
here is in line with the semiparametric literature, but it should not be confused with the score function
s_{\pi}(x)
that we have typically referred to throughout this thesis, although they are related by line (
22
).
S_{\pi}
is the score function for the parameter of interest, and
S_{\rho}
is the score function for the nuisance parameter (aka the
nuisance score function
). Intuitively,
S_{\pi}
captures the sensitivity of the density
\mathcal{J}^{\pi,\rho^{\ast},\tau}
to changes in
\pi
, or equivalently, the degree of dependence of
\mathcal{J}^{\pi,\rho^{\ast},\tau}
on
\pi
, when the component densities are fixed at the truth
\mathbf{p}
. Likewise,
S_{\rho}
captures the degree of dependence of
\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\rho,\tau}
on
\rho
, when the parameter of interest is fixed at the truth
\pi^{\ast}
. This perspective encourages an interesting interpretation of the magnitude for both types of score functions. On the one hand, one can think of the magnitude of
S_{\pi^{\ast}}(Z)
as a measure of the amount of information contained in a random variable
Z\sim\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\rho^{\ast},\tau}
about
\pi^{\ast}
when
\rho^{\ast}
is already known. On the other hand, one can think of the magnitude of
S_{\rho^{\ast}}(Z)
as a measure of the amount of information contained in
Z\sim\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\rho^{\ast},\tau}
about
\rho^{\ast}
when
\pi^{\ast}
is already known.

These information-centric interpretations of
S_{\pi^{\ast}}
and
S_{\rho^{\ast}}
are vital for understanding the CRLB in a parametric submodel. As argued in
Newey, 1990
, one way to interpret the CRLB for learning
\pi^{\ast}
(without necessarily learning
\mathbf{p}
) is that it is
the inverse of the variance matrix of the residual we obtain from projecting
S_{\pi^{\ast}}(Z)
onto
S_{\rho^{\ast}}(Z)
, where in this case, the projection is in terms of mean-square distance, which is generally defined as
\mathbb{E}||V_{1}-V_{2}||_{2}^{2}
for any two random vectors
V_{1},V_{2}
. Notationally, this means that for some conformable matrix
B
, the aforementioned CRLB is equal to

\bigg(\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau}}\Big[\big(S_{\pi^{\ast}}-BS_{\rho^{\ast}}\big)\big(S_{\pi^{\ast}}-BS_{\rho^{\ast}}\big)^{T}\Big]\bigg)^{-1}

(23)

In other words, from a semiparametric perspective, the average information content contained in a single sample
Z
about
\pi^{\ast}
absent a priori knowledge about
\mathbf{p}
is equal to the variance (i.e., average magnitude) of what is left over from
S_{\pi}(Z)

after we remove
the part of
S_{\pi}(Z)
that lies in the same direction as
S_{\rho^{\ast}}(Z)
. Based on our previous interpretations of
S_{\pi}
and
S_{\rho}
, this
“removed”
part of
S_{\pi}(Z)
can be thought of in terms of
\log\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\rho,\tau}
’s sensitivity to changes in
\pi^{\ast}
: that is, the
“removed”
part is the part of that sensitivity which had been due to freezing
\rho
at
\rho^{\ast}
, as opposed to any other member of
\mathcal{R}
.

This interpretation of a CRLB is useful for understanding how we were able to identify the semiparametric efficiency bound in the Fixed
\tau
-IID Regime. As mentioned earlier, the semiparametric bound can be thought of as the supremum of CRLBs for estimating
\pi^{\ast}
in the presence of unknown
\mathbf{p}
, where the supremum indexes over all possible smooth parametric submodels. While intuitive as a definition, this does not lend itself to easy evaluation– indeed, how would one even find that supremum? The trick lies in the CRLB interpretation we established in the previous paragraph. First, note that
S_{\pi^{\ast}}
is the same for all smooth parametric submodels in question; this is because in the definition of
S_{\pi^{\ast}}
, the finite dimensional nuisance parameter
\rho
is always frozen at
\rho=\rho^{\ast}
, and the underlying truth
\mathbf{p}
is the same for all submodels. Thus, to make the aforementioned residual as small as possible, we need to choose a parametric family for
\mathbf{p}
for which the distance between
S_{\pi^{\ast}}(Z)
and the resulting linear span of the nuisance score is as small as possible. However, rather than looking over all possible parametric families separately and computing the residual each time so as to find the one that is the smallest stochastically, one should note that the minimum of those individual residuals is the same as the residual obtained from the projection of
S_{\pi^{\ast}}(Z)
onto the
union
of all the aforementioned nuisance scores. Indeed, this is a simple consequence of the fact that a projection is always based on the shortest distance between
S_{\pi^{\ast}}(Z)
and the set in question.

Of course, in semiparametric statistics, this
“union”
is replaced by a more formal notion of taking the mean square closure of all
m
-dimensional linear combinations of nuisance score functions, but the fundamental idea is the same. This mean square closure is often called the
nuisance tangent set
, and once one is able to identify the residual obtained from projecting
S_{\pi^{\ast}}
onto this set, finding the semiparametric efficiency bound becomes a routine calculation that mimics what is done in display (
23
) for the CRLB of smooth parametric submodels. Indeed, as
Newey, 1990
states in their Theorem 3.2, the semiparametric bound is precisely the inverse of the variance matrix of the aforementioned residual!

At last, having motivated the steps necessary to identify the semiparametric efficiency bound for the fixed
\tau
-IID Regime, we can finally walk through those exact steps as it applies to quantification. As mentioned in the previous paragraph, there are two steps. The first step consists of identifying the residual of the projection of
S_{\pi^{\ast}}(Z)
onto the nuisance tangent set. This is concisely summarized via the following lemma.

Lemma 3.6

(
Projection of
S_{\pi^{\ast}}
onto
\mathcal{T}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
denote the semiparametric nuisance tangent set defined in the Appendix. Under Assumptions (
B1
), (
B2
) and (
B3
), we have that

ds_{\pi^{\ast}}-\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau)^{-1}\psi_{\tau}^{\textup{eff}}\in\mathcal{T}

and

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau}}\Big[t^{\prime}\big(S_{\pi^{\ast}}-\big(Ds_{\pi^{\ast}}-\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau)^{-1}\psi_{\tau}^{\textup{eff}}\big)\big)\Big]=0\qquad\forall\ t\in\mathcal{T},

where

\displaystyle\psi_{\tau}^{\textup{eff}}(z)

\displaystyle:=\frac{d}{\tau}\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast}(\tau))^{-1}\big(s_{\gamma^{\ast}(\tau)}(x)-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}(\tau)}]\big)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1-d}{1-\tau}\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast}(\tau))^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\mathbbold{1}{\{y=j\}}\frac{\pi_{j}^{\ast}}{\pi_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\big(s_{\gamma^{\ast}(\tau)}(x)-\mathbb{E}_{j}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}(\tau)}]\big)

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau):=\bigg[\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{1}{1-\tau}\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{k}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{k}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\bigg]\Big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast}(\tau))^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast}(\tau);\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Big)+\frac{1}{\tau}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}.

Lemma (
3.6
) tells us that the projection of
S_{\pi^{\ast}}
onto the nuisance tangent set
\mathcal{T}
is given by
Ds_{\pi^{\ast}}(X)-\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau)^{-1}\psi_{\tau}^{\textup{eff}}(Z)
, and that the residual from this projection is equal to
\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau)^{-1}\psi_{\tau}^{\textup{eff}}(Z)
. The proof for this lemma can be found in the Appendix. Next, having identified the residual, the second step is to compute the inverse of the variance of
\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau)^{-1}\psi_{\tau}^{\textup{eff}}(Z)
when
Z\sim\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau}
, as the resulting matrix will be equal to the semiparametric efficiency bound. This is a simple but algebraically intense calculation, the results of which are summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem 3.7

(
Semiparametric Efficiency Bound in Fixed
\tau
-IID Regime
)
.

Under Assumptions (
B1
), (
B2
) and (
B3
), the efficient influence function is
\psi^{\textup{eff}}_{\tau}
, the semiparametric efficiency bound is
\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau)
, and
\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau)=\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau}}[\psi^{\textup{eff}}_{\tau}]
.

Theorem (
3.7
) identifies
\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau)
as the semiparametric efficiency bound for quantifiers that are regular and asymptotically linear within the Fixed
\tau
-IID Regime. This result is extremely encouraging, because upon inspection,
\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau)
is the Fixed
\tau
-IID Regime’s analogue of the variance matrix identified for SELSE in the Fixed Sequence Regime within subsection (
3.1
) in Theorem (
3.4
). Furthermore, in establishing this result, we were also able to identify
\psi_{\tau}^{\textup{eff}}
as the efficient influence function, which we had first mentioned in subsection (
3.6
). Also, recall from that same subsection that the variance of the efficient influence function should be equal to the semiparametric efficiency bound. As a sanity check, Theorem (
3.7
) verifies that this is indeed the case.

3.7.2
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime

The parallel between the asymptotic variance matrix identified for SELSE in the Fixed Sequence Regime and the semiparametric efficiency bound identified in the Fixed
\tau
-IID Regime was exciting, because it suggested that the two variance matrices would align upon translating our results into the final
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime.

Encouraged by this, we first focused on the translation for the asymptotic results of SELSE. While algebraically tedious, the strategy was quite straightforward. Essentially, the main insight comes from recognizing that the stochastic behavior of SELSE in the Fixed Sequence Regime can be thought of as its behavior in the
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime
when we condition
on the event that
N^{\textup{\text{tr}}}=(1-\tau_{n})n
and
N^{\textup{\text{te}}}=\tau_{n}n
. Since
N^{\textup{\text{tr}}}/n
and
N^{\textup{\text{te}}}/n
will concentrate around
1-\tau_{n}
and
\tau_{n}
respectively, it follows that the behavior of SELSE in the Fixed Sequence Regime based on setting
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}=(1-\tau_{n})n
and
n^{\textup{\text{te}}}=\tau_{n}n
is representative of its behavior in the
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime. Hence, combining this intuition with Theorem (
3.4
) from the Fixed Sequence Regime, we were well positioned to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 3.8

(
First and Second Order Error of SELSE in
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime
)
.

Under Assumptions (
B1
), (
B2
), (
4
), (
5
), (
6
), (
7
), (
8
) and (
9
), the SELSE quantifier
\widehat{\pi}_{n}
satisfies

\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\pi}_{n}-\pi^{\ast})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{\tau_{n}}^{\textup{eff}}(Z_{i})+\delta_{\tau_{n}}

where
\psi_{\tau_{n}}^{\textup{eff}}:\mathcal{Z}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
is as defined in Lemma (
3.6
) and satisfies

\frac{(1-{\tau}_{n})^{2}}{{\tau}_{n}n}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}\Big[||{\tau}_{n}\psi^{\textup{eff}}_{{\tau}_{n}}(X,-1,1)||_{2}^{4}\Big]+\frac{{\tau}_{n}^{2}}{(1-{\tau}_{n})n}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[||(1-{\tau}_{n})\psi^{\textup{eff}}_{{\tau}_{n}}(X,y,0)||_{2}^{4}\Big]\to 0,

and
\delta_{\tau_{n}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
satisfies

\displaystyle||\delta_{\tau_{n}}||_{2}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbb{P}}\Bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})}}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bar{n}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}+\max_{y}\lambda_{y}\Omega(s_{\bar{\gamma},y})^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\min_{y}\lambda_{y}}}e^{-C\bar{n}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{\mathbb{P}}\Bigg(\sqrt{\max_{y}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Big[\bar{\mathcal{U}}_{y}(\lambda_{y})\Big]+\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Big[||\widehat{\gamma}-\bar{\gamma}||_{2}^{2}\Big]}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{\mathbb{P}}\Bigg(\sqrt{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})n}e^{-C(\min_{y}\lambda_{y})^{2}\bar{n}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}+\sqrt{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})n}\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Big[\big|\big|\widehat{\gamma}-\bar{\gamma}\big|\big|_{2}\geq C\Big]\Bigg),

where
\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
,
\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\textup{\text{II}}}
,
\bar{\gamma}
and
\bar{\mathcal{U}}_{y}(\lambda_{y})
are as defined in subsection (
3.5.3
) and correspond to some sequence
(\bar{n}^{\textup{\text{te}}},\bar{n}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr}}},\dots,\bar{n}_{m}^{\textup{\text{tr}}})
that satisfies
\big|\bar{n}^{\textup{\text{te}}}/n-\tau_{n}\big|,\big|\bar{n}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}/n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}-(1-\tau_{n})\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\big|\to 0
where
\bar{n}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}:=n-\bar{n}^{\textup{\text{te}}}
.

The proof for Lemma (
3.8
) is in the Appendix. Lemma (
3.8
) establishes several facts about SELSE. First, it shows that SELSE is asymptotically linear in the efficient influence function under the
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime. Since the Fixed
\tau
-IID Regime is a special case of the
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime, this means that SELSE satisfies property (
C1
), modulo the regularity condition, which we prove SELSE satisfies in Lemma (
6.25
) in the Appendix. Second, Lemma (
3.8
) establishes that the second order
\delta_{\tau_{n}}
satisfies property (
C2a
) and that its efficient influence function
\psi_{\tau_{n}}^{\textup{eff}}
satisfies property (
C2b
). Thus, overall, Lemma (
3.8
) verifies all the conditions needed to show that SELSE is a member of
\mathcal{E}
. The fact that the efficient influence function appears again in SELSE’s first order error but now in the context of the
\tau_{n}-IID
Regime is also exciting, as it means that we have only a single step remains for establishing SELSE’s semiparametric efficiency: we must now translate the semiparametric efficiency bound from the Fixed
\tau
-IID Regime to the
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime. The culmination of that endeavor, along with all of our results so far, is presented in Theorem (
3.9
) in the next subsection.

3.8
Main Result

We now present our main theoretical result on SELSE’s performance and state the semiparametric efficiency bound for quantification tasks, both in the context of the
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime.

Theorem 3.9

(
Semiparametric Efficiency in
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime
)
.

Let
\tau_{n}
denote any sequence satisfying Assumptions (
8
) and (
9
), and let
\widetilde{\pi}_{n}
denote any quantifier and
\psi_{\tau_{n}}
its influence function. Under the Assumptions in subsections (
3.5.1
), (
3.5.2
), (
3.5.3
) and (
3.5.4
), if
\widetilde{\pi}_{n}\in\mathcal{E}
, then there exists a corresponding random vector
G_{n}
such that
G_{n}\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.09723ptd\hskip 4.09723pt}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{m\times m})
and

\sqrt{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})n}(\widetilde{\pi}_{n}-\pi^{\ast})=\Big(\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}[\psi_{\tau_{n}}]\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}G_{n}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),

where
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}[\psi_{\tau_{n}}]\succeq\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau_{n})
for each
n
, and the matrix
\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau_{n})
is given by

\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau_{n}):=\bigg[\frac{1}{\tau_{n}}+\frac{1}{1-\tau_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{k}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{k}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\bigg]\Big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast}(\tau_{n}))^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast}(\tau_{n});\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Big)+\frac{1}{\tau_{n}}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1},

where
\gamma^{\ast}(\tau_{n}):=(\gamma_{1}^{\ast}(\tau_{n}),\dots,\gamma_{m}^{\ast}(\tau_{n}))
and

\gamma_{y}^{\ast}(\tau_{n}):=\frac{\frac{\pi_{y}^{\ast}}{\tau_{n}}+\frac{1}{1-\tau_{n}}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}{\frac{1}{\tau_{n}}+\frac{1}{1-\tau_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{k}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{k}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}\qquad\forall\ y\in\mathcal{Y}.

Furthermore, our SELSE quantifier
\widehat{\pi}_{n}
has an influence function
\psi_{\tau_{n}}^{\textup{eff}}
which satisfies

\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}[\psi_{\tau_{n}}^{\textup{eff}}]=\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau_{n})

for each
n
, and
\widehat{\pi}_{n}\in\mathcal{E}
.

In a nutshell, Theorem (
3.9
) states that SELSE is a member of
\mathcal{E}
, and that among all members of
\mathcal{E}
, SELSE has the smallest possible asymptotic variance matrix for its normalized error. This variance matrix is equal to
\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})\mathbf{V}^{\text{eff}}(\tau_{n})
, and it is the acclaimed semiparametric efficiency bound for the
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime. The main logic underlying this result is that the asymptotic variance of a quantifier’s error is simply the variance (scaled by
\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})
) of its own influence function, and the variance of our quantifier’s influence function can be shown to be always less than or equal to the variance of any other member’s. This result is largely due to SELSE’s usage of
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
as a proxy for
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
in its ACC/PACC approach: as indicated by the dependence of
\mathbf{V}^{\text{eff}}(\tau_{n})
on
\tau_{n}
, the
“optimal”
performance for estimating
\pi^{\ast}
varies greatly with
\tau_{n}
, and the dependence of
\gamma^{\ast}
on the number of train and test samples allows our quantifier to account for this fact when estimating
\pi^{\ast}
.

The fact that SELSE has the smallest possible eigenvalues for its asymptotic variance matrix is attractive from two different points of view. First, it follows that our quantifier is optimal for learning
\pi^{\ast}
under
L_{2}
loss: for any other quantifier
\widetilde{\pi}\in\mathcal{E}
, the asymptotic variance of
\sqrt{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})n}||\widehat{\pi}_{n}-\pi^{\ast}||_{2}
will always be less than or equal to that of
\sqrt{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})n}||\widetilde{\pi}_{n}-\pi^{\ast}||_{2}
. Second, our quantifier is optimal for estimating smooth functions of
\pi^{\ast}
. That is, for any integer
l\geq 1
and smooth function
\ell:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{l}
, the asymptotic variance matrix of
\sqrt{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})n}(\ell(\widehat{\pi}_{n})-\ell(\pi^{\ast}))
will always be less than or equal to that of
\sqrt{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})n}(\ell(\widetilde{\pi}_{n})-\ell(\pi^{\ast}))
. This can be easily deduced via a first order Taylor expansion of both normalized errors. From a practical point of view, this means that our quantifier is optimal for learning at least three different, interesting scalar quantities, each of which correspond to setting
l=1
and
\ell(\beta)=c^{\prime}\beta+d
for an appropriate choice of
c\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
d\in\mathbb{R}
: the probability of
Y^{\textup{\text{te}}}
belonging to a specific class (or group of classes), the difference in probability of belonging to one class (or group) versus another class (or group), and the expected value of a function of
Y^{\textup{\text{te}}}
.

Lastly, in addition to verifying the optimality of our quantifier, the semiparametric efficiency bound also provides us a deeper understanding of the general problem of quantification. In particular, it reveals that quantification problems are actually a
“blend”
of two other statistical estimation tasks:

(a)

estimating
\pi^{\ast}
when
\mathbf{p}
is known but
p_{\pi^{\ast}}
is unknown, and we have IID samples from
p_{\pi^{\ast}}
, and

(b)

estimating
\pi^{\ast}
when
p_{\pi^{\ast}}
is known but
\mathbf{p}
is unknown, and we have IID samples from each density in
\mathbf{p}
.

The degree to which quantification is similar to problem (
a
) versus (
b
) depends on
\tau_{n}
. If
\tau_{n}\to 0
, then quantification becomes identical to problem (
a
), and if
\tau_{n}\to 1
, then quantification becomes identical to problem (
b
). To see why, start by considering the former situation when
\tau_{n}\to 0
. When this happens, there are far more train samples than test samples, and so for large
n
, we essentially have perfect knowledge of
\mathbf{p}
and must estimate
\pi^{\ast}
using samples from the unknown
p_{\pi^{\ast}}
. This is exactly the definition of problem (
a
). Furthermore, the equivalence between quantification and problem (a) when
\tau_{n}\to 0
also agrees with the limiting behavior of the semiparametric efficiency bound. Indeed, since
\gamma^{\ast}(0)=\pi^{\ast}
, we have that

\displaystyle\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau_{n})

\displaystyle=\bigg[(1-\tau_{n})+\tau_{n}\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{k}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{k}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\bigg]\Big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast}(\tau_{n}))^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast}(\tau_{n});\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+(1-\tau_{n})\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.09723pt\tau_{n}\to 0\hskip 4.09723pt}\Big(\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast})^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Big)+\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast})^{-1}.

\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast})^{-1}
is the CRLB for estimating
\pi^{\ast}
in problem (
a
), and so the fact that
\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau_{n})\to\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast})^{-1}
as
\tau_{n}\to 0
means that the difficulty of learning
\pi^{\ast}
in quantification becomes equal to the difficulty in problem (
a
), further verifying the equivalence between the two problems.

The opposite happens when
\tau_{n}\to 1
: in this situation, there are far more test samples than train samples, and so for large
n
, we essentially have perfect knowledge of
p_{\pi^{\ast}}
and must estimate
\pi^{\ast}
using the samples obtained from each density in
\mathbf{p}
. This is exactly the definition of problem (
b
). Now, similar to before, one can also show that the semiparametric efficiency bound for quantification converges to the bound for problem (
b
) when
\tau_{n}\to 1
, but proving the bound for problem (
b
) is tedious since the problem is still semiparametric. Therefore, as a compromise, we instead point out the equivalence for a special
parametric
case of problem (
b
): namely, when
m=1
and both
p_{\pi^{\ast}}
and
p_{0}
are known but
p_{1}
is unknown, and we obtain IID samples from
p_{1}
. This special case of problem (
b
) corresponds to a quantification task where
\tau_{n}\to 1
and
\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\to 0
, because when those limits hold, we will have very few train samples from class
1
, but an abundance of train samples from class
0
, as well as many test samples. As such, when
n
is large, we will have near perfect knowledge of
p_{\pi^{\ast}}
and
p_{0}
, and must estimate
\pi^{\ast}
using the samples from
p_{1}
. This equivalence is corroborated by the limiting behavior of the semiparametric efficiency bound, since

\displaystyle\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}(1-\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}})\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau_{n})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.09723pt(\tau_{n},\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}})\to(1,0)\hskip 4.09723pt}(\pi^{\ast})^{2}\mathcal{I}(1)^{-1},

where
(\pi^{\ast})^{2}\mathcal{I}(1)^{-1}
is the CRLB for estimating
\pi^{\ast}
in the special parametric version of problem
(b)
.

3.9
Proof of Main Result

Proof of Theorem (
3.9
).
.

First, we verify that
\widehat{\pi}_{n}\in\mathcal{E}
. Note that, under Assumptions (
B1
), (
B2
), (
B3
), (
4
), (
5
), (
6
) and (
7
), we have by Lemma (
6.25
) that, for each fixed
\tau\in(0,1)
, the estimator
\widehat{\pi}_{n}
is
\tau
-RAL with influence function
\psi_{\tau}^{\textup{eff}}
and second order error
\delta_{\tau}
. Thus, property (
C1
) holds for
\widehat{\pi}_{n}
. Furthermore, under Assumptions (
B1
), (
B2
), (
4
), (
5
), (
6
) and (
7
), for any sequence
\tau_{n}
that satisfies Assumptions (
8
) and (
9
), it follows from Lemma (
3.8
) that properties (
C2a
) and (
C2b
) also hold. Thus,
\widehat{\pi}_{n}\in\mathcal{E}
.

Second, we verify the theorem’s asymptotic statements concerning an arbitrary quantifier
\widetilde{\pi}_{n}\in\mathcal{E}
. Since
\widetilde{\pi}_{n}
is
\tau
-RAL for all
\tau\in(0,1)
by virtue of property (
C1
), it follows that for any
n
, the equation

\sqrt{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})n}(\widetilde{\pi}_{n}-\pi^{\ast})=\sqrt{\frac{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})}{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z_{i})+\sqrt{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})}\delta_{\tau_{n}}(Z_{1:n})

holds for every dataset
Z_{1:n}
. Further, due to Assumptions (
8
) and (
9
), we have by property (
C2a
) that
\delta_{\tau_{n}}(Z_{1:n})\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.09723ptp\hskip 4.09723pt}0
. So, because
\sqrt{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})}
is bounded, it follows that

\implies\sqrt{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})n}(\widetilde{\pi}_{n}-\pi^{\ast})=\sqrt{\frac{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})}{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z_{i})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

Additionally, since
\widetilde{\pi}_{n}
is
\tau
-RAL for all
\tau\in(0,1)
, the matrix
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\big[\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)\big]
is nonsingular for all
n
. Thus, the first order term above may be written as:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})}{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z_{i})

\displaystyle=\Big(\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\big[\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)\big]\Big)^{\tfrac{1}{2}}\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overbrace{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\big[\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)\big]^{-\tfrac{1}{2}}\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z_{i})}^{=:W_{n,i}}}_{=:G_{n}}.

We will now prove that
G_{n}\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{n,i}\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.09723ptp\hskip 4.09723pt}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{m})
. Towards that end, observe that
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}[W_{n,i}]=0
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\big[W_{n,i}W_{n,i}^{\prime}\big]=I_{m}
. Also, for any
c>0
, we have that:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\Big[||W_{n,i}||_{2}^{2}\mathbbold{1}{\big\{||W_{n,i}||_{2}>c\big\}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\Big[||W_{n,i}||_{2}^{2}\mathbbold{1}{\big\{||W_{n,i}||_{2}^{2}>c^{2}\big\}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\Big[\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z_{i})^{\prime}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\big[\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)\big]^{-1}\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z_{i})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\times\mathbbold{1}{\big\{\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z_{i})^{\prime}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\big[\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)\big]^{-1}\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z_{i})>nc^{2}\big\}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\Big[\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)^{\prime}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\big[\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)\big]^{-1}\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)\mathbbold{1}{\big\{\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)^{\prime}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\big[\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)\big]^{-1}\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)>nc^{2}\big\}}\Big].

Also note that:

\displaystyle\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)^{\prime}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\big[\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)\big]^{-1}\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)

\displaystyle\leq||\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)||_{2}^{2}\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\big[\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)\big]^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{||\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)||_{2}^{2}}{\lambda_{\textup{min}}\big(\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\big[\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)\big]\big)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{||\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)||_{2}^{2}}{\lambda_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau_{n})\big)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})||\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)||_{2}^{2}}{(\xi)^{2}\nu\big(\frac{L}{m+1}\big)^{2}}.

The third line is due to the fact that
\widetilde{\pi}_{n}
is
\tau
-RAL for each fixed
\tau\in(0,1)
, which under Assumptions (
B1
), (
B2
) and (
B3
), implies that
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\big[\psi_{\tau_{n}}^{\textup{eff}}\big]=\mathbf{V}^{\textup{eff}}(\tau_{n})\preceq\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\big[\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)\big]
for each
n
by virtue of Theorem (
3.7
). The fourth line is due to Lemma (
6.19
) and Assumption (
B1
). For notational convenience, temporarily define
b:=(\xi)^{2}\nu\big(\frac{L}{m+1}\big)^{2}
. Then, it follows that:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\Big[||W_{n,i}||_{2}^{2}\mathbbold{1}{\big\{||W_{n,i}||_{2}>c\big\}}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})}{b}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\Big[||\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)||_{2}^{2}\mathbbold{1}{\big\{||\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)||_{2}^{2}>nc^{2}\tfrac{b}{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})}\big\}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})}{b}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\Bigg[\frac{||\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)||_{2}^{4}}{||\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)||_{2}^{2}}\mathbbold{1}{\big\{||\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)||_{2}^{2}>nc^{2}\tfrac{b}{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})}\big\}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\tau_{n}^{2}(1-\tau_{n})^{2}}{nc^{2}b^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}}\Big[||\psi_{\tau_{n}}(Z)||_{2}^{4}\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau_{n}^{2}(1-\tau_{n})^{2}}{nc^{2}b^{2}}\Bigg\{\tau_{n}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}\Big[||\psi_{\tau_{n}}(X,-1,1)||_{2}^{4}\Big]+(1-\tau_{n})\sum_{y=0}^{m}\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[||\psi_{\tau_{n}}(X,y,0)||_{2}^{4}\Big]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c^{2}b^{2}}\Bigg\{\frac{\tau_{n}^{3}(1-\tau_{n})^{2}}{n}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}\Big[||\psi_{\tau_{n}}(X,-1,1)||_{2}^{4}\Big]+\frac{\tau_{n}^{2}(1-\tau_{n})^{3}}{n}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[||\psi_{\tau_{n}}(X,y,0)||_{2}^{4}\Big]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c^{2}b^{2}}\Bigg\{\frac{(1-\tau_{n})^{2}}{\tau_{n}n}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}\Big[||\tau_{n}\psi_{\tau_{n}}(X,-1,1)||_{2}^{4}\Big]+\frac{\tau_{n}^{2}}{(1-\tau_{n})n}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[||(1-\tau_{n})\psi_{\tau_{n}}(X,y,0)||_{2}^{4}\Big]\Bigg\},

where the last line goes to zero as
n\to\infty
by virtue of property (
C2b
). Therefore, by Theorem 11.1.6 in
Athreya and Lahiri, 2006
, we may conclude that
G_{n}\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.09723ptd\hskip 4.09723pt}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{m})
.
∎

4
Numerical Experiments

We now study SELSE’s empirical performance. This is an important supplement to our theoretical analysis, because while we have established the optimality of SELSE’s first order error term, we have not discussed the optimality of its higher order ones, and in practice, a quantifier’s total error always depends on both. The experiments in this section therefore aim to provide a more complete, practical picture of SELSE and its competitors.

4.1
Experimental Set-Up

We performed experiments on synthetic and real world data. The synthetic data involved two classes, with the covariates from each class sampled from different 2D gaussian distributions, each with identity covariance. For class
0
, the associated mean vector was
(-1,-1)^{T}
, and for class
1
, the associated mean vector was
(1,1)^{T}
