. In terms of real world data, we considered a blood transfusion dataset. This dataset was used as a benchmark in
Moreo et al., 2021
’s systematic comparison of different quantification methods, which includes all the quantifiers considered here except for SELSE.

The experimental set-up for the synthetic and real world datasets were similar. For a given combination of
n,\tau,\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
and
\pi^{\ast}
, we generated
n^{\textup{\text{te}}}=\tau n
test samples and
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}=(1-\tau)n
train samples. For the real world data, the train data for each class
y
was generated by sampling with replacement from the points in the original dataset that belonged to class
y
. The test data was generated in a similar fashion, except for each test sample, the class that we took the sample from was determined randomly according to
\text{Cat}(\pi^{\ast})
. Then, once the train and test data were formed, we computed six different quantifiers
5
5

5

The performance of SELSE and MLLS was measured in the R programming language via our own in-house implementations of those quantifiers, whereas the measurements for ACC, PACC, HDy and EMQ were done via their Python implementations in the QuaPy package
[
Moreo et al., 2021
]
.
: ACC, PACC, HDy, EMQ, MLLS and SELSE, all of which were described in the Methodology section. For each quantifier
\widetilde{\pi}
, the normalized error
\sqrt{\tau_{n}(1-\tau_{n})n}||\widetilde{\pi}-\pi^{\ast}||_{2}
was recorded. This experiment was repeated 100 times, and for each quantifier, the average of its normalized error across those experiments was calculated.

This average served as our main metric for the performance of a quantifier under a given combination of
n,\tau,\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
and
\pi^{\ast}
. The combinations we considered were as follows. For both datasets, we set
n=500
and
\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}=\frac{1}{2}
, and computed our metric over all combinations of
\tau\in\{0.2,0.5,0.8\}
and
\pi^{\ast}\in\{0.05,0.2,0.35,0.5,0.65,0.80,0.95\}
. We froze
\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
at
\frac{1}{2}
to limit the amount of simulations that needed to be done.

4.2
Quantifier Configuration Details

The configuration details of the six quantifiers are as follows. Recall that all the quantifiers in question make use of some form of classifier. For ACC, this is a hard classifier, and for PACC, HDy, EMQ and MLLS, this is a soft classifier. SELSE also uses a soft classifier indirectly, since in Step 2, a preliminary quantifier for
\pi^{\ast}
is utilized to form the plug-in estimator
\widehat{\gamma}
, and in our experiments, MLLS served as that preliminary quantifier. For all six quantifiers and both datasets, the classifier in question was a SVM with either a radial or polynomial kernel and
\ell_{2}
penalty (the exact kernel and penalization parameters were selected via 3-fold cross validation) Finally, SELSE has several other unique parameters in Step 3 that relate to how the score function is estimated, which need to be chosen. Specifically, in our experiments, we chose each space
\mathcal{H}_{y}
to be a RKHS, with a gaussian reproducing kernel. The kernel width and penalty parameters used to construct
f=\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
were chosen to minimize the maximum eigenvalue of the empirical counterpart to
A_{f}^{-1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\widehat{\gamma}}[f]A_{f}^{-T}
, which was computed on a separate validation set.

(a)
\tau=0.2

(b)
\tau=0.8

(c)
\tau=0.5

(d)
SELSE: True Score Correlation

Figure 1:
Simulation Results on Synthetic Gaussian Mixture Data.

(a)
\tau=0.2

(b)
\tau=0.5

(c)
\tau=0.8

Simulation Results on Transfusion Data.

4.3
Results & Discussion

We now turn to the results of our experiments. We begin by making two general observations. First, in both experiments, all quantifiers tended to preform worse for larger values of
\tau
than for smaller values of
\tau
. This means that, at least in these two types of data, a scarcity of training samples is harder to handle than a scarcity of test samples. Second, when
\tau
is small, estimating
\pi^{\ast}
when
\pi^{\ast}
is close to
0
or
1
is far easier than when
\pi^{\ast}
is close to
\frac{1}{2}
. This makes sense in light of our discussion at the end of the Theoretical Results section, and holds for all datasets (not just the ones considered here). This is because when
\tau\to 0
, the semiparametric efficiency bound approaches the CRLB for the classic parametric mixture problem. The parametric problem is easier when
\pi^{\ast}
takes on extreme values, much in the same way that estimating
p
using independent flips of a
p
-biased coin is easier when
p\approx 0
or
p\approx 1
than when
p\approx\frac{1}{2}
. However, this pattern tends to weaken as
\tau
increases.

Next, we comment on the performance of SELSE specifically. In our synthetic data experiment, SELSE outperforms the other quantifiers for nearly all combinations of
\tau
and
\pi^{\ast}
. This makes sense in light of Figure (1d), which shows that SELSE’s score function estimates tend to be highly correlated with
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
. This correlation is desirable when one estimates
\pi^{\ast}
because, as discussed in subsections (
2.5.1
) and (
2.5.2
), using
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
allows one to strike the ideal balance between one’s uncertainty about the mixture distribution (caused by the finite amount of test data) and the component densities (caused by the finite amount of train data). Correspondingly, the fact that the performance gap between SELSE and the other quantifiers grows as
\tau\to 1
suggests that the other quantifiers are unable to effectively orchestrate this balance. This especially makes sense for EMQ and MLLS, since both of those methods are based on the maximum likelihood principle. As mentioned in subsection (
2.2.2
), maximum likelihood assumes that either the
Y\mid X
or
Y\mid f(X)
conditional can be learned, which is a difficult requirement to satisfy when the training set is extremely small. Finally, the same observations made about SELSE for our synthetic data experiment also hold true for the transfusion data experiment. The only exception to this is HDy, which tends to outperform SELSE when
\tau
is small. However, the performance of HDy also degrades for extreme values of
\pi^{\ast}
as
\tau\to 1
, making HDy unreliable in those situations. In contrast, SELSE is able to avoid this pitfall: with the exception of HDy, SELSE’s error is almost uniformly smaller than the error of any other quantifier, regardless of the value of
\tau
, making it a favorable, general-purpose quantifier to turn to when one does not want to worry about the effect of
\tau
.

5
Conclusion

In summary, we proposed the SELSE quantifier as a method for inferring the response distribution in the test set under the label shift assumption. We proved that SELSE is semiparametric efficient for a broad class of quantifiers, implying that SELSE’s first order error term is optimally small. We also grounded our theoretical results of SELSE’s optimality in two empirical studies, and numerically showed the distinct advantages of SELSE over many of the existing approaches to quantification.

6
Appendix

6.1
Three Regimes

Here we provide a more in depth description of the three regimes initially presented in subsection (
3.3
), as this will aid us in proving the supporting lemmas and theorems in the Appendix. Recall that the regimes differ from each other in two key regards: the data generation mechanism, and how the true parameters behave as
n\to\infty
.

6.1.1
Fixed Sequence Regime

In the Fixed Sequence Regime, there exists a fixed sequence

\big\{(n^{\textup{\text{te}}},n^{\textup{\text{tr}}},\pi^{\ast},\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}},p_{0},p_{1},\dots,p_{m})\big\}_{n=1}^{\infty}

where the sample sizes
0\leq n^{\textup{\text{te}}},n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\to\infty
satisfy
n^{\textup{\text{te}}}+n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}=n
, and the class probabilities
\pi^{\ast},\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
and class densities
p_{0},\dots,p_{m}
are subject to various restrictions that shall be described later. The class densities are supported on some set
\mathcal{X}
, and are dominated w.r.t some measure
\mu
. The class probabilities and class densities may all depend on
n
. We assume
\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
is known, and that
\pi^{\ast}
and
p_{0},\dots,p_{n}
are unknown.

In terms of data, for each
n
, we obtain
n^{\textup{\text{te}}}
test samples from the mixture
\sum_{y=0}^{m}\pi_{y}^{\ast}p_{y}
, as well as
\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
train samples from
p_{y}
for each class
y\in\mathcal{Y}:=\{0,1,\dots,m\}
. The
n
samples are mutually independent. For convenience, we let
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{te}}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
denote the collection of test and train samples, respectively. Our goal is to use
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{te}}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
to create an estimator
\widehat{\pi}
that “reacts” to the evolution in the sequence of parameters/sample sizes as
n\to\infty
so as to ensure
\widehat{\pi}-\pi^{\ast}
becomes small.

6.1.2
Fixed
\tau
-IID Regime

Let any
\tau\in(0,1)
be given. In the Fixed
\tau
-IID Regime, we consider an explicit semiparameteric model,
\textbf{M}^{\text{semi},\tau}
, with parameter space
\Theta^{\text{semi}}
. We define
\Theta^{\text{semi}}
in the following manner. Let
\Delta
denote a subset of the
m+1
dimensional probability simplex. Let
\mathcal{Q}_{0},\mathcal{Q}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{Q}_{m}
denote sets of densities, and define
\mathcal{Q}:=\mathcal{Q}_{0}\times\mathcal{Q}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathcal{Q}_{m}
. The parameter space for the parameter of interest is
\Delta
, and the nuisance parameter space is
\mathcal{Q}
; both
\Delta
and
\mathcal{Q}
are subject to various restrictions that shall be described later. Then,
\Theta^{\text{semi}}:=\Delta\times\mathcal{Q}
.

The semiparametric model is then given by
\textbf{M}^{\text{semi},\tau}:=\big\{\mathcal{J}^{\pi,\mathbf{q},\tau}\ \big\lvert\ (\pi,\mathbf{q})\in\Theta^{\text{semi}}\big\}
, where for each
(\pi,\mathbf{q})\in\Theta^{\text{semi}}
,
\mathcal{J}^{\pi,\mathbf{q},\tau}
is the density function for the joint distribution of a random vector
Z\equiv(X,Y,D)
that is generated in the following manner:

•

D\sim\text{Bern}(\tau)

•

If
D=1
:
Y=-1
,
X\sim q_{\pi}

•

If
D=0
:
Y\sim\text{Cat}(\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}})
,
X\mid Y=y\sim q_{y}
,

where
\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\in\Delta
is some fixed vector. One can show that
\mathcal{J}^{\pi,\mathbf{q},\tau}
has the following explicit form:

\mathcal{J}^{\pi,\mathbf{q},\tau}(z):=\big(\tau q_{\pi}(x)\big)^{d}\Bigg((1-\tau)\prod_{j=0}^{m}\big(\pi_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}q_{j}(x)\big)^{\mathbbold{1}{\{y=j\}}}\Bigg)^{1-d}\mathbbold{1}{\big\{x\in\mathcal{X},\text{ }y\in\mathcal{Y},\text{ }d\in\{0,1\}\big\}}.

In terms of data, we receive
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau}
, where
(\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p})\in\Theta^{\text{semi}}
denotes the true parameters. Here,
\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}},\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p}
and
\Theta^{\text{semi}}
do
not
depend on
n
. We assume
\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}},\tau
are known, and that
\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p}
are unknown.

6.1.3
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime

Let
\tau_{n}\in(0,1)
be a sequence such that
\tau_{n}n,(1-\tau_{n})n\to\infty
and
\tau_{n}\to\tau_{\infty}
for some
\tau_{\infty}\in[0,1]
. In the
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime, we receive
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}
for each
n
, where
\mathcal{J}^{\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p},\tau_{n}}\in\textbf{M}^{\text{semi},\tau_{n}}
and

\textbf{M}^{\text{semi},\tau_{n}}:=\big\{\mathcal{J}^{\pi,\mathbf{q},\tau_{n}}\ \big\lvert\ (\pi,\mathbf{q})\in\Theta^{\text{semi}}\big\}.

Like in the Fixed
\tau
-IID Regime,
(\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p})\in\Theta^{\text{semi}}
and
\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
do not depend on
n
; also, we assume
\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}},\tau_{n}
are known, and
\pi^{\ast},\mathbf{p}
are unknown.

6.2
Fixed Sequence Regime

6.2.1
SELSE for Other Regimes

The SELSE procedure at the end of subsection (
2.3
) is written using the notation of the Fixed Sequence Regime, but it is well defined for all three regimes considered in this Appendix. To obtain the equivalent procedure for the Fixed
\tau
-IID Regime and
\tau_{n}
-IID Regime, make the following notational adjustments:

•

\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
is those samples
(X_{i},Y_{i},D_{i})
for which
D_{i}=0
;
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{te}}}
is those samples for which
D_{i}=1
.

•

Replace
n^{\textup{\text{te}}}
and
n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
with
N^{\textup{\text{te}}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}
and
N^{\textup{\text{tr}}}:=n-N^{\textup{\text{te}}}
, respectively.

•

For each
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, replace
\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
with
\widehat{\pi}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}:=\frac{N_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}{N^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}
, where
N_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-D_{i})\mathbbold{1}{\{Y_{i}=y\}}
.

•

Replace Assumption (
A7
) with Assumption (
7
).

6.2.2
Assumptions

We make the following assumptions about the space that
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
lives in:

A1.

There exists a set of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
-valued functions
\mathcal{H}
(which may depend on
n
) s.t.
\mathcal{H}
is closed under scalar multiplication and
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}\in\mathcal{H}
.

A2.

For each
n
and
y\in[m]
, let
\mathcal{H}_{y}:=\{h_{y}\mid h\in\mathcal{H}\}
where
h_{y}
denotes the
{y}^{\text{th}}
component of a function
h
. Then, the function
\Omega
satisfies the following properties:

•

\Omega:\mathcal{H}_{y}\mapsto[0,\infty)

•

\Omega
is zero at the identically zero function

•

\Omega(ch_{y})=|c|\Omega(h_{y})
for all
h_{y}\in\mathcal{H}_{y}
and
c\in\mathbb{R}

•

\Omega(h_{y})\geq D||h_{y}||_{\infty}
for all
h_{y}\in\mathcal{H}_{y}
and some fixed global constant
D>0
.

A3.

For each
y\in[m]
, the sequence
\lambda_{y}
satisfies the following properties:

•

0<\lambda_{y}<\infty

•

\lambda_{y}=o(1)

•

\lambda_{y}\Omega^{2}(s_{\gamma^{\ast},y})=o(1)

•

\lambda_{y}^{2}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}=\omega(1)

•

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}[\mathcal{U}_{y}(\lambda_{y})]=o(1)
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{y}(\lambda_{y})

\displaystyle:=\sup\limits_{h_{y}\in\mathcal{H}_{y},\text{ }\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[h_{y}]\Big|

\displaystyle\qquad+\sup\limits_{h_{y}\in\mathcal{H}_{y},\text{ }\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{y}[h_{y}]\Big|

\displaystyle\qquad+\sup\limits_{h_{y}\in\mathcal{H}_{y},\text{ }\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[h_{y}]\Big|.

We also make the following assumptions about our sequence of
“true”
parameters:

A4.

\exists\ \nu>0
such that
\sigma_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\text{Cat})-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})\big)\geq\nu
, for all
n
.

A5.

\exists\ \xi\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
such that
\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}},\pi_{y}^{\ast}\in(\xi,1-\xi)
, for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
and
n
.

A6.

\exists\ \Lambda>0
such that
\sigma_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})\big)\geq\sqrt{\Lambda}
, for all
n
.

Lastly, we assume several properties about the estimator
\widehat{\gamma}
:

A7.

\widehat{\gamma}\in[L,U]
,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\big[||\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\ast}||_{2}^{2}\big]=o(1)
, and for each
a>0
,
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\big[||\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\ast}||_{2}\geq a\big]=o\Big(\sqrt{\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}\Big)
.

6.2.3
Lemmas

It is important to note that all Corollaries, Lemmas, and Theorems before Corollary (
6.15.1
) in the Fixed Sequence Regime concern themselves with
only
the first run through of the SELSE procedure. That is, in those Corollaries, Lemmas, and Theorems,
\widehat{\gamma}
is computed on
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}
,
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
is computed using
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}
and
\widehat{\gamma}
, and
\widehat{\pi}^{(a)}
is computed using
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}
and
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
. In addition, several references are made to an estimator
\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast}}
. This refers to a
“hypothetical”
estimator from the first run through of the SELSE procedure, obtained if we somehow knew
\gamma^{\ast}
and so could replace
\widehat{\gamma}
with
\gamma^{\ast}
in the constraints of the optimization problem. As
\gamma^{\ast}
is unknown (it depends on the unknown
\pi^{\ast}
),
\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast}}
cannot be computed, but for our theoretical analysis, analyzing its statistical properties help to elucidate those of
\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}
and therefore of
\widehat{\pi}^{(a)}
, and so ultimately, of
\widehat{\pi}
.

Lemma 6.1

(
Bounds on
\gamma^{\ast}
)
.

Define

L:=\frac{1}{1+m\big(\frac{1-\xi}{\xi}\big)^{3}},\qquad U:=\max\Bigg\{1-\xi,\text{ }\frac{1}{1+m\big(\frac{\xi}{1-\xi}\big)^{3}}\Bigg\}.

Then, we have that

L\leq\frac{a\alpha_{y}+b\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{a+b\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}\leq U\qquad\forall\ y\in\mathcal{Y},

whenever
a,b>0
and
\alpha,\beta
belong to the
m+1
dimensional probability simplex and satisfy
\xi\leq\alpha_{y},\beta_{y}\leq 1-\xi
for each
y\in\mathcal{Y}
. Furthermore, as a special case, if we set
a=\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}
,
b=\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}
,
\alpha=\pi^{\ast}
and
\beta=\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
, then we have that
L\leq\gamma_{y}^{\ast}\leq U
for each
y\in\mathcal{Y}
under Assumption (
A5
).

Lemma 6.2

(
Upper Bound on
\Omega(\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y})
)
.

Under Assumptions (
A1
), (
•
‣
A2
) and (
A5
), we have that

\Omega(\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}

for all
y\in[m]
.

Lemma 6.3

(
Bounds on Eigenvalues of
\mathcal{I}(\beta;\textup{Cat})
)
.

Let
\beta
be a vector in the
m+1
dimensional probability simplex, and suppose that the entries in
\beta
are bounded away from
0
and
1
. Then, we have that

2\leq\lambda_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\beta;\textup{Cat})\big),\qquad\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\beta;\textup{Cat})\big)\leq\frac{1}{\min_{y}\beta_{y}}+\frac{m}{\beta_{0}}.

Lemma 6.4

(
Bounds on Eigenvalues of
\mathcal{I}(\alpha;\text{Cat})\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})^{-1}
)
.

Let
\alpha,\beta
be vectors in the
m+1
dimensional probability simplex, and suppose that the entries in both vectors are bounded away from
0
and
1
. Then, we have that

1-(m+1)\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{y}\alpha_{y}}\leq\lambda_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\alpha;\textup{Cat})\mathcal{I}(\beta;\textup{Cat})^{-1}\big)

and

\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\alpha;\textup{Cat})\mathcal{I}(\beta;\textup{Cat})^{-1}\big)\leq 1+(m+1)\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{y}\alpha_{y}}.

Lemma 6.5

(
Bounds on Eigenvalues of
\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]
)
.

Under Assumptions (
A5
) and (
A6
), we have that

\frac{\Lambda\xi}{m+1}\leq\lambda_{\textup{min}}\big(\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big),\qquad\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)\leq\frac{m^{2}}{L^{2}}.

Lemma 6.6

(
Bounds on
s_{\beta,j}-s_{\alpha,j}
)
.

Let
\alpha,\beta
be vectors in the
m+1
dimensional probability simplex, and suppose that the entries in both vectors are bounded away from
0
and
1
. Then, we have that

\displaystyle|s_{\beta,j}-s_{\alpha,j}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\Big(\min\limits_{0\leq k\leq m}\alpha_{k}\Big)\Big(\min\limits_{0\leq k\leq m}\beta_{k}\Big)}\sum_{k=0}^{m}|\alpha_{k}-\beta_{k}|,

for each
j\in[m]
.

Lemma 6.7

(
Lower Bound on
\sigma_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\big)
)
.

Under Assumptions (
A4
) and (
A5
), we have that

\sigma_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\big)\geq\nu\bigg(\frac{L}{m+1}\bigg)^{2}.

Corollary 6.7.1

(
Relationship between Variances of Different Mixtures
)
.

Let
\alpha,\beta
be vectors in the
m+1
dimensional probability simplex, and suppose that the entries in both vectors are bounded away from
0
and
1
. Then, for any function
f:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, we have that:

\textup{\text{Var}}_{\alpha}[f]\leq\bigg(\frac{1}{\min_{y}\beta_{y}}+\frac{m}{\beta_{0}}\bigg)\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f].

Corollary 6.7.2

(
First Order Constant for
f=s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
)
.

The matrix
\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})
is positive definite under Assumption (
A6
) and satisfies
\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})=A_{s_{\gamma^{\ast}}}
. Further,

\displaystyle\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\Big[\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}])\Big]

\displaystyle=\Bigg[\frac{2}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{2}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg]\Big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{2}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1},

which is positive definite under Assumption (
A5
).

Lemma 6.8

(
Alternative Expression for First Order Error
)
.

Consider an arbitrary ordering
i=1,\dots,n
of the data points in
\mathcal{D}
, where
i\in[1,n/2]
if the
{i}^{\text{th}}
data point is from
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}
and
i\in[n/2+1,n]
if the
{i}^{\text{th}}
data point is from
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}
. Let
Z_{i}:=(X_{i},Y_{i},D_{i})
be defined as follows. If the
{i}^{\text{th}}
data point is from the test set, then
(X_{i},Y_{i},D_{i})=(X_{i},-1,1)
. If the
{i}^{\text{th}}
data point is from the training set, then
(X_{i},Y_{i},D_{i})=(X_{i},Y_{i},0)
. Then, we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}\big(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}\psi^{\textup{eff}}(Z_{i}),

where
\mathcal{Z}:=\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\{0,1\}
and, for each
z=(x,y,d)\in\mathcal{Z}
, the function
\psi^{\textup{eff}}:\mathcal{Z}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{m}
is given by

\displaystyle\psi^{\textup{eff}}(z)

\displaystyle:=\frac{d}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}/n}\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}\big(s_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{1-d}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}/n}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\mathbbold{1}{\{y=j\}}\frac{\pi_{j}^{\ast}}{n_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}/n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}\big(s_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)-\mathbb{E}_{j}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)

where
n_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}:=\frac{1}{n/2}\sum_{k=1}^{n/2}\mathbbold{1}{\{Y_{k}=j\}}
is the fraction of the training points in
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}
that belong to class
j
. Analogously, we also have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}\big(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi^{\textup{eff}}(Z_{i}).

Lemma 6.9

(
Uniform Deviation Tail Bounds
)
.

Under Assumptions (
•
‣
A2
) and (
A5
), we have for each
y\in[m]
,
j\in 0\cup[m]
and all
a>0
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{j}[h_{y}]\Big|\geq a\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\Bigg\{-\frac{D^{2}L^{2}\xi}{16}\lambda_{y}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}a^{2}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbbold{1}{\Bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\bigg(\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{j}[h_{y}]\Big|\bigg)\geq\frac{1}{2}a\Bigg\}}

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[h_{y}]\Big|\geq a\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\Bigg\{-\frac{D^{4}L^{4}}{1024}\lambda_{y}^{2}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}a^{2}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbbold{1}{\Bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg(\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[h_{y}]\Big|\Bigg)\geq\frac{1}{2}a\Bigg\}}.

Lemma 6.10

(
Rate for Learning Diagonal of
\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})
, with Unknown
\gamma^{\ast}
)
.

Under Assumptions (
A1
), (
•
‣
A2
), (
A3
), (
A5
), (
A6
) and (
A7
), we have for each
y\in[m]
that

\big(\mathbb{E}_{y}[s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\big)-\big(\mathbb{E}_{y}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}]\big)\leq\widetilde{T}_{y},

where
\widetilde{T}_{y}>0
is a random variable that satisfies

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}[\widetilde{T}_{y}]=O\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}[T_{y}]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}[\mathcal{U}_{y}(\lambda_{y})]+\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Big[||\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\ast}||_{2}^{2}\Big]}+\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Big[\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}-s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\Big]\Bigg).

Further, for all
a>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}[\widetilde{T}_{y}\geq a]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}[T_{y}\geq a/7]+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{y}[h_{y}]\big|\geq\frac{1}{14}a\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[h_{y}]\big|\geq\frac{1}{14}a\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\big|\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[h_{y}]\big|\geq\frac{1}{14}\frac{L}{m+1}a\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+2\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Bigg[||\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\ast}||_{2}\geq\widetilde{\tau}_{1}(a)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+2\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}-s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\geq\frac{1}{14}\frac{L^{2}}{m+1}a\Bigg],

where

\widetilde{\tau}_{1}(a):=\min\Bigg\{\frac{L^{4}}{21\sqrt{m}(m+1)+14\sqrt{m}(m+1)^{2}}a,\sqrt{\frac{1}{14}\frac{L^{2}}{m(m+1)}a}\Bigg\}.

Lemma 6.11

(
Rate for Learning the Score Function, with Unknown
\gamma^{\ast}
)
.

Under Assumptions (
A1
), (
•
‣
A2
), (
A3
), (
A5
), (
A6
) and (
A7
), we have for each
y\in[m]
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Big[\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}-s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\Big]

\displaystyle=O\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}[\widetilde{T}_{y}]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Big[\mathcal{U}_{y}(\lambda_{y})\Big]+\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Big[||\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\ast}||_{2}^{2}\Big]}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Big[\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}-s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\Big]\Bigg).

Further, for all
a>0
, we have that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Big[\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},y}-s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\geq a\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}[\widetilde{T}_{y}\geq a/7]+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{y}[h_{y}]\Big|\geq a/7\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[h_{y}]\Big|\geq a/7\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[h_{y}]\Big|\geq\frac{L^{3}}{77(m+2)^{3}}a\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+2\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Bigg[||\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\ast}||_{2}\geq\widetilde{\tau}_{2}(a)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+2\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}-s_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\geq\frac{L^{4}}{70(m+2)^{3}}a\Bigg],

where

\widetilde{\tau}_{2}(a):=\min\Bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{L^{4}}{70m(m+2)^{3}}a},\frac{L^{5}}{66\sqrt{m}(m+2)^{3}}a\Bigg\}.

Lemma 6.12

(
Rate for Learning Columns of
\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})
)
.

Under Assumptions (
A1
), (
•
‣
A2
), (

), (

), (

) and (

), we have for each

that

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}}\bigg[\Big|\Big|\textup{col }y\textup{ of }\widehat{A}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})\Big|\Big|_{2}^{2}\bigg]=O\Bigg(\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Big[\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},j}-s_{\gamma^{\ast},j}]\Big]\Bigg).

Further, for all
a>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}}\Big[||\textup{col }y\textup{ of }\widehat{A}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})||_{2}^{2}\geq a\Big]

\displaystyle\leq 4\sum_{j=1}^{m}\exp\bigg\{-\frac{\xi L^{2}D^{2}\lambda_{j}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}{64m}a\bigg\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\bigg[\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},j}-s_{\gamma^{\ast},j}]\geq\frac{L^{2}}{4m}a\bigg].

Lemma 6.13

(
Learning the Inverse of
\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}

and that

)

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}}\bigg[\Big|\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}-s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}-s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\Big|\Big|_{2}^{2}\bigg]=O\Bigg(\bigg(\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\bigg)\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Big[\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[\widehat{s}_{\widehat{\gamma},j}-s_{\gamma^{\ast},j}]\Big]\Bigg).

Theorem 6.15

(
Error Rate of
\widehat{\pi}^{(a)}
)
.

Under Assumptions (
A1
), (
•
‣
A2
), (
A3
), (
A4
), (
A5
), (
A6
) and (
A7
), we have that

\displaystyle\widehat{\pi}^{(a)}-\pi^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}\psi^{\textup{eff}}(Z_{i})+\epsilon^{(a)}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\Bigg(\Bigg[\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg]\Big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Big)+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}Z^{(a)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\epsilon^{(a)},

where
Z^{(a)}\to\mathcal{N}(0,I_{m})
, and
\epsilon^{(a)}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a random vector satisfying

\displaystyle||\epsilon^{(a)}||_{2}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbb{P}}\Bigg(\sqrt{\bigg(\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\bigg)\bigg\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}+\max_{y}\lambda_{y}\Omega(s_{\gamma^{\ast},y})^{2}+\frac{e^{-Cn^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}{\sqrt{\min_{y}\lambda_{y}}}\bigg\}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt+O_{\mathbb{P}}\Bigg(\sqrt{\bigg(\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\bigg)\bigg\{\max_{y}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Big[\mathcal{U}_{y}(\lambda_{y})\Big]+\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Big[||\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\ast}||_{2}^{2}\Big]}\bigg\}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt+O_{\mathbb{P}}\Bigg(e^{-C(\min_{y}\lambda_{y})^{2}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Big[\big|\big|\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\ast}\big|\big|_{2}\geq C\Big]\Bigg),

for some global constant
C>0
.

Corollary 6.15.1

(
Error Rate of
\widehat{\pi}
)
.

Under Assumptions (
A1
), (
•
‣
A2
), (
A3
), (
A4
), (
A5
), (
A6
) and (
A7
), we have that

\displaystyle\widehat{\pi}-\pi^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi^{\textup{eff}}(Z_{i})+\epsilon

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\Bigg[\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg]\Big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Big)+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}Z+\epsilon,

where
Z\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.09723ptd\hskip 4.09723pt}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{m})
, and
\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a random vector satisfying

\displaystyle||\epsilon||_{2}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbb{P}}\Bigg(\sqrt{\bigg(\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\bigg)\bigg\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}+\max_{y}\lambda_{y}\Omega(s_{\gamma^{\ast},y})^{2}+\frac{e^{-Cn^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}{\sqrt{\min_{y}\lambda_{y}}}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt+O_{\mathbb{P}}\Bigg(\sqrt{\bigg(\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\bigg)\bigg\{\max_{y}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Big[\mathcal{U}_{y}(\lambda_{y})\Big]+\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Big[||\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\ast}||_{2}^{2}\Big]}\bigg\}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt+O_{\mathbb{P}}\Bigg(e^{-C(\min_{y}\lambda_{y})^{2}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Big[\big|\big|\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\ast}\big|\big|_{2}\geq C\Big]\Bigg),

for some global constant
C>0
.

Corollary 6.15.2

(
RKHS Application
)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
denote a set of reproducing kernels, where
\mathcal{K}
may depend on
n
. For each kernel
k\in\mathcal{K}
, let
H_{k}
denote the corresponding RKHS and
||\cdot||_{k}
the RKHS norm. Define
H_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\Omega_{\mathcal{K}}:H_{\mathcal{K}}\to[0,\infty)
by

H_{\mathcal{K}}:=\cup_{k\in\mathcal{K}}H_{k}

\Omega_{\mathcal{K}}(\ell):=\inf\Big\{||\ell||_{k}\ \Big\lvert\ k\in\mathcal{K},\ \ell\in H_{k}\Big\}\qquad\forall\ \ell\in H_{\mathcal{K}}.

Assume that:

•

For each
n
,
\sup\limits_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\sup\limits_{x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}}\sqrt{k(x_{1},x_{2})}\leq\kappa
, where
\kappa>0
is a finite, global constant.

•

For each
n
, the psuedo-dimension
d_{\mathcal{K}}
of
\mathcal{K}
(see Definition 3 in
[
Yiming and Colin, 2010
]
) is finite.

If
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}\in H_{\mathcal{K}}^{m}
,
\mathcal{H}=H_{\mathcal{K}}^{m}
and
\Omega=\Omega_{\mathcal{K}}
, then Assumptions (
A1
) and (
•
‣
A2
) are satisfied with
D=\frac{1}{\kappa}
. Further, for each class
y\in[m]
:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\big[\mathcal{U}_{y}(\lambda_{y})\big]=O\Bigg(\frac{1}{\lambda_{y}}\sqrt{\frac{1+d_{\mathcal{K}}}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}\Bigg).

Corollary 6.15.3

(
Standardized Error Rate of
\widehat{\pi}
, Application to RKHS
)
.

In the setting of Corollary (
6.15.2
), if Assumptions (
A4
), (
A5
), (
A6
) and (
A7
) hold, and if additionally we have that

•

\frac{1+d_{\mathcal{K}}}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}=o(1)

•

\max_{j}\Omega(s_{\gamma^{\ast},j})=o\bigg(\frac{(n^{\textup{\text{tr}}})^{\frac{1}{4}}}{\max\big\{\sqrt{\log(n^{\textup{\text{tr}}})},(1+d_{\mathcal{K}})^{1/4}\big\}}\bigg)
,

then, by choosing

\lambda_{y}\asymp\frac{1}{\max_{j}\Omega(s_{\gamma^{\ast},j})}\bigg(\frac{1+d_{\mathcal{K}}}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{4}}\qquad\forall y\in[m],

we obtain that

\displaystyle\Bigg(\Bigg[\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg]\Big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Big)+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Bigg)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\widehat{\pi}-\pi^{\ast})=Z+\epsilon,

where

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\Bigg[\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg]\Big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Big)+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\Bigg)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi^{\textup{eff}}(Z_{i})\bigg)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.09723ptd\hskip 4.09723pt}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{m}),

and
\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a random vector satisfying

\displaystyle||\epsilon||_{2}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbb{P}}\Bigg(\bigg(\frac{1+d_{\mathcal{K}}}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\bigg)^{\tfrac{1}{8}}\sqrt{\max_{y}\Omega(s_{\gamma^{\ast},y})}+\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\Big[||\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\ast}||_{2}^{2}\Big]\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}+\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{II}}}}\big[||\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\ast}||_{2}\geq C\big]}{\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\tfrac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),

and
C>0
is a global constant.

6.2.4
Proofs

Proof of Lemma (
6.1
)
.

Let any
y\in\mathcal{Y}
be given, and for convenience, define

q_{y}:=\frac{a\alpha_{y}+b\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{a+b\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}

We will prove the desired inequality for
q_{y}
; the desired result for
\gamma_{y}^{\ast}
then follows as a special case of this more general inequality, namely by setting
a=\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}
,
b=\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}
,
\alpha=\pi^{\ast}
and
\beta=\pi^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
.

To prove the desired inequality for
q_{y}
, begin by applying the mediant inequality to the two fractions
\alpha_{y}=\frac{a\alpha_{y}}{a}
and
\frac{\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}=\frac{b\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{b\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}
; then, because
q_{y}
is their mediant, we find ourselves with the following two cases:

•

If
\alpha_{y}\leq\frac{\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}
, then
\alpha_{y}\leq q_{y}\leq\frac{\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}
.

•

If
\frac{\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}<\alpha_{y}
, then
\frac{\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}\leq q_{y}\leq\alpha_{y}
.

Since
\xi\leq\alpha_{y}\leq 1-\xi
, so the display above implies that:

•

If
\alpha_{y}\leq\frac{\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}
, then
\xi\leq q_{y}\leq\frac{\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}
.

•

If
\frac{\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}<\alpha_{y}
, then
\frac{\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}\leq q_{y}\leq 1-\xi
.

Next, let’s obtain upper and lower bounds on
\frac{\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}
in terms of
\xi
. Note that:

\frac{\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}=\frac{\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}+\sum\limits_{k=0,k\neq y}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}=\frac{1}{1+\frac{\beta_{y}}{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}\sum\limits_{k=0,k\neq y}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}

Now, for each
k\in\mathcal{Y}
, the fact that
\xi\leq\alpha_{k},\beta_{k}\leq 1-\xi
implies that
\frac{\xi^{2}}{1-\xi}\leq\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}\leq\frac{(1-\xi)^{2}}{\xi}
and
\frac{\xi}{(1-\xi)^{2}}\leq\frac{\beta_{y}}{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}\leq\frac{1-\xi}{\xi^{2}}
, so

m\frac{\xi^{2}}{1-\xi}\leq\sum\limits_{k=0,k\neq y}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}\leq m\frac{(1-\xi)^{2}}{\xi}

and so

\implies m\frac{\xi^{3}}{(1-\xi)^{3}}\leq\frac{\beta_{y}}{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}\sum\limits_{k=0,k\neq y}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}\leq m\frac{(1-\xi)^{3}}{\xi^{3}}

\implies 1+m\bigg(\frac{\xi}{1-\xi}\bigg)^{3}\leq 1+\frac{\beta_{y}}{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}\sum\limits_{k=0,k\neq y}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}\leq 1+m\bigg(\frac{1-\xi}{\xi}\bigg)^{3}

\implies\frac{1}{1+m\big(\frac{1-\xi}{\xi}\big)^{3}}\leq\frac{1}{1+\frac{\beta_{y}}{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}\sum\limits_{k=0,k\neq y}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}\leq\frac{1}{1+m\big(\frac{\xi}{1-\xi}\big)^{3}}

\implies\frac{1}{1+m\big(\frac{1-\xi}{\xi}\big)^{3}}\leq\frac{\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}\leq\frac{1}{1+m\big(\frac{\xi}{1-\xi}\big)^{3}}

Thus, based on our earlier observation from the mediant inequality, our two cases now become:

•

If
\alpha_{y}\leq\frac{\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}
, then
\xi\leq q_{y}\leq\frac{1}{1+m\big(\frac{\xi}{1-\xi}\big)^{3}}
.

•

If
\frac{\frac{(\alpha_{y})^{2}}{\beta_{y}}}{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(\alpha_{k})^{2}}{\beta_{k}}}<\alpha_{y}
, then
\frac{1}{1+m\big(\frac{1-\xi}{\xi}\big)^{3}}\leq q_{y}\leq 1-\xi
.

Now, let
L=\min\Bigg\{\xi,\text{ }\frac{1}{1+m\big(\frac{1-\xi}{\xi}\big)^{3}}\Bigg\}
and
U=\max\Bigg\{1-\xi,\text{ }\frac{1}{1+m\big(\frac{\xi}{1-\xi}\big)^{3}}\Bigg\}
. Then, regardless of which of the above two cases we are in, we always have that:

\implies L\leq q_{y}\leq U.

Finally, the desired result comes from noticing that:

\min\Bigg\{\xi,\text{ }\frac{1}{1+m\big(\frac{1-\xi}{\xi}\big)^{3}}\Bigg\}=\frac{1}{1+m\big(\frac{1-\xi}{\xi}\big)^{3}}.

∎

Proof of Lemma (
6.2
)
.

For each
k\in\mathcal{Y}
, let
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
denote the subset of
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
that belongs to class
k
. Let
\widehat{p}_{k}(x):=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}|}\mathbbold{1}{\big\{x\in\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}\big\}}
be the empirical probability mass function associated with
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
. In addition, define
\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x):=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\gamma_{k}^{\ast}\widehat{p}_{k}(x)
.

Now, let any
y\in[m]
be given. By the constraint in our procedure, we have that:

\displaystyle\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\widehat{p}_{y}(x)\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}(x)-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\widehat{p}_{0}(x)\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\big(\widehat{p}_{y}(x)-\widehat{p}_{0}(x)\big)\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)\frac{\widehat{p}_{y}(x)-\widehat{p}_{0}(x)}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)}\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}(x)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\textup{\text{Cov}}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}\bigg[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y},\text{ }\frac{\widehat{p}_{y}-\widehat{p}_{0}}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]}\sqrt{\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}\bigg[\frac{\widehat{p}_{y}-\widehat{p}_{0}}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}}\bigg]}.

Ergo:

\implies\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\leq\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}\bigg[\frac{\widehat{p}_{y}-\widehat{p}_{0}}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}}\bigg].

Next, let us obtain upper and lower bounds on
\frac{\widehat{p}_{y}(x)-\widehat{p}_{0}(x)}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)}
, for
x\in\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
. Let’s start with upper bounds. For any
x\in\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
, we have that

\frac{\widehat{p}_{y}(x)-\widehat{p}_{0}(x)}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)}\leq\frac{\widehat{p}_{y}(x)}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)},

where
\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)>0
since
x\in\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
. Now, if
x\notin\mathcal{D}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
, then
\frac{\widehat{p}_{y}(x)}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)}=0
, whereas if
x\in\mathcal{D}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
, then

\frac{\widehat{p}_{y}(x)}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)}\leq\frac{\widehat{p}_{y}(x)}{\gamma_{y}^{\ast}\widehat{p}_{y}(x)}=\frac{1}{\gamma_{y}^{\ast}}
. Therefore, in general, it follows that

\frac{\widehat{p}_{y}(x)-\widehat{p}_{0}(x)}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)}\leq\frac{1}{\gamma_{y}^{\ast}}\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}.

Next, let’s address the lower bound. For any
x\in\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
, we have that

\frac{\widehat{p}_{y}(x)-\widehat{p}_{0}(x)}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)}\geq-\frac{\widehat{p}_{0}(x)}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)}.

If
x\notin\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
, then
\frac{\widehat{p}_{0}(x)}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)}=0
, whereas if
x\in\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
, then
-\frac{\widehat{p}_{0}(x)}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)}\geq-\frac{\widehat{p}_{0}(x)}{\gamma_{0}^{\ast}\widehat{p}_{0}(x)}=-\frac{1}{\gamma_{0}^{\ast}}
. Therefore, in general, it follows that:

\frac{\widehat{p}_{y}(x)-\widehat{p}_{0}(x)}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)}\geq-\frac{1}{\gamma_{0}^{\ast}}.

So, overall, we’ve shown that

-\frac{1}{\gamma_{0}^{\ast}}\leq\frac{\widehat{p}_{y}(x)-\widehat{p}_{0}(x)}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)}\leq\frac{1}{\gamma_{y}^{\ast}},

for all
x\in\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}
. So,

\displaystyle\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}\bigg[\frac{\widehat{p}_{y}-\widehat{p}_{0}}{\widehat{p}_{\gamma^{\ast}}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\bigg(\frac{1}{\gamma_{0}^{\ast}}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{y}^{\ast}}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\bigg(\frac{2}{L}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L^{2}}.

where the first line is due to Popoviciu’s variance inequality, and the second line is due to Assumption (
A5
) and Lemma (
6.1
). By our work earlier in this proof, it follows that

\implies\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\leq\frac{1}{L^{2}}.

Next, consider again the procedure for constructing
\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}
. By Assumption (
A1
), the identically zero function is included in
\mathcal{H}_{y}
. Also note that the identically zero function trivially satisfies the moment constraint in the procedure, and that
\Omega(\cdot)
equals zero when applied to the identically zero function, by Assumption (
•
‣
A2
). Altogether, these observations imply that:

0\leq\big(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]\big)-\lambda_{y}\Omega(\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y})^{2}

\displaystyle\implies\Omega(\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y})

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]}{\lambda_{y}}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{\textup{\text{Var}}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[\widehat{s}_{\gamma^{\ast},y}]}{\lambda_{y}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{\lambda_{y}L^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}},

where the second line is due to the procedure’s moment constraints.

∎

Proof of Lemma (
6.3
)
.

It is easy to show that, for
i,j\in[m]
:

\mathcal{I}(\beta;\textup{Cat})_{i,j}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\beta_{i}}+\frac{1}{\beta_{0}}&i=j\\
\frac{1}{\beta_{0}}&i\neq j.\end{cases}

Therefore:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\beta;\textup{Cat})\big)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\big|\mathcal{I}(\beta;\textup{Cat})_{i,j}\big|

\displaystyle=\max_{i}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{\beta_{i}}+\frac{1}{\beta_{0}}+\frac{m-1}{\beta_{0}}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\max_{i}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{\beta_{i}}+\frac{m}{\beta_{0}}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\min_{i}\beta_{i}}+\frac{m}{\beta_{0}},

as claimed. As for the upper bound, it is easy to show that
\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})^{-1}
is equal to the expression given in Lemma (
2.5
). It follows that:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})\big)}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\big|\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})^{-1}_{i,j}\big|

\displaystyle=\max_{i}\beta_{i}\Bigg\{(1-\beta_{i})+\sum_{j=1\neq i}^{m}\beta_{j}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{i}\beta_{i}(1-\beta_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2},

meaning that
\lambda_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})\big)\geq 2
, as claimed.
∎

Proof of Lemma (
6.4
)
.

One can verify that, for any
i,j\in[m]
:

\big[\mathcal{I}(\alpha;\text{Cat})\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})^{-1}\big]_{i,j}=\begin{cases}\beta_{j}\Big(\frac{\beta_{0}}{\alpha_{0}}+\frac{1-\beta_{j}}{\alpha_{j}}\Big)&i=j\\
\\
\beta_{j}\Big(\frac{\beta_{0}}{\alpha_{0}}-\frac{\beta_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}\Big)&i\neq j.\end{cases}

Now, let
(\lambda,u)
denote an eigenvalue and unit eigenvector pair for the matrix
\mathcal{I}(\alpha;\text{Cat})\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})^{-1}
. Then, for each
i\in[m]
,
(\lambda,u)
satisfies

\displaystyle\lambda u_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\big[\mathcal{I}(\alpha;\text{Cat})\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})^{-1}\big]_{i,j}u_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1\neq i}^{m}\big[\mathcal{I}(\alpha;\text{Cat})\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})^{-1}\big]_{i,j}u_{j}+\big[\mathcal{I}(\alpha;\text{Cat})\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})^{-1}\big]_{i,i}u_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1\neq i}^{m}\beta_{j}\bigg(\frac{\beta_{0}}{\alpha_{0}}-\frac{\beta_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}\bigg)u_{j}+\beta_{i}\Big(\frac{\beta_{0}}{\alpha_{0}}+\frac{1-\beta_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}\Big)u_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1\neq i}^{m}\beta_{j}\bigg(\frac{\beta_{0}}{\alpha_{0}}-\frac{\beta_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}\bigg)u_{j}+\beta_{i}\Big(\frac{\beta_{0}}{\alpha_{0}}-\frac{\beta_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}\Big)u_{i}+\frac{\beta_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}u_{i}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{\beta_{0}}{\alpha_{0}}-\frac{\beta_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}\bigg)\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta_{j}u_{j}+\frac{\beta_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}u_{i},

meaning that:

\implies\bigg(\lambda-\frac{\beta_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}\bigg)u_{i}=\bigg(\frac{\beta_{0}}{\alpha_{0}}-\frac{\beta_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}\bigg)\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta_{j}u_{j}

\displaystyle\implies\bigg|\lambda-\frac{\beta_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}\bigg||u_{i}|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg|\frac{\beta_{0}}{\alpha_{0}}-\frac{\beta_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}\bigg|||u||_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{m}\bigg|\frac{\beta_{0}}{\alpha_{0}}-\frac{\beta_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}\bigg|,

where the second inequality is because
||u||_{2}=1
. Now, the inequality above holds for all
i\in[m]
, so let us now pick a specific
\tilde{i}
. In particular, let
\tilde{i}
denote an index for which
\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\leq|u_{\tilde{i}}|
. To see that such an index
\tilde{i}
always exists, suppose towards a contradiction that
|u_{i}|<\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}
for all
i\in[m]
. Then,
u_{i}^{2}<\frac{1}{m}
for all
i\in[m]
, and so
\sum_{i=1}^{m}u_{i}^{2}<1
. This implies that
||u||_{2}<1
, contradicting the fact that
||u||_{2}=1
. Ergo, there must exist at least one
\tilde{i}\in[m]
such that
|u_{\tilde{i}}|\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}
, as claimed. Applying this fact to the display above, it follows that:

\displaystyle\implies\bigg|\lambda-\frac{\beta_{\tilde{i}}}{\alpha_{\tilde{i}}}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq m\bigg|\frac{\beta_{0}}{\alpha_{0}}-\frac{\beta_{\tilde{i}}}{\alpha_{\tilde{i}}}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq m\bigg|\frac{\beta_{0}}{\alpha_{0}}-1\bigg|+m\bigg|1-\frac{\beta_{\tilde{i}}}{\alpha_{\tilde{i}}}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq m\frac{\big|\beta_{0}-\alpha_{0}\big|+\big|\alpha_{\tilde{i}}-\beta_{\tilde{i}}\big|}{\min_{k}\alpha_{k}}

\displaystyle\leq m\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{k}\alpha_{k}}.

Thus, we have that:

\implies-m\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{k}\alpha_{k}}\leq\lambda-\frac{\beta_{\tilde{i}}}{\alpha_{\tilde{i}}}\leq m\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{k}\alpha_{k}}

\implies\frac{\beta_{\tilde{i}}}{\alpha_{\tilde{i}}}-m\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{k}\alpha_{k}}\leq\lambda\leq\frac{\beta_{\tilde{i}}}{\alpha_{\tilde{i}}}+m\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{k}\alpha_{k}}

\implies 1+\bigg(\frac{\beta_{\tilde{i}}}{\alpha_{\tilde{i}}}-1\bigg)-m\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{k}\alpha_{k}}\leq\lambda\leq 1+\bigg(\frac{\beta_{\tilde{i}}}{\alpha_{\tilde{i}}}-1\bigg)+m\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{k}\alpha_{k}}

\implies 1-\bigg|\frac{\beta_{\tilde{i}}-\alpha_{\tilde{i}}}{\alpha_{\tilde{i}}}\bigg|-m\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{k}\alpha_{k}}\leq\lambda\leq 1+\bigg|\frac{\beta_{\tilde{i}}-\alpha_{\tilde{i}}}{\alpha_{\tilde{i}}}\bigg|+m\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{k}\alpha_{k}}

\implies 1-\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{k}\alpha_{k}}-m\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{k}\alpha_{k}}\leq\lambda\leq 1+\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{k}\alpha_{k}}+m\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{k}\alpha_{k}}

\implies 1-(m+1)\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{k}\alpha_{k}}\leq\lambda\leq 1+(m+1)\frac{||\alpha-\beta||_{1}}{\min_{k}\alpha_{k}}.

Since the display above holds for all eigenvalues, we have proven the desideratum.
∎

Proof of Lemma (
6.5
)
.

Under Assumption (
A6
), we have that:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})\big)

\displaystyle=\min_{||c||_{2}=1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}c_{i}c_{j}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast},i}s_{\gamma^{\ast},j}]

\displaystyle=\min_{||c||_{2}=1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}c_{i}c_{j}\int p_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)\frac{p_{i}(x)-p_{0}(x)}{p_{\gamma^{\ast}}(x)}s_{\gamma^{\ast},j}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\min_{||c||_{2}=1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}c_{i}c_{j}\int p_{\pi^{\ast}}(x)\frac{p_{i}(x)-p_{0}(x)}{p_{\pi^{\ast}}(x)}s_{\gamma^{\ast},j}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\min_{||c||_{2}=1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}c_{i}c_{j}\textup{\text{Cov}}_{\pi^{\ast}}\big[s_{\pi^{\ast},i},s_{\gamma^{\ast},j}\big]

\displaystyle=\min_{||c||_{2}=1}\textup{\text{Cov}}_{\pi^{\ast}}\big[c^{T}s_{\pi^{\ast}},c^{T}s_{\gamma^{\ast}}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\min_{||c||_{2}=1}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}\big[c^{T}s_{\pi^{\ast}}\big]\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}\big[c^{T}s_{\gamma^{\ast}}\big]}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\min_{||c||_{2}=1}\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast})\big)\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}\big[c^{T}s_{\gamma^{\ast}}\big]}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast})\big)}\sqrt{\lambda_{\textup{min}}\big(\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})\big)}\sqrt{\lambda_{\textup{min}}\big(\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{\min_{y}\pi_{y}^{\ast}}+\frac{m}{\pi_{0}^{\ast}}}\sqrt{\lambda_{\textup{min}}\big(\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{m+1}{\xi}}\sqrt{\lambda_{\textup{min}}\big(\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)},

where the third to last line is because
\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast})\preceq\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\text{Cat})
, the second to last line is by Lemma (
6.3
), and the last line follows from Assumption (
A5
). Thus, it follows that:

\implies\frac{\Lambda\xi}{m+1}\leq\lambda_{\textup{min}}\big(\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big),

as claimed. As for the upper bound on
\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)
, observe that:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\Big|\textup{\text{Cov}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast},i}\text{ },\text{ }s_{\gamma^{\ast},j}]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sqrt{\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast},i}]\textup{\text{Var}}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast},j}]}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{m^{2}}{L^{2}},

where the third inequality uses Popoviciu’s variance inequality.

∎

Proof of Lemma (
6.6
)
.

Observe that, for each
j\in[m]
:

\displaystyle s_{\beta,j}-s_{\alpha,j}

\displaystyle=s_{\alpha,j}\frac{p_{\alpha}}{p_{\beta}}-s_{\alpha,j}

\displaystyle=s_{\alpha,j}\bigg(\frac{p_{\alpha}}{p_{\beta}}-1\bigg)

\displaystyle=s_{\alpha,j}\bigg(\frac{p_{\alpha}-p_{\beta}}{p_{\beta}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=s_{\alpha,j}\sum_{k=0}^{m}(\alpha_{k}-\beta_{k})\frac{p_{k}}{p_{\beta}}.

Thus, we have that

\displaystyle|s_{\beta,j}-s_{\alpha,j}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\min\limits_{0\leq k\leq m}\alpha_{k}}\sum_{k=0}^{m}|\alpha_{k}-\beta_{k}|\frac{p_{k}}{p_{\beta}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\min\limits_{0\leq k\leq m}\alpha_{k}}\sum_{k=0}^{m}|\alpha_{k}-\beta_{k}|\frac{p_{k}}{\beta_{k}p_{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\Big(\min\limits_{0\leq k\leq m}\alpha_{k}\Big)\Big(\min\limits_{0\leq k\leq m}\beta_{k}\Big)}\sum_{k=0}^{m}|\alpha_{k}-\beta_{k}|,

as claimed.

∎

Proof of Lemma (
6.7
)
.

Observe that:

\displaystyle\sigma_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}[\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})]\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\geq\sigma_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}\big)\sigma_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})\big)\sigma_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})\big)}{\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})\big)\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})\big)}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\nu}{\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})\big)\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})\big)}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\nu}{\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\textup{Cat})\big)^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\nu}{\Big(\frac{1}{\min_{y}\gamma_{y}^{\ast}}+\frac{m}{\gamma_{0}^{\ast}}\Big)^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\nu}{\Big(\frac{m+1}{L}\Big)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\nu\bigg(\frac{L}{m+1}\bigg)^{2}.

where the fourth line follows from Assumption (
A4
), the fifth line uses Lemma (
3.5
), the sixth line uses Lemma (
6.3
), and the seventh line uses Lemma (
6.1
).
∎

Proof of Corollary (
6.7.1
)
.

Let
a=\Big[\mathbb{E}_{1}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f],\dots,\mathbb{E}_{m}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{0}[f]\Big]
. By Lemma (
2.5
), we have that:

\displaystyle\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=0}^{m}\beta_{y}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+a\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})^{-1}a^{T}

\displaystyle\geq(\min_{y}\beta_{y})\sum_{y=0}^{m}\alpha_{y}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+\frac{a\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})^{-1}a^{T}}{a\mathcal{I}(\alpha;\text{Cat})^{-1}a^{T}}a\mathcal{I}(\alpha;\text{Cat})^{-1}a^{T}

\displaystyle\geq(\min_{y}\beta_{y})\sum_{y=0}^{m}\alpha_{y}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+\frac{\lambda_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})^{-1}\big)}{\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\alpha;\text{Cat})^{-1}\big)}a\mathcal{I}(\alpha;\text{Cat})^{-1}a^{T}

\displaystyle=(\min_{y}\beta_{y})\sum_{y=0}^{m}\alpha_{y}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+\frac{\lambda_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\alpha;\text{Cat})\big)}{\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})\big)}a\mathcal{I}(\alpha;\text{Cat})^{-1}a^{T}

\displaystyle\geq(\min_{y}\beta_{y})\sum_{y=0}^{m}\alpha_{y}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+\frac{2}{\frac{1}{\min_{y}\beta_{y}}+\frac{m}{\beta_{0}}}a\mathcal{I}(\alpha;\text{Cat})^{-1}a^{T}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\min_{y}\beta_{y}}+\frac{m}{\beta_{0}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\alpha_{y}\textup{\text{Var}}_{y}[f]+\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\min_{y}\beta_{y}}+\frac{m}{\beta_{0}}}a\mathcal{I}(\alpha;\text{Cat})^{-1}a^{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\min_{y}\beta_{y}}+\frac{m}{\beta_{0}}}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\alpha}[f],

where the third inequality is by Lemma (
6.3
), and the last line again uses Lemma (
2.5
). Thus, it follows that

\bigg(\frac{1}{\min_{y}\beta_{y}}+\frac{m}{\beta_{0}}\bigg)\textup{\text{Var}}_{\beta}[f]\geq\textup{\text{Var}}_{\alpha}[f],

as claimed

∎

Proof of Lemma (
3.5
)
.

Let
(X,Y)
be random variables, with marginal distribution given by
Y\sim\text{Cat}(\beta)
and conditional distribution given by
X|Y=y\sim p_{y}
. Let
p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(y):=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}^{\mathbbold{1}{\{y=i\}}}\cdot\Big(1-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}\Big)^{\mathbbold{1}{\{y=0\}}}
denote the probability mass function for the
Y
marginal. Observe that:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\beta}\log\big[p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(y)p_{y}(x)\big]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\beta}\log\big[p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(y)\big]+\nabla_{\beta}\log\big[p_{y}(x)\big]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\beta}\log\big[p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(y)\big],

so

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X,Y}\bigg[\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log\big[p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(Y)p_{Y}(X)\big]\Big)\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log\big[p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(Y)p_{Y}(X)\big]\Big)^{T}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{X,Y}\bigg[\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log\big[p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(Y)\big]\Big)\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log\big[p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(Y)\big]\Big)^{T}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{Y}\bigg[\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log\big[p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(Y)\big]\Big)\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log\big[p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(Y)\big]\Big)^{T}\bigg]

\displaystyle\equiv\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat}).

Now, define
p_{\beta}(y|x):=\frac{p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(y)p_{y}(x)}{p_{\beta}(x)}
. By Lemma 1 in
[
Zamir, 1998
]
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X,Y}\bigg[\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log\big[p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(Y)p_{Y}(X)\big]\Big)\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log\big[p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(Y)p_{Y}(X)\big]\Big)^{T}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{X}\bigg[\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log\big[p_{\beta}(X)\big]\Big)\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log\big[p_{\beta}(X)\big]\Big)^{T}\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt+\mathbb{E}_{X,Y}\bigg[\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log p_{\beta}(Y|X)\Big)\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log p_{\beta}(Y|X)\Big)^{T}\bigg]

\displaystyle\equiv\mathcal{I}(\beta)+\mathbb{E}_{X,Y}\bigg[\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log p_{\beta}(Y|X)\Big)\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log p_{\beta}(Y|X)\Big)^{T}\bigg].

As a quick aside, we note that the proof in Lemma 1 in
[
Zamir, 1998
]
assumes that a certain derivative and integral operator can be exchanged, without rigorous proof. However, for our case, this exchange is justified, because the integral in question amounts to a finite summation over
m+1
classes. Now, bringing our work in this proof together, it follows that:

\displaystyle\implies\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})-\mathcal{I}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{X,Y}\bigg[\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log p_{\beta}(Y|X)\Big)\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log p_{\beta}(Y|X)\Big)^{T}\bigg].

Note that the matrix in the RHS above can be rewritten as an expected value of a covariance matrix,

\mathbb{E}_{X}\mathbb{E}_{Y|X}\bigg[\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log p_{\beta}(Y|X)\Big)\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log p_{\beta}(Y|X)\Big)^{T}\bigg],

so the aforementioned matrix is positive semidefinite, and thus,
\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})\succeq\mathcal{I}(\beta)
, as claimed. Next, it remains for us to demonstrate that
\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})=\mathcal{I}(\beta)
if and only if the supports of the class densities are all disjoint. Towards that end, observe that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})=\mathcal{I}(\beta)

\displaystyle\iff\mathbb{E}_{X,Y}\bigg[\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log p_{\beta}(Y|X)\Big)\Big(\nabla_{\beta}\log p_{\beta}(Y|X)\Big)^{T}\bigg]=0

\displaystyle\iff\nabla_{\beta}\log p_{\beta}(Y|X)=\vec{0}\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{Y\sim\text{Cat}(\beta)}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{X|Y}

\displaystyle\iff\nabla_{\beta}\log\Big\{p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(Y)p_{Y}(X)\Big\}=\nabla_{\beta}\log\Big\{p_{\beta}(X)\Big\}\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{Y\sim\text{Cat}(\beta)}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{X|Y}

\displaystyle\iff\nabla_{\beta}\log\Big\{p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(Y)\Big\}=s_{\beta}(X)\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{Y\sim\text{Cat}(\beta)}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{X|Y}.

Now, since
\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{m}>0
, it follows that:

\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})=\mathcal{I}(\beta)\iff\nabla_{\beta}\log\Big\{p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(y)\Big\}=s_{\beta}(X)\qquad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|y},\quad\forall y\in 0\cup[m].

Next, note that for each
j\in[m]
:

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\delta{\beta_{j}}}\log\Big\{p_{\text{Cat}(\beta)}(y)\Big\}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta}{\delta{\beta_{j}}}\Bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbbold{1}{\{y=i\}}\log\beta_{i}+\mathbbold{1}{\{y=0\}}\log\bigg(1-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}\bigg)\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\mathbbold{1}{\{y=j\}}\frac{\delta}{\delta{\beta_{j}}}\log\beta_{j}+\mathbbold{1}{\{y=0\}}\frac{\delta}{\delta{\beta_{j}}}\log\bigg(1-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbbold{1}{\{y=j\}}}{\beta_{j}}-\frac{\mathbbold{1}{\{y=0\}}}{1-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}}.

Thus, we have that, for each
y\in\mathcal{Y}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})=\mathcal{I}(\beta)

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\mathbbold{1}{\{y=j\}}}{\beta_{j}}-\frac{\mathbbold{1}{\{y=0\}}}{1-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}}=\frac{p_{j}(X)-p_{0}(X)}{p_{\beta}(X)}\quad\forall j\in[m]\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|y}.

Now, consider the RHS above, when
y\in[m]
. In this case, we have that:

\frac{\mathbbold{1}{\{y=j\}}}{\beta_{j}}-\frac{\mathbbold{1}{\{y=0\}}}{1-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}}=\frac{p_{j}(X)-p_{0}(X)}{p_{\beta}(X)}\quad\forall j\in[m]\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|y}

\iff\frac{\mathbbold{1}{\{y=j\}}}{\beta_{j}}=\frac{p_{j}(X)-p_{0}(X)}{p_{\beta}(X)}\quad\forall j\in[m]\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|y}

\iff\mathbbold{1}{\{y=j\}}p_{\beta}(X)=\beta_{j}\big(p_{j}(X)-p_{0}(X)\big)\quad\forall j\in[m]\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|y}

\iff\begin{cases}p_{j}(X)=p_{0}(X)&j\neq y\\
p_{\beta}(X)=\beta_{j}\big(p_{j}(X)-p_{0}(X)\big)&j=y\end{cases}\qquad\forall j\in[m]\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|y}

\iff\begin{cases}p_{j}(X)=p_{0}(X)&j\neq y\\
p_{0}(X)=-\sum\limits_{k=1\neq j}^{m}\beta_{k}\big(p_{k}(X)-p_{0}(X)\big)&j=y\end{cases}\qquad\forall j\in[m]\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|y}.

\iff\begin{cases}p_{j}(X)=p_{0}(X)&j\in[m]/y\\
p_{0}(X)=-\sum\limits_{k=1\neq y}^{m}\beta_{k}\big(p_{k}(X)-p_{0}(X)\big)&\end{cases}\qquad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|y}.

If the
m
equalities above hold simultaneously
\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|y}
, then it follows that
p_{0}(x)=0=p_{j}(x)
, for all
j\in[m]/y
. Conversely, if
p_{0}(x)=0=p_{j}(x)
for all
j\in[m]/y
, then the
m
equalities displayed above are trivially true. Ergo, the previous display occurs iff

\iff p_{0}(x)=0=p_{j}(x)\quad\forall j\in[m]/y,\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|y}.

Now, what about when
y=0
? In this case:

\frac{\mathbbold{1}{\{y=j\}}}{\beta_{j}}-\frac{\mathbbold{1}{\{y=0\}}}{1-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}}=\frac{p_{j}(X)-p_{0}(X)}{p_{\beta}(X)}\quad\forall j\in[m]\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|y}

\iff-\frac{1}{1-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}}=\frac{p_{j}(X)-p_{0}(X)}{p_{\beta}(X)}\quad\forall j\in[m]\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|0}

\iff p_{\beta}(X)=-\big(p_{j}(X)-p_{0}(X)\big)\bigg(1-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}\bigg)\quad\forall j\in[m]\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|0}

\iff\bigg(1-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}\bigg)p_{j}(X)=-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}p_{i}(x)\qquad\forall j\in[m]\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|0}

(24)

Now, if the display above is true, then it follows that
p_{1}(X)=\dots=p_{m}(X)
, a.e.
\mathbb{P}_{X|0}
. As such, it would follow that

\implies\Bigg[\bigg(1-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}\bigg)+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}\Bigg]p_{1}(X)=0\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|0}

\implies p_{1}(X)=0\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|0}

\implies p_{1}(X)=\dots=p_{m}(X)=0\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|0}.

Conversely, if
p_{1}(X)=\dots=p_{m}(X)=0\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|0}
, then (
24
) holds trivially. Ergo, overall, we have shown that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\beta;\text{Cat})=\mathcal{I}(\beta)

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\mathbbold{1}{\{y=j\}}}{\beta_{j}}-\frac{\mathbbold{1}{\{y=0\}}}{1-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}}=\frac{p_{j}(X)-p_{0}(X)}{p_{\beta}(X)}\quad\forall j\in[m]\quad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|y},\forall y\in\mathcal{Y}

\displaystyle\iff\begin{cases}p_{j}(x)=0\quad\forall j\in 0\cup[m]/y&:y\in[m]\\
p_{j}(x)=0\quad\forall j\in[m]&:y=0\end{cases}\qquad\text{ a.e. }\mathbb{P}_{X|y},\quad\forall y\in\mathcal{Y},

which is equivalent to the Lemma’s statement.

∎

Proof of Corollary (
6.7.2
)
.

First, note that
s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
is a
\mathbb{R}^{m}
-valued function, so
A_{s_{\gamma^{\ast}}}
is square. Also note that, by Lemma (
2.2
),

\displaystyle A_{s_{\gamma^{\ast}}}

\displaystyle=\textup{\text{Cov}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}},s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]

\displaystyle=\textup{\text{Var}}_{\gamma^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]

\displaystyle\equiv\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast}),

so
A_{s_{\gamma^{\ast}}}=\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})
, as claimed. Furthermore, under Assumption (
A6
),
\sigma_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})\big)>\sqrt{\Lambda}>0
, so
A_{s_{\gamma^{\ast}}}
is positive definite. Thus, by applying Corollary (
2.1
) to
f=s_{\gamma^{\ast}}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\Big[\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}])\Big]

\displaystyle=\Bigg[\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg]\Big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{T})^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\text{Cat})^{-1}\Big)+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\Bigg[\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\sum_{y=0}^{m}\frac{(\pi_{y}^{\ast})^{2}}{\pi_{y}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\Bigg]\Big(\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\text{Cat})^{-1}\Big)+\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1},

as was claimed! Lastly, we need to show that
\frac{1}{2}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\Big[\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}])\Big]
is positive definite. It suffices to show that the smallest eigenvalue is strictly positive. Towards that end, observe that under Assumption (
A5
), we have that
\gamma_{y}^{\ast}>0
for each
y\in\mathcal{Y}
due to Lemma (
6.1
). Thus,
\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})\preceq\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\text{Cat})
by Lemma (
3.5
). So,
\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}\succeq\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\text{Cat})^{-1}
, meaning that the minimum eigenvalue of
\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}-\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast};\text{Cat})^{-1}
is non-negative. Ergo:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\textup{min}}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{2}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\Big[\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}])\Big]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\textup{min}}\Bigg\{\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\Big[\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}])\Big]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\lambda_{\textup{min}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})\big)}.

Now, by Lemma (
6.3
) and Assumption (
A5
), we have
\lambda_{\textup{max}}\big(\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\ast};\textup{Cat})\big)\leq\frac{1}{\min_{y}\pi_{y}^{\ast}}+\frac{m}{\pi_{0}^{\ast}}\leq\frac{1}{\xi}+\frac{m}{\xi}=\frac{m+1}{\xi}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\textup{min}}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{2}\textup{\text{Var}}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{I}}}}\Big[\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}])\Big]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\frac{\xi}{m+1},

where the RHS is strictly greater than
0
because
\xi
and
m+1
are both fixed, positive constants.

∎

Proof of Lemma (
6.8
)
.

Observe that:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]

\displaystyle=\Big(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\Big)-\Big(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\Big)-\sum_{j=0}^{m}\pi_{j}^{\ast}\Big(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\mathbb{E}_{j}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\Big)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}\big[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big]-\sum_{j=0}^{m}\pi_{j}^{\ast}\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}\big[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}-\mathbb{E}_{j}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}D_{i}\big(s_{\gamma^{\ast}}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)-\sum_{j=0}^{m}\pi_{j}^{\ast}\Bigg(\frac{1}{n_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}(1-D_{i})\mathbbold{1}{\{Y_{i}=j\}}\big(s_{\gamma^{\ast}}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{j}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}\frac{D_{i}}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}}\big(s_{\gamma^{\ast}}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)-2\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\frac{\pi_{j}^{\ast}}{n_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}(1-D_{i})\mathbbold{1}{\{Y_{i}=j\}}\big(s_{\gamma^{\ast}}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{j}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}\frac{D_{i}}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}/n}\big(s_{\gamma^{\ast}}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)-\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\frac{1-D_{i}}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}/n}\frac{\pi_{j}^{\ast}}{n_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}/n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\mathbbold{1}{\{Y_{i}=j\}}\big(s_{\gamma^{\ast}}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{j}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}\Bigg\{\frac{D_{i}}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}/n}\big(s_{\gamma^{\ast}}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)-\sum_{j=0}^{m}\frac{1-D_{i}}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}/n}\frac{\pi_{j}^{\ast}}{n_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}/n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\mathbbold{1}{\{Y_{i}=j\}}\big(s_{\gamma^{\ast}}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{j}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}\Bigg\{\frac{D_{i}}{n^{\textup{\text{te}}}/n}\big(s_{\gamma^{\ast}}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\ast}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)-\frac{1-D_{i}}{n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}/n}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\mathbbold{1}{\{Y_{i}=j\}}\frac{\pi_{j}^{\ast}}{n_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}/n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}}\big(s_{\gamma^{\ast}}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{j}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)\Bigg\}.

This implies that

\displaystyle\implies\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}\big(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr,I}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}\psi^{\textup{eff}}(Z_{i}),

as claimed. The derivation for showing the analogous identity involving
\mathcal{I}(\gamma^{\ast})^{-1}\big(\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{te}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi^{\ast}}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}[s_{\gamma^{\ast}}]\big)
is identical.

∎

Proof of Lemma (
6.9
)
.

First, we will address the uniform deviation tail bounds for the difference between the empirical class expectations, and their population level counterparts. Fix
y\in[m]
. Let any
a>0
and class
j\in\mathcal{Y}
be given. By standard arguments using McDiarmid’s inequality, we have that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{j}[h_{y}]\Big|\geq a\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\Bigg[\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{j}[h_{y}]\Big|-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\bigg(\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{j}[h_{y}]\Big|\bigg)\geq\frac{1}{2}a\Bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt+\mathbbold{1}{\Bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\bigg(\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{j}[h_{y}]\Big|\bigg)\geq\frac{1}{2}a\Bigg\}}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\Bigg\{-2\frac{a^{2}/4}{\tfrac{1}{2}\pi_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}\big(\tfrac{2}{\tfrac{1}{2}\pi_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}DL\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}\big)^{2}}\Bigg\}+\mathbbold{1}{\Bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\bigg(\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{j}[h_{y}]\Big|\bigg)\geq\frac{1}{2}a\Bigg\}}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\Bigg\{-\frac{D^{2}L^{2}\xi}{16}\lambda_{y}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}a^{2}\Bigg\}+\mathbbold{1}{\Bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}}\bigg(\sup_{\Omega(h_{y})\leq\frac{1}{L\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}}\Big|\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr,II}}}[h_{y}]-\mathbb{E}_{j}[h_{y}]\Big|\bigg)\geq\frac{1}{2}a\Bigg\}},

where the second inequality is because, under Assumption (
•
‣
A2
), each
h_{y}
in the supremum satisfies
||h_{y}||_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{DL\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}}
, so it can be shown that each of the
\frac{1}{2}\pi_{j}^{\textup{\text{tr}}}n^{\textup{\text{tr}}}
bounded differences coefficients (for McDiarmid’s inequality) equal
